                                            iv
                                            Abstract

    Property testing is a computational paradigm that aims to design algorithms for ex-
tremely fast decision-making about massive inputs. Property testing has been studied for
nearly three decades, primarily with a focus on testing properties about discrete objects
such as Boolean functions and graphs. In this thesis, we study property testing for in-
herently continuous objects, namely functions with real-valued domain and range—which
we call the analytic setting. We study the central problem of monotonicity testing in this
setting, where the input is a continuous function f : [0, 1]d → R and the algorithm must
decide whether f is monotone with respect to its input coordinates, or far from monotone
in the appropriate sense (namely with respect to the Lp distance).
    The central theme of this thesis is a connection between monotonicity testing and
directed isoperimetric inequalities, which are analogues of classical isoperimetric inequalities
that have been shown to be intimately related to monotonicity testing in discrete settings.
Indeed, many algorithmic advances in monotonicity testing over the last decade have been
obtained via new directed isoperimetric inequalities.
    We show that the connection between directed isoperimetry and monotonicity also holds
in the analytic setting, and indeed reveals new relationships between property testing and
areas of mathematics such as partial differential equations and optimal transport theory.
The main results in this thesis are the directed Poincaré inequality distmono
                                                                          2    (f )2 ≲ E [|∇− f |2 ]
for Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R defined over the solid unit cube, where distmono   2  (f )
denotes the L distance to monotonicity of f and the “directed gradient” operator ∇− f
               2

measures the local violations
                           √ of monotonicity of f ; and a monotonicity tester for this setting
with query complexity O( d). We obtain the directed Poincaré inequality by studying a
                         e
new partial differential equation called the directed heat equation.
    In our study of monotonicity testing and its connection to directed isoperimetry, we
also systematize classical and directed isoperimetric inequalities in continuous and discrete
settings; obtain a variety of upper and lower bounds for monotonicity testing of Lipschitz
functions in other settings of interest such as the one-dimensional line and the hypergrid;
and develop directed Poincaré inequalities for directed graphs by studying a dynamical
process called the directed heat flow via directed analogues of classical spectral theory.

                                            x
Chapter 1

Introduction

                                           “But equations belong to everybody. We’re going
                                           to roll up our sleeves and give it our best shot.”
                                                        Cédric Villani, Birth of a Theorem: A
                                                                       Mathematical Adventure


    When is it possible to make reliable decisions using an extremely small amount of
information? Can we derive knowledge about a system purely from local observations of
it? In philosophy, this kind of question dates back to Hume’s treatment of the problem
of induction [Hum08], i.e. the problem of deducing laws about the natural world from
experience. In that context, Hume posited that any such process must be predicated
on some form of regularity of nature: the expectation – which cannot be ascertained from
experience alone – that local observations made today reflect general principles that extend
through time and space, thus enabling the acquisition of knowledge.
    This thesis considers a computational instantiation of this general question: how can
algorithms make reliable decisions about extremely large inputs using only a small number
of local observations? Specifically, we study the ability of algorithms to draw conclusions
about continuous objects, whose infinite-dimensional nature appears to pose a formidable
challenge to any finite observer. Nevertheless, under reasonable assumptions of mathemat-
ical regularity, we will uncover local to global phenomena which algorithms can exploit to
achieve the foregoing goal.
   The computational paradigm of making decisions by looking at only a small portion of
the input is called property testing [RS96; GGR98]. The setting is the following. Suppose
we have access to an input object x from a universe X , and we would like to check whether

                                             1
x satisfies some property P ⊂ X while reading only a small portion of x. This goal is too
ambitious as stated: if x does not satisfy property P but only differs from some y ∈ P in
a few bits, then no algorithm can hope to catch this difference without inspecting most of
x. However, as we will see, the following two relaxations suffice to arrive at a tractable
problem:

   1. Distance: we only require to distinguish between objects x ∈ P and those that are
      far from P, i.e. they are substantially different from every y ∈ P (in a precise sense
      specified by the problem formulation).

   2. Randomness: we allow ourselves to make randomized decisions, and admit a small
      probability of error.

Algorithms that accomplish this relaxed task are called property testers.


1.1      Property testing in the analytic setting
Property testing has been an explicit topic of study for about three decades [RS96; GGR98]
(see also the books [Gol17; BY22]), with origins tracing back to program checking [BLR90]
and probabilistically checkable proofs [BFLS91]. At a high level, most existing work in
property testing can be classified under one or more of the following broad themes:1

   1. Algebraic: the input is a function f taking values in a field, and the goal is to check
      whether f satisfies some algebraic property such as linearity or being a low-degree
      function; see e.g. [BLR90; RS96; KS08; RS97; FY20; ABFKY23] and the survey
      [KMS00].

   2. Combinatorial : here we may distinguish between two salient areas with combinatorial
      flavour. In one line of study, the input is a combinatorial object such as a graph or
      hypergraph, and the goal is to test properties such as bipartiteness, k-colorability, ex-
      istence of a large clique, connectivity, and planarity or minor-freeness; see e.g. [Gol97;
      GGR98; GR02; FN05; ELR24; BS24; SS24; Set25]. In another line of work, the in-
      put is a Boolean or real-valued function on a discrete domain such as the hypercube,
      and properties of interest include being a junta [FKRSS04; Bla09] (i.e. completely
   1
   It is impractical to give a full account of past works under all of these themes, so we give a small
sample of pointers to the literature instead.


                                                  2
     determined by only a few input variables), a decision list or tree [DLMORSW07],
     or a monotone function [EKKRV98; Ras99; DGLRRS99; GGLRS00; CS13; CS16;
     KMS18; BCS23a].

  3. Geometric: the input is typically a Boolean function f , and the goal is to check
     whether f can be captured by a “simple” geometric concept such as a halfspace, an
     intersection of halfspaces, or a low-degree polynomial threshold function [MORS10;
     BBBY12; DMN19; Har19; BFH21]. Closely related perspectives ask whether the set
     f −1 (1) satisfies properties such as convexity [RV04; CFSS17; BB20; HY22; BBH24;
     CDNSW25] or connectedness [Ras03; BMRR24], or whether real-valued f satisfies a
     property such as convexity [BBB20].

  4. Statistical : here, the input is a probability distribution p, and the algorithm typically
     only has access to independent samples from p (or perhaps certain kinds of con-
     ditional samples). We may ask about properties such as uniformity [GR11; DK16;
     VV17a], independence [BFFKRW01] or support size [VV17b; WY19; AFL24; FH25],
     or versions of the properties mentioned above applied to probability distributions,
     e.g. monotonicity [AGPRY19; RV20; CCRSW25]. See also the survey [Can22].

    Of course, the lines between these categories are not sharp, and some problems (and
associated mathematical techniques) do straddle them. For example, in distribution-free
testing [HK07], distance between the input f and the property P is measured against an
unknown input probability distribution from which the algorithm can only take samples,
so statistical considerations play a key role in this setting along with the other themes.
Property testing of Boolean functions is also intimately connected with Fourier analysis,
which lends an analytic flavour to this area of study.
    One common feature of the themes above, and indeed of most prior works in property
testing, is some form of discreteness – either of the underlying domain, e.g. a graph or the
Boolean hypercube; or of the range, e.g. Boolean-valued functions (but see Chapter 3 for
a discussion of related work, including research initiated by [FY20] on algebraic property
testing in the real-valued setting). In this thesis, we ask: what kinds of decisions are
algorithms able to make in the fully continuous setting, where the input function f has
real-valued domain and range? Can we obtain query-efficient property testers, despite
the fact that any finite number of queries only captures a measure-zero portion of any
continuous object?
    This thesis explores an instantiation of the question above which we call analytic prop-
erty testing. This setting is characterized by two features of the problem description.


                                              3
First, the input is a real-valued function defined on Euclidean space, for example a func-
tion f : [0, 1]d → R over the solid unit cube; typically, we require some regularity from f
such as Lipschitz continuity. Second, we measure distance between the input f and the
property P according to Lp distance (for some appropriate choice of p). In contrast with
the Hamming distance, which is more common in discrete settings, the Lp distances are
a natural fit for the continuous nature of the analytic setting, since they are sensitive not
only to the presence of errors but also to their magnitude2. These features imply a third
characteristic of the analytic setting, which is that new mathematical techniques must be
brought to bear compared to previous work in the other settings. Indeed, this thesis ob-
tains its computational results (new algorithms) by recruiting tools from real and functional
analysis, ordinary and partial differential equations, and optimal transport theory.
   We initiate research into analytic property testing with a study of monotonicity testing,
which we introduce next.


1.2       Monotonicity testing
Monotonicity testing has been a central topic of study in property testing for over 25
years [Ras99; DGLRRS99; GGLRS00], and continues to be a prominent object of recent
research. Very informally, the problem asks: given a function f , is it true that the value
f (x) can only grow larger when we make the input x larger? We may motivate the study
of this problem from at least three perspectives:

   1. As a simple instantiation of property testing. In theoretical research, it is often
      sensible and productive to understand the simple cases of a theory as well as possible
      as a first step toward more elaborate settings. Monotonicity testing, as we will
      see below, is a natural problem with a simple statement, and therefore provides an
      excellent testbed for property testing research more generally.
   2. As a natural problem connected to real-world concepts. The question of whether one
      or more variates are positively associated with a response variable is fundamental to
      much of empirical science, and parametric forms of this question have been exten-
      sively studied in statistics [BJG98; Che19]; property testing offers a complementary
      angle on this class of problems. From a more contemporary standpoint, monotonicity
      has also been proposed and studied as a desirable characteristic of machine learning
      models due to fairness, interpretability, and quality assurance considerations [SW20].
   2
    Indeed, in the statistical setting of distribution testing, the standard notion of distance is the total
variation or L1 distance.


                                                     4
  3. As a source of theoretical insights and innovation. Many mathematical tools have
     been developed, or found novel applications, in the course of research in monotonicity
     testing. A non-exhaustive list includes routing on the hypercube [LR01; BCGM12],
     isoperimetric inequalities [CS16; KMS18; BCS23b; BKKM23], analysis of random
     walks [BCS23a], domain reduction techniques [BCS20; HY22], Ramsey theory [Fis04],
     and constructions of Ruzsa-Szemerédi graphs [FLNRRS02]. This thesis adds to this
     list topics such as partial differential equations (PDEs) and optimal transport theory.

   Here, we briefly summarize the landscape of monotonicity testing, and then provide a
more systematic review in Chapter 3. The general formulation of the problem is as follows.
Let (X, ⪯) be a partially-ordered set, i.e. X is a ground set and ⪯ is a partial order on
X. Let R be a totally ordered set (which is typically either {0, 1} or R). Then, given an
input function f : X → R, the goal of a monotonicity testing algorithm is to distinguish
between the following two cases:

  1. f is monotone, that is, for every x, y ∈ X satisfying x ⪯ y, we have f (x) ≤ f (y).

  2. f is far from monotone, meaning that dist(f, g) > ε for all monotone g : X → R,
     where dist is a fixed distance metric on functions from X to R and ε > 0 is an
     input parameter. (Most often, dist has been the relative Hamming distance, i.e. the
     fraction of inputs on which f and g disagree.)

The algorithm may access f via queries to an oracle, which typically takes an arbitrary
element x ∈ X as input and provides the value f (x) back to the algorithm. The goal of the
algorithm is to reach a correct decision about f using as few queries as possible, i.e. with
the smallest possible query complexity. Specifically, the algorithm should accept when f
is monotone and reject when f is far from monotone, both with probability3 at least 2/3.
(When f is neither monotone nor far from monotone, either outcome is acceptable. As
already remarked, this flexibility is key to making efficient property testing possible.)
    One fundamental setting for monotonicity testing is the fully Boolean regime, where
1) the partially-ordered set is the Boolean hypercube {0, 1}d , under the natural partial order
whereby x ⪯ y if xi ≤ yi for all i ∈ [d]; and 2) we take Boolean range R = {0, 1}. The
first works on monotonicity testing [Ras99; DGLRRS99; GGLRS00] studied this setting,
as well as the generalizations where the domain is allowed to be the hypergrid [n]d and the
range is allowed to be any set R. A hallmark result established that, in the fully Boolean
case, monotonicity can be tested using O(d/ε) queries [Ras99; GGLRS00].
  3
      This probability may be amplified to any desired level 1 − δ by repeating the test O(log(1/δ)) times.


                                                     5
    Since then, a vast array of works has continued to drive progress on this problem.
                                                                                     √ 2A
series of works on the Boolean case has pushed the query complexity down to O( d/ε )
                                                                                   e
[KMS18], and more recent works have obtained similar results for the case of Boolean-
valued functions on the hypergrid [BCS23a]; monotonicity testing of real-valued functions
on the hypercube and hypergrid has also been studied [CS13; BKR24]; and the challenging
setting of general partially-ordered domains was studied in early work and recently revisited
[FLNRRS02; LRV22; LV23].


1.2.1      Monotonicity testing in the analytic setting.
As already mentioned, in this thesis we study monotonicity testing in the analytic setting.
Specifically, we consider functions f : [0, 1]d → R defined on the solid unit cube, and make
the following modelling choices.


Lp distance. First, we let the distance metric dist be an Lp metric, such as L1 or L2 .
This is a more natural choice for our continuous setting than the Hamming metric because
it captures not only whether two functions disagree at a point x, but also by how much
they disagree. For example, by slightly perturbing a monotone function f , we may obtain
a function fe which is Ω(1)-far from monotone in Hamming distance even though any algo-
rithm will observe, via its queries, an output that is consistent with a monotone function4.
In this example, the perturbed function fe is indeed arbitrarily close to f in Lp distance,
in line with our intuition that this notion of distance is a better fit to our setting. This
choice follows the work of [BRY14a], who introduced Lp testing with a similar motivation
and indeed studied monotonicity testing (for functions with discrete domain), along with
other problems (we review this and other prior work in Chapter 3).


Lipschitz regularity. Even after choosing an appropriate distance metric, it turns out
that continuous space is too unwieldy to permit testability without further assumptions
on f . For example, we may perturb f in an arbitrarily small region by an arbitrarily large
amount to obtain a function fe that is very far from monotone, yet indistinguishable from
f . We consider Lipschitz regularity to be a natural enough constraint on f which, as we
will see, is also sufficient to enable efficient testers as well as mediate connections between
   4
   Formally, for any nonadaptive algorithm A, i. e. one that determines all of its queries in advance, we
may make the perturbation to f fine enough so that A will not observe any violations of monotonicity.
We may extend this argument to adaptive testers under reasonable assumptions, e. g. that A only uses a
bounded number of bits of precision from each output before making its next query.


                                                   6
monotonicity and well-established areas of mathematical analysis such as isoperimetric
inequalities, rearrangements, partial differential equations, and optimal transport.
      Therefore, our problem is the following:

  Given oracle access to an M -Lipschitz function f : [0, 1]d → R, distinguish between
  1) f is monotone; and 2) f is far from every monotone g : [0, 1]d → R in Lp distance.

  To complete the specification of the problem, we must define the oracle access in this
model. We consider two types of oracle:

      Value oracle: Given point x, this oracle outputs the value f (x).
      Directional derivative oracle: Given point x and vector v ∈ Rd , this oracle outputs
      the derivative of f along v at point x, given by ∇v f (x) = v · ∇f (x), as long as f is
      differentiable at x. Otherwise5, it outputs a special symbol ⊥.

A directional derivative oracle is weaker than a full first-order oracle, which would return
the entire gradient [BV04], and it seems like a reasonable model for the high-dimensional
setting; for example, obtaining the full gradient costs d queries, rather than a single query.
This type of oracle has also been studied in optimization research, see e.g. [CWZ21].
Finally, we remark (in Chapter 5) that, under reasonable assumptions, these oracle queries
may be simulated by value queries in our applications, so we consider directional derivative
queries to be a primitive that is both natural and reasonable in our model.


1.3        Directed isoperimetry
Since the introduction of monotonicity testing and especially over the last decade, a series
of works has revealed striking connections between monotonicity testing and directed ana-
logues of well-studied and ubiquitous isoperimetric inequalities such as Poincaré, Margulis,
and Talagrand inequalities. Directed isoperimetric inequalities and their role in monotonic-
ity testing were first recognized and formalized in the breakthrough work of [CS16], and
lie at the core of a number of subsequent advances [KMS18; BCS23b; BKKM23; BKR24].
This thesis builds upon these ideas by showing that continuous functions also enjoy di-
rected isoperimetric phenomena, which underlie algorithms for monotonicity testing in the
analytic setting.
  5
     By Rademacher’s theorem, a Lipschitz function is differentiable almost everywhere, so non-
differentiability will never be a concern for us.


                                                 7
    Before summarizing the connections between directed isoperimetry and monotonicity
and previewing our contributions, let us start with an example that will already motivate
the overall picture by returning to the classic monotonicity tester of [GGLRS00] for the
fully Boolean case of functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}.
   The tester of [GGLRS00] is remarkably simple: first, sample a vertex x ∈ {0, 1}d and
another vertex y directly “above” x, i.e. y is obtained from x by flipping a single bit of x
from 0 to 1; we call (x, y) an edge. If f (x) > f (y), i.e. f violates monotonicity along this
edge, reject. If no violating edge is found after repeating this test O(d/ε) times, accept.
This algorithm is called the edge tester.
    Why should this algorithm work? Certainly it accepts whenever f is monotone, so the
point is to argue that this algorithm samples a violating edge with good probability when
f is far from monotone. A key result in [GGLRS00] shows exactly that:

Theorem 1.1 ([Ras99; GGLRS00]). Suppose f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} is ε-far from monotone
in relative Hamming distance. Let V (f ) denote the number of violating edges in f . Then

                                        V (f ) ≥ ε · 2d .

  Thus, when f is ε-far from monotone, at least an Ω(ε/d)-fraction of the edges violates
monotonicity. Hence O(d/ε) queries suffice to detect one of them and successfully reject.
    Broadly speaking, isoperimetric phenomena are relationships between the volume and
surface area of sets or, under suitable interpretation, functions. As first observed by [CS16],
statements such as Theorem 1.1 may be seen as directed analogues of classical isoperimetry.
Indeed, if we think of “distance to monotonicity” as a notion of volume (e.g. the volume of
the smallest set where f needs to change to reach any monotone function), and “violating
edges” as a notion of surface area, then Theorem 1.1 is a lower bound on (directed) surface
area in terms of (directed) volume.
    In this thesis, we observe that the family of Poincaré inequalities provides a suitable
framework for discussing, systematizing, and extending this connection between classical
isoperimetry, directed analogues, and monotonicity testing, as we now preview.
   Let p, q ≥ 1. We say a (classical) (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality is an inequality of the
form
                              distconst (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇f ∥pq
                                                           
                                  p

for, say, all functions f : {0, 1}d → R on the Boolean cube, or all functions f : [0, 1]d → R
on the unit cube, or perhaps only the Boolean-valued functions on these domains. Here
distconst
    p     (f ) denotes the Lp distance of f to the closest constant function when the domain

                                               8
is given uniform probability measure. Then the following isoperimetric results are classical
Poincaré inequalities:

  1. for functions f : {0, 1}d → R, the classical Poincaré inequality Var [f ] ≤ C Inf [f ],
     where Inf [f ] denotes the total influence of f , is an (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality (see
     e.g. [O’D14]);

  2. in the same setting, the Talagrand inequality [Tal93] is an (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality;

  3. for smooth functions f : [0, 1]d → R, the (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality is often called the Poincaré
     inequality, especially in mathematical analysis (see e.g. [BGL14]); and

  4. in the same setting, the (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality was proved by Bobkov & Houdré [BH97].

    Now, let distmono
                  p    (f ) denote the Lp distance of f to the closest monotone function. Then
a directed (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality is an inequality of the form

                                  distmono (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥pq ,
                                                               
                                      p                                                   (1.1)

where ∇− f := min{0, ∇f }, the directed gradient of f , captures the local violations of
monotonicity of f . Note that both the classical and directed inequalities relate a local
property (violations of the “constant” or “monotone” property, captured by the gradient)
to a global one (distance to a constant or monotone function).
   As observed by [CS16] and mentioned above, it turns out that many classical isoperi-
metric inequalities enjoy directed analogues. For example,

  1. Theorem 1.1 above is a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequality for functions f : {0, 1}d →
     {0, 1};

  2. [KMS18] proved the stronger (L1 , ℓ2 ) inequality for functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}, in
     analogy with Talagrand’s result in the classical setting; and

  3. Related directed isoperimetric statements include a directed analogue of the Mar-
     gulis inequality by [CS16], and isoperimetric inequalities for real-valued functions on
     the Boolean cube [BKR24] and for Boolean functions on the hypergrid [BCS23b;
     BKKM23]. We review these in Chapter 3.

   What makes the story above especially compelling is that each new directed isoperi-
metric inequality also enabled an algorithmic result, namely a monotonicity tester with

                                               9
improved query complexity in the same setting. While we defer a more detailed review to
Chapter 3, one central example is that, for functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}, the celebrated
work
   √ of 2[KMS18] essentially resolved the question for nonadaptive testers by giving an
O( d/ε ) query tester.
 e
    Previously, directed isoperimetric inequalities and their applications to monotonicity
testing were only known in discrete domains. On the other hand, ever since the origi-
nal “isoperimetric problem” about shapes in Euclidean space, isoperimetric inequalities
have enjoyed a rich and fruitful history with connections to mathematical physics, geom-
etry, probability theory, diffusion processes, optimal transport, and so on; see e.g. [Vil09;
BGL14]. Closest to our subject, the Poincaré inequality itself first appeared in the study
of partial differential equations arising in mathematical physics by Poincaré [Poi90]. Thus,
if there are unifying principles underlying both classical and directed isoperimetric phe-
nomena, as well as the connection to monotonicity, it seems reasonable to expect such a
principle to manifest itself in the analytic setting.
    Accordingly, this thesis strengthens the connection between classical isoperimetry, di-
rected isoperimetry, and monotonicity testing by extending it to the analytic setting. We
show that sufficiently regular (e.g. Lipschitz) functions f : [0, 1]d → R satisfy directed
Poincaré inequalities, and that this implies query-efficient monotonicity testers for such
functions. Toward this goal, we extend the aforementioned classical connections between
isoperimetry and other areas of mathematical analysis to the directed setting. For exam-
ple, in Chapter 7 we introduce a new nonlinear partial differential equation, the directed
heat equation, whose convergence properties we use to prove a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré
inequality for functions f : [0, 1]d → R via optimal transport theory.


1.4      Main results and organization
In Chapter 2, we introduce notation and technical preliminaries used throughout the thesis,
and formally define the problem of monotonicity testing in the analytic setting. Chapter 3
summarizes prior work relevant to this thesis. After that, the main body of the thesis can
be roughly divided into three parts, which proceed as follows.


Part I: Monotonicity testing in the analytic setting. This part encompasses Chap-
ters 4 and 5. In Chapter 4, we begin our study of directed isoperimetry by giving directed
(L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequalities in discrete and continuous settings, and study their implica-
tions to monotonicity testing. We give upper and lower bounds for testing monotonicity of

                                              10
functions over the unit cube [0, 1]d and the hypergrid [n]d , including nearly-tight bounds
for the one-dimension cases of the discrete and continuous line. The main result from
Chapter 4 is an O(d)-query tester for monotonicity of Lipschitz functions on the unit cube:

Theorem 1.2 (Testing monotonicity with O(d) queries). There is a nonadaptive L1 mono-
tonicity tester for M -Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R with query complexity O dM
                                                                                      
                                                                                    ε
                                                                                        and
one-sided error.

    Theorem 1.2 is shown via a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequality, which is conceptually
similar to how Theorem 1.1 is used to show the classical O(d/ε) bound for monotonicity
testing of Boolean functions. Specifically, the tester from Theorem 1.2 works by sampling
points x and coordinates i ∈ [d] uniformly at random, and using directional derivative
queries to reject if f is decreasing in direction i at point x. Correctness is shown using
the directed Poincaré inequality, which says that if f is far from monotone, then the
total magnitude of its negative partial derivatives must be large – and since each partial
derivative is bounded thanks to the Lipschitz assumption, the partial derivatives must be
strictly negative in a set of large measure, which the tester stands good chance of hitting
with the given query complexity.
     Theorem 1.2 suggests a natural follow-up question: can we obtain a o(d)-query tester?
A complementary lower bound result of Chapter 4 shows that any tester that works as
above, i.e. by sampling directional derivatives aligned with a single direction i one at a time,
cannot do better than Theorem 1.2. Hence any tester with sublinear query complexity, if
it is based on directional derivatives, must make use of directional derivative queries that
are not aligned with any single direction i – intuitively, the tester would need to “probe”
many directions at once.
    Chapter 5 shows that this strategy can indeed work, if we show a different directed
Poincaré inequality, namely an (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality (which is indeed more standard in the
                                                                              2 2
classical, undirected case). Specifically, we show that assuming a directed
                                                                          √ (L ,2 ℓ )-Poincaré
                                                                       e dM /ε2 ) queries.
inequality, monotonicity of M -Lipschitz functions can be tested using O(
Intuitively, note that directional derivative queries are inner products between a query
vector v and a (directed) gradient vector ∇− f (x), so it may not be entirely unexpected
that a guarantee in terms of ℓ2 geometry is better suited to proving bounds on such inner
products. The main task left to the rest of the thesis, then, is to prove such an (L2 , ℓ2 )
inequality.

Interlude: Directed isoperimetry on graphs via the heat equation. Our ap-
proach to proving the (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality will be to analyze a certain dynamical system –

                                              11
the directed heat equation – and show that its convergence properties imply the desired
inequality. This idea draws from the original motivation of the Poincaré inequality itself,
which, as we already mentioned, arose from Poincaré’s work on partial differential equations
in mathematical physics [Poi90], including the classical heat equation. The novel aspect
of our approach, then, is to instantiate and study the appropriate directed analogues of
these classical connections. At a high level, we will study dynamical systems which take
an initial state f (our input function) and converge, over time, to an equilibrium state f ∗
which is monotone. Then, by characterizing the rate of convergence of this process, we may
place an upper bound on the distance between f and f ∗ in terms of an “energy functional”
which, it turns out, is precisely the right-hand side in our desired Poincaré inequality (1.1).
   However, before moving to the setting of nonlinear PDEs – whose rigorous study brings
about a host of technical considerations – we first study, in Chapter 6, a version of this
approach for discrete domains, with the goal of first showing a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré
inequality for real-valued functions f : {0, 1}d → R defined on the Boolean cube.
    In fact, it is convenient and informative to consider the more general setting of directed
graphs. In this setting, the appropriate dynamical system is given by a directed version of
the classical Laplacian operator from spectral graph theory, whose study was initiated by
[Yos16] in the context of network analysis. We show that the dynamical system (heat flow)
generated by the directed Laplacian yields a directed Poincaré inequality for any directed
graph; this inequality is made quantitative by the dynamical spectral gap of the graph,
which characterizes the convergence rate of the dynamical system. Chapter 6 concludes by
exactly characterizing the dynamical spectral gap of the (directed) Boolean cube, which
implies the directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality for that domain, and by showing a family
of reverse Poincaré inequalities quantifying the extent to which our directed isoperimetric
inequalities are tight.

Remark 1.3. In the manuscript [FF25], we combined the directed Poincaré inequalities
from Chapter 6 with an approximate version of the FKG inequality [FKG71] to obtain im-
proved bounds on the mixing time of random walks on monotone subsets of the hypercube.
That application falls outside the scope of this thesis, so we refer to [FF25] for details.


Part II: The continuous setting via PDEs and optimal transport. The final
Chapter 7 executes the plan outlined above to obtain a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality
                                  d
                                √ → R on the unit cube, and therefore a monotonicity tester
for Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]
for this setting using roughly d queries. The following two theorems summarize the main
results that follow from this chapter.


                                              12
Theorem 1.4 (Directed Poincaré inequality). There exists a universal constant C > 0
such that, for all Lipschitz f : [0, 1]d → R,

                                distmono (f )2 ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥22 .
                                                             
                                     2                                                  (1.2)
                                                   √
Theorem 1.5 (Testing monotonicity with O(        e d) queries). There exists a nonadaptive,
directional derivative√L2 monotonicity tester for M -Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R with
query complexity O(e dM 2 /ε2 ) and one-sided error.

    (In comparison with Theorem 1.2, we note that an L2 tester is also an L1 tester, because
L2 -distance over a probability space is larger than L1 -distance. See Chapter 2 for formal
definitions, and Chapter 5 for further discussion.)
    As mentioned above, the proof of Theorem 1.4 proceeds via the analysis of a dynamical
system which takes f into a monotone equilibrium f ∗ ; informally, we show that this process
convergences exponentially fast, which implies that the initial “energy” of f – namely the
right-hand side of (1.2) – yields an upper bound on the distance from f to f ∗ , and hence
on distmono
       2    (f ). However, the analysis of the continuous case is more intricate than in the
discrete setting of the Boolean cube from Chapter 6, as we outline next.
    The first step is to define and analyze a nonlinear PDE, the directed heat equation,
whose behaviour matches the description above. However, even in one dimension, the
nonlinear aspect of this process proves surprisingly resistant to standard arguments, and
showing basic results such as existence, uniqueness and regularity of solutions already re-
quires a number of targeted technical arguments. Therefore, we constrain our PDE analysis
to the one-dimensional case. We analyze the directed heat equation in the formalism of
gradient flows and maximal monotone operators [Bre73; AC84]. To handle the nonlinear
nature of our system, we introduce a canonical decomposition u = u↑ + u↓ of a function
u : [0, 1] → R into a nondecreasing u↑ and a nonincreasing u↓, which allows us to isolate
the phenomena we can control in u↓ (and in fact recover a bit of linearity), and deal with
the less well-behaved u↑ only when necessary.
    To promote the conclusions of this study to our multidimensional setting of interest,
we draw from another area of mathematics – optimal transport. At a high level, optimal
transport is concerned with the “cost” of transforming a source probability measure µ
into a target probability measure ν, both over an underlying metric space Ω, when we
measure cost as a combination of 1) how much probability mass is moved and 2) how
far each “particle” of mass moves in Ω. The connection to our PDE study is natural:
a PDE stipulates, at each moment in time, how mass must be flowing at each point in
the system. By tracking this flow over time, we recover an optimal transport picture

                                             13
of the directed heat equation; then, by sequentially applying our one-dimensional results
to each direction i = 1, 2, . . . , d in turn, we can reason – in optimal transport terms –
about the total distance travelled by each “particle” at the end of the entire process. This
study yields a Poincaré-type inequality that is indeed multidimensional, but which bounds
optimal transport distance as opposed to L2 distance. Fortunately, there exist relatively
standard analytical arguments to translate back and forth between these two flavours of
inequalities, and we conclude the proof of Theorem 1.4 by showing that the appropriate
directed analogue of such an argument does hold in our setting.


Conclusion. Finally, Chapter 8 concludes this thesis with some reflections on the results
obtained, concrete open questions suggested by our results, and broader directions for
future research.

Chapter 2

Preliminaries

2.1      General notation
In this thesis, N denotes the set of strictly positive integers {1, 2, . . . }. Throughout this
thesis, d ∈ N denotes the dimension of the ambient space Rd unless otherwise specified.
For m ∈ N, we write [n] to denote the set {i ∈ N : i ≤ n}.
   For any x ∈ R, we write x+ for max{0, x} and x− for max{0, −x}, and we extend this
notation to vectors u ∈ Rd in the natural way: u+ , u− ∈ Rd are given by u+i := (ui )
                                                                                     +
       −       −
and ui := (ui ) . For real numbers a and b, we use the notation a ∧ b := min(a, b) and
a ∨ b := max(a, b).
   We write 1 [·] for the indicator function, which takes value in {0, 1}. We write ei for
the i-th basis vector, i.e. ei ∈ {0, 1}d satisfies ei (j) = 1 [i = j] for all j. Given a vector
u ∈ Rd and q ≥ 1, we write ∥u∥q for its ℓq -norm and |u| := ∥u∥2 for its Euclidean norm.
   For a vector u ∈ Rd , we let supp(u) ⊆ [d] denote the set of indices where u is non-zero,
and write ∥u∥0 := |supp(u)|.
    For two points x, y ∈ Rd , we write x ⪯ y if xi ≤ yi for every i ∈ [d], and y ⪰ x if
x ⪯ y. Given set Ω ⊂ Rd and function f : Ω → R, we say f is monotone if, for all x, y ∈ Ω
satisfying x ⪯ y, we have f (x) ≤ f (y).
                             ?
                                     ?
   We use the notation a ≤ b, a = b, etc. within a proof to denote (in)equalities that
have not yet been established. We write a ≲ b to denote that a ≤ c · b for some absolute
constant c > 0, and similarly for a ≳ b. We write a ≍ b to denote that a ≲ b and b ≲ a.


                                              15
2.2      Sets and measures
We denote the closure of a set D ⊂ Rd by D.
    We write dx for the “natural” measure for integration in a particular context, namely
the Lebesgue measure or discrete measure when we are working with continuous or discrete
domains, respectively, as follows. In continuous contexts, where Ω ⊂ Rd is a Lebesgue- R
measurable set (e.g. Ω = [0, 1]d ), and for Lebesgue integrable f : Ω → R, we write Ω f dx
for the Lebesgue integral of f over Ω. In discrete contexts,
                                                    R        where Ω ⊂ Zd is a finite set (e.g.
Ω = [n]d ), and for function f : Ω → R, we write Ω f dx := x∈Ω f (x). It will always be
                                                               P
clear from context whether we are working with the continuous or discrete setting. We will
occasionally employ similar notation with other variable names, such as dy or dt, which
should be understood in the same way.
   When we need to refer to the Lebesgue measure of a set Ω explicitly, we write L(Ω).
   Throughout this thesis, all measures on Rd are Borel measures. We say that measure
µ over Ω ⊂ Rd is absolutely continuous if it is absolutely continuous with respect to
the Lebesgue measure, i.e. if there exists a measurable function f : Ω → R≥0 satisfying
dµ = f dx. In this case, f is called the Radon-Nikodym derivative, or density of µ.


2.3      Lp spaces and Lipschitz functions
                                                                                R
For a measure space (Ω, Σ, µ) and measurable function f : Ω → R, we write Ω f dµ for the
Lebesgue integral of f over this space when this integral exists. Then for 1 ≤ p < +∞, the
space Lp (Ω, µ) is the setR of measurable   functions f such that |f |p is Lebesgue integrable
with respect to µ, i.e. Ω |f |p dµ < +∞, and we write the Lp norm of such functions as
               R          1/p
∥f ∥Lp (Ω,µ) = Ω |f |p dµ      . When µ is the Lebesgue or counting measure, we also shorten
this notation and simply write Lp (Ω).
    Let q ≥ 1, and let Ω ⊂ Rd be a set endowed with the ℓq distance metric (note that
Ω could be a discrete set). Given function f : Ω → R and parameter M > 0, we say f
is (ℓq , M )-Lipschitz, or M -Lipschitz with respect to the ℓq geometry if, for every x, y ∈ Ω,
we have |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M ∥x − y∥q . We say that f is Lipschitz if it is (ℓq , M )-Lipschitz
for some choice of M (in which case this also holds for any other choice of geometry ℓq
and appropriate choice of M ′ ). When we are considering the standard ℓ2 (Euclidean)
geometry, we also omit q from the notation and simply say that f is M -Lipschitz (which
is the standard notion in the continuous setting).


                                              16
Remark 2.1. If p ≤ q and f is (ℓq , M )-Lipschitz, then f is also (ℓp , M )-Lipschitz.

Remark 2.2. If Ω ⊂ Rd is a bounded, convex open set, then every Lipschitz function
f : Ω → R is bounded, and hence Lebesgue integrable and in space Lp (Ω) for every p ≥ 1.
If Ω ⊂ Zd is a finite set, then every function f : Ω → R is Lipschitz.

  We also recall that Lipschitz functions over an open set are differentiable almost every-
where, i.e. outside of a set of measure zero:

Theorem 2.3 (Rademacher’s theorem). If Ω ⊂ Rd is an open set and f : Ω → R is
Lipschitz, then f is differentiable almost everywhere in Ω.

rescaling the measure ν, that is, µ(S) := ν(Ω) for all measurable S ⊆ Ω. We call µ the
uniform measure over Ω.

2.5       Directed partial derivatives and gradients
Continuous setting. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be an open set, and let f : Ω → R be Lipschitz. Then
by Rademacher’s theorem f is differentiable almost everywhere in Ω. For each x ∈ Ω where
f is differentiable, let ∇f (x) = (∂1 f (x), . . . , ∂d f (x)) denote its gradient, where ∂i f (x) is
the partial derivative of f along the i-th coordinate at x. Then, let ∂i− := 0 ∧ ∂i , i.e. for
every x where f is differentiable we have ∂i− f (x) = − (∂i f (x))− . We call ∂i− the directed

                                                 17
partial derivative operator in direction i. Then we define the directed gradient operator by
∇− := (∂1− , . . . , ∂d− ), again defined on every x where f is differentiable. Note that ∇− f ⪯ 0.
We also similarly define ∂i+ := 0 ∨ ∂i and ∇+             +            +
                                                   i := (∂1 , . . . , ∂d ).



Discrete setting. Now considering the hypergrid domains, let f : [n]d → R. Fix x ∈ [n]d
and i ∈ [d]. We then define the (discrete) partial derivative of f along the i-th coordinate
at x by ∂i f (x) := f (x + ei ) − f (x) if xi < n, and ∂i f (x) := 0 if xi = n. We then define its
discrete gradient by ∇ := (∂1 , . . . , ∂n ). Their directed counterparts are defined as above:
∂i− := min{0, ∂i } and ∇− := (∂1− , . . . , ∂n− ), and similarly for ∂i+ and ∇+ .


2.6      Monotonicity testing
Here, we define monotonicity testing of Lipschitz functions against the Lp distance, which
is the main problem studied in this thesis. Our definition follows the original definition of
Lp testing by [BRY14a], with the differences that 1) [BRY14a] focused on functions with
bounded range, whereas we focus on Lipschitz functions whose range can grow with the
dimension d; and 2) [BRY14a] focused on the discrete setting, whereas we will cover both
discrete and continuous settings (and focus on the latter).

Definition 2.4 (Monotonicity tester). Let p, q ≥ 1. Let Ω be either the unit cube [0, 1]d
(continuous setting) or the hypergrid [n]d for some n ∈ N (discrete setting). Given pa-
rameters M, ε > 0, we say a randomized algorithm A is an Lp monotonicity tester for
(ℓq , M )-Lipschitz functions with query complexity m = m(Ω, M, ε) if, for every (ℓq , M )-
Lipschitz input function f : Ω → R, algorithm A makes m oracle queries to f and 1) accepts
with probability at least 2/3 if f is monotone; 2) rejects with probability at least 2/3 if
distmono
     p   (f ) > ε.

    As stated in Chapter 1, oracle queries may be either value queries or directional deriva-
tive queries:

   Value query Given point x, this oracle outputs the value f (x).

   Directional derivative query Given point x and vector v ∈ Rd , this oracle outputs
   the derivative of f along v at point x, given by ∇v f (x) = v · ∇f (x), as long as f is
   differentiable at x. Otherwise, it outputs a special symbol ⊥.


                                                18
   A monotonicity tester is nonadaptive if it decides all its queries in advance before seeing
any output from the oracle; otherwise, it is adaptive. A monotonicity tester has one-sided
error if it accepts monotone functions with probability 1; otherwise, it has two-sided error.


2.7         Classical and directed Poincaré inequalities
Let Ω be either a bounded open subset of Rd endowed with Lebesgue measure, or the
hypergrid [n]d endowed with the counting measure. Let µ be the uniform measure over Ω.
   Given p, q ≥ 1, we say a (classical) (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality is an inequality of the
form
                               distconst (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇f ∥pq
                                                             
                                   p

for all Lipschitz1 functions f : Ω → R. A directed (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequality is an
inequality of the form
                              distmono (f )p ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥pq
                                                            
                                  p

for all Lipschitz functions f : Ω → R.
    Note that the parameter p determines the order of integration, whereas q determines
the geometry in which we measure the norms of gradient vectors. These are orthogonal
concerns, and we may vary each parameter independently to obtain stronger, weaker, or
incomparable inequalities. In general, making q larger yields stronger inequalities for a
fixed p, while varying p yields incomparable inequalities for a fixed q.




  1
      Our results hold under a relaxed condition, but we state the Lipschitz condition here for simplicity.


                                                     19
Chapter 3

Prior work

In this chapter, we summarize the lines of research that are most closely related to the work
in this thesis. We start with an overview of the landscape of monotonicity testing; give
some background on classical and directed isoperimetric inequalities in continuous and
discrete domains; describe prior work on property testing with respect to Lp distances;
and conclude by outlining a closely related line of works on testing algebraic properties of
functions in the real-valued setting, and other recent work on testing properties of functions
in real-valued settings.


3.1      Monotonicity testing
We first summarize results on testing monotonicity with respect to the Hamming distance,
which is the most prevalent notion of distance in prior works in property testing. We also
point the reader to the recent thesis of Black [Bla23] as an excellent resource.


Boolean-valued functions. Goldreich et al. [GGLRS00] formally introduced the prob-
lem of monotonicity testing, and that work and the Master’s thesis [Ras99] gave testers for
functions on the hypergrid [n]d with query complexities O(d log(n)/ε) and O((d/ε)2 ); note
that the latter bound is independent of n, and the query complexity of testers with this
property was subsequently improved to O((d/ε) log2 (d/ε)) by Dodis et al. [DGLRRS99]
and to O((d/ε) log(d/ε)) by Berman, Raskhodnikova and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14a]. For
functions on the Boolean cube {0, 1}d , Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [CS16] gave the first
o(d) tester, subsequently improved by Chen, Servedio and Tan [CST14], culminating in

                                             20
       √
     e d/ε2 ) tester of Khot, Minzer and Safra [KMS18], which essentially resolved the
the O(
question for nonadaptive testers. Whether adaptivity helps in monotonicity testing is still
an open question; see the lower bounds below, and also work of Chakrabarty and Se-
shadhri [CS19] on a connection between the query complexity of monotonicity testing and
the total influence of the input function.
    Returning to hypergrid domains [n]d , Black, Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [BCS18; BCS20]
established first testers with o(d) query complexity and, via a domain reduction tech-
nique, also obtained o(d) testers for product distributions on Rd (and the alternative
proof of Harms and Yoshida [HY22] improves the number of samples drawn by the tester
when the distribution is unknown). Subsequent works of Black, Chakrabarty and Se-
shadhri [BCS23b] and Braverman et al. [BKKM23] attained the optimal     √ dependence on d
at the cost of a dependence on n, with upper bounds of the form O( d poly(n)). Most
                                                                      e
recently, Black, Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [BCS23a] gave a tester with query complexity
O(d1/2+o(1) /ε2 ), which is almost optimal for nonadaptive algorithms, and again extends to
product measures on Rd .

Real-valued functions. Ergün et al. [EKKRV98] gave a tester with query complexity
O(log(n)/ε) for real-valued functions on the line [n]; the tight query complexity of this
problem was more recently shown to be Θ(log(εn)/ε) by Belovs [Bel18]. As for functions on
the hypergrid [n]d , the early works on monotonicity testing [GGLRS00; DGLRRS99; Ras99]
also gave testers for larger ranges, but the query complexity depends on the size of the
range. Then, Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [CS13] gave a nonadaptive tester with one-sided
error and (optimal) query complexity O(n log(d)/ε). On the Booleancube,nBlack, Kalemaj
                                                                              √          o
and Raskhodnikova [BKR24] gave a tester with query complexity O    e min r d/ε2 , d/ε
for real-valued functions f with image size r, and showed that this is optimal (for constant
ε) for nonadaptive testers with one-sided error.

Lower bounds. We briefly summarize the known lower bounds for these problems; all
lower bounds listed are for testers with two-sided error unless noted otherwise. For Boolean
                                                                                     √
functions on the Boolean cube {0, 1}d , there is a near-optimal lower bound of Ω(  e d) for
nonadaptive testers by Chen, Waingarten and Xie [CWX17], which improves on a line of
prior results [FLNRRS02; CST14; CDST15]. For adaptive testers, Belovs and Blais [BB16]
gave the first polynomial lower bound of Ω(d  e 1/4 ), since improved to Ω(d
                                                                           e 1/3 ) by Chen,
Waingarten and Xie [CWX17].
   Turning to real-valued functions, Fischer [Fis04] combined Ramsey theory arguments
with a result of Ergün et al. [EKKRV98] to show a Ω(log n) lower bound for adaptive testers

                                            21
on the line [n]. On the Boolean cube, Briët et al. [BCGM12] gave a Ω(d/ε) nonadaptive one-
sided lower bound, and Blais, Brody and Matulef [BBM12] gave an adaptive lower bound
of Ω(d). On the hypergrid, Blais, Raskhodnikova and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14b] gave a non-
adaptive lower bound of Ω(d log n) by communication complexity arguments, Chakrabarty
and Seshadhri [CS14] showed the optimal lower bound of Ω(d log(n)/ε − log(1/ε)/ε) for
adaptive testers using Ramsey theory (which involves functions with large range), and
Belovs[Bel18] gave an alternative proof of this bound that does not use Ramsey theory.


Other settings and models of testing. This thesis focuses on monotonicity testing
with respect to the uniform distribution over product domains (the Boolean cube, hyper-
grids, and the solid unit cube), when the algorithm is allowed to query the input function
at arbitrary points. Each of these conditions may be altered to obtain different, usually
more challenging settings. While we refrain from a full review here, we give some pointers
to the literature. If we drop the assumption that the distribution over the input domain is
uniform, but rather allow it to be an arbitrary unknown distribution D to which the algo-
rithm gets sample access, we obtain the challenging distribution-free testing setting, which
was defined in the early work of [GGR98] and directly studied by Halevy and Kushile-
vitz [HK07], who already showed that monotonicity testing requires exp(Θ(d)) queries in
this setting. We may instead keep the uniform distribution but require that our access to
the input f come from uniformly sampled points x only, rather than arbitrary points x.
This setting is called sample-based testing [GR16], and in this case, the number of samples
required is again known to be exponential in Θ(d) [GGLRS00; Bla24]. Finally, early work
[FLNRRS02] studied the challenging setting where the domain of f is a general partially-
ordered set, and recent work on proper learning of monotone functions has also obtained
progress in this setting [LRV22; LV23].


3.2         Classical and directed isoperimetric inequalities
The central object of study of this thesis is the connection between monotonicity and
directed analogues of classical isoperimetric inequalities. Let us trace here an extremely
brief history of developments that are most relevant to our study. We start with the
original work of Poincaré [Poi90], which yields inequalities of the type ∥f − E [f ] ∥Lp (Ω) ≤
C(Ω)∥|∇f |∥Lp (Ω) , i.e. (Lp , ℓ2 )-inequalities in our notation, for sufficiently smooth domains
Ω and sufficiently integrable1 functions f . The optimal constant C(Ω), also called the
  1
      More precisely, for f in the appropriate Sobolev space.


                                                     22
Poincaré constant of Ω, depends on properties of this domain2, and often the goal is to
establish the sharp constant for families of domains Ω. Payne and Weinberger [PW57] and
Acosta and Durán [AD04] (see also [Beb03]) showed that, for convex domains in the cases
p = 2 and p = 1 respectively, C(Ω) is essentially upper bounded by the diameter of Ω,
and this bound is tight in general. However, for specific structured domains such as the
product domain [0, 1]d , the diameter characterization falls short of yielding a dimension-free
inequality (see also the literature on logarithmic Sobolev inequalities [Gro75]).
    Making progress on this front in the discrete setting, the landmark work of Talagrand
[Tal93] gave an (L1 , ℓ2 )-inequality for domain Ω = {0, 1}d , with C = C(Ω) independent of
d, and established connections with earlier works of Margulis on graph connectivity [Mar74]
and Pisier on probability in Banach spaces [Pis86]. (More recently, Fourier-analytic proofs
of Talagrand’s inequality have also been given [KKKMS21; EKLM24].) In continuous set-
tings, similar results were first established for the Gaussian measure in connection with
the Gaussian isoperimetric inequality [Bob97; BL96; ST78; Bor75; Lat03]. Tying back
to our present settings of interest, Bobkov and Houdré [BH97] showed that a dimension-
independent Poincaré inequality also holds for product measures in Rn , implying in par-
ticular an (L1 , ℓ2 )-inequality (analogously to Talagrand’s result) for domain [0, 1]d .
    As outlined in Chapter 1, it is these dimension-free isoperimetric inequalities for dis-
crete and continuous product measures whose directed analogues have implications for the
structure of monotone functions and therefore for monotonicity testing. This connection
was first explicitly observed by Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [CS16], who obtained the first
o(d) query tester for monotonicity of functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} by showing a directed
analogue of the Margulis inequality for such functions. That inequality is similar to the
Poincaré inequalities we have discussed so far, but its guarantee is a combination of an
“edge boundary size” and a “vertex boundary size”, as follows. For each vertex x, write3
Γf (x) := 1 [∇− f (x) ̸= 0]. Chakrabarty and Seshadhri showed that, for Boolean functions
f , we have                                      − 2
                             distmono
                                 2    (f )2
                                            ≲ E  ∥∇ f ∥2 · E [Γf (x)] .                 (3.1)
                                                                      √ 2
Later on, Khot, Minzer and Safra [KMS18] obtained their O(         e d/ε ) tester for Boolean
                                           1 2
functions by showing the directed (L , ℓ )-Poincaré inequality (up to a logarithmic factor,

By writing ∥∇− f (x)∥2 = ∥∇− f (x)∥2 · Γf (x) and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
we see that (3.2) implies (3.1).
   Recent work [BCS23b; BKKM23; BKR24] has also explored close analogues of these
inequalities to tackle the problems of testing monotonicity of Boolean functions on the hy-
pergrid and real-valued functions on the Boolean cube (with respect to Hamming distance),
as we now briefly summarize.
   Define the vector-valued operators ϕ and ϕ− on functions f : [n]d → R as follows: for
each x ∈ [n]d and i ∈ [d],

          (ϕf (x))i := 1 [∃y : (x ⪯i y or y ⪯i x) and f (x) ̸= f (y)] ,
         (ϕ− f (x))i := 1 [(∃y : x ⪯i y, f (x) > f (y)) or (∃y : y ⪯i x, f (y) > f (x))] ,

where we write x ⪯i y if xj = yj for every j ̸= i, and xi ≤ yi . Compared to the
gradient, these operators 1) are only sensitive to the order relation between function values
(which suits the setting of Hamming testing); and 2) capture “long range” violations
of monotonicity (accordingly, the corresponding hypergrid testers are not edge testers).
Then, Poincaré-type inequalities where the gradient ∇ is replaced with ϕ (and similarly
for their directed counterparts) were given by Black, Chakrabarty and Seshadhri [BCS23b]
and Braverman et al. [BKKM23] for Boolean functions on the hypergrid, and by Black,
Kalemaj and Raskhodnikova [BKR24] for real-valued functions on the Boolean cube; and
these works gave monotonicity testers in the respective settings.
    One important aspect omitted in the discussion above is the notion of robustness for
isoperimetric inequalities in discrete settings, introduced by Khot, Minzer and Safra [KMS18]
and subsequently featuring in most other monotonicity testing results as well. At a high
level, the idea is that a directed isoperimetric inequality can be used to obtain an efficient
monotonicity tester by establishing the existence of a “good subgraph” of violating edges,
which is a generalization of a matching. However, even under a directed Poincaré inequal-
ity, it could informally be the case that a few vertices are responsible for many violating
edges, which seems insufficient to yield the existence of such a good subgraph (indeed, a
similar difficulty was already tackled in [CS16]). The idea of robustness, then, is to assign
each edge (x, y) a colour, red or blue, and then break up the directed gradient ∇− into two
components – a “red component” ∇−       red , which counts red edges but only at the vertices x
with f (x) = 1, and a “blue component” ∇−        blue , which counts blue edges but only at the

    Finally, in a recent development subsequent to the work in this thesis, Chakrabarty et

Boolean-valued functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} to all real-valued functions f : {0, 1}d → R,
using a thresholding argument of Berman, Raskhodnikova and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14a].
This answered (up to a logarithmic factor) a question left open by the work of Chap-

monotonicity testing of probability distributions, rather than functions, in a certain “sub-
cube conditioning” sampling model.

                                                           25
3.3     Lp testing
We have mentioned that the analytic setting of property testing, which we study in this the-
sis, departs from most previously-studied settings by making two complementary choices:
1) the input functions have real-valued domain and range; and 2) we measure distance with
respect to the Lp distance, rather than Hamming distance. The latter choice puts us in the
field of “Lp testing”, which was first systematically investigated by Berman, Raskhodnikova
and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14a]. We briefly summarize that work here, and then discuss the
connection with our results in more detail in Chapter 4.
   In [BRY14a], the authors initiated the systematic study of Lp testing of real-valued
functions defined on hypergrid domains, i.e. f : [n]d → R. Some type of regularity must be
assumed if one wishes to perform Lp testing of real-valued functions, and in [BRY14a] the
authors assumed that f has bounded range: their results are stated for functions taking
values in [0, 1], and other ranges [0, r] may be handled by appropriately rescaling both
the input function f and the parameter ε. They studied the properties of monotonicity,
Lipschitzness, convexity and submodularity.
     For monotonicity specifically, the authors gave nonadaptive Lp monotonicity testers
with one-sided error on the hypergrid [n]d with query complexity O((d/εp ) log(d/εp ))—
note this is independent of n, bypassing the Hamming testing lower bound—and a lower
bound of Ω((1/εp ) log(1/εp )) for nonadaptive testers with one-sided error; on the line [n],
they showed there is an O(1/εp ) nonadaptive tester with one-sided error and a matching
lower bound for adaptive testers with two-sided error. They also gave a reduction, based
on a thresholding argument, from L1 monotonicity testing to Hamming testing of Boolean
functions for nonadaptive one-sided testers, so in particular L1 testing functions with range
[0, 1] is no harder than Hamming testing functions with Boolean range. Finally, they stud-
ied the problem of approximating the L1 distance of f to monotonicity (i.e. the tolerant
testing framework of Parnas, Ron and Rubinfeld [PRR06]), and gave approximation al-
gorithms up to additive error δ with poly(1/δ) query complexity for the line (d = 1) and
two-dimensional grid (d = 2).


Parameterized testing. We also remark that our problem, which is parameterized by
the upper bound M on the Lipschitz constant of input functions, lies under the umbrella
of parameterized property testing, formally introduced by Pallavoor, Raskhodnikova and
Varma [PRV17], and refer to [PRV17] for an introduction to, and results on this type of
tester.



                                             26
3.4       Algebraic property testing over the reals
Another recent line of research [FY20; ABFKY23; AKM25], which is closely related to
the topic of this thesis, has revisited classic problems from algebraic property testing in
the fully real-valued setting: given query access to a function f : Rd → R, the goal is to
test whether f satisfies is a property such as linearity, or being a low-degree multivariate
polynomial.
     Specifically, Fleming and Yoshida [FY20] initiated this line of investigation by giv-
ing a linearity tester for functions f : Rd → R using O(1/ε)
                                                         e       queries. Then, Arora et
al. [ABFKY23] obtained a tester for degree-k functions using poly(k/ε) queries, and Arora,
Kelman and Meir [AKM25] simplified some of the techniques from the previous works while
eliminating logarithmic factors from the query complexity.
    The main difference between those works and ours lies in the notion of distance in the
formulation of the problem: the works [FY20; ABFKY23; AKM25] considered Hamming
distance with respect to an underlying distribution D over Rd , that is, the distance between
two functions f, g : Rd → R is
                                   dist(f, g) = P [f (x) ̸= g(x)] .
                                                 x∼D

In fact, those works studied the distribution-free testing setting studied by Halevy and
Kushilevitz [HK07], where the distribution D with respect to which distances are measured
is an unknown input, to which the algorithm only has sample access. Building upon the
“self-correct and test” approach of Halevy and Kushilevitz, they augmented central ideas
from algebraic property testing with a host of arguments and tools to handle the challenges
of the real-valued setting.
     In contrast, in this thesis we build upon earlier work of Berman, Raskhodnikova and
Yaroslavtsev [BRY14a] and measure distance according to the Lp norm, which contem-
plates not only whether functions f and g disagree at a point x, but also by how much.
While we make this choice primarily for conceptual reasons – Lp testing is a natural compu-
tational task – it is also interesting to observe the technical ramifications of such a choice:
Hamming testing and Lp testing seem to call for different mathematical tools that reveal
different aspects of our problems of interest, with Hamming distance being a more natural
fit for combinatorial and algebraic arguments, and Lp distance showing better affinity with
analytic arguments.4
   4
    Nevertheless, in Chapter 4 we will also see that the Lipschitz condition provides a bridge of sorts
between these two scenarios, indeed leading to a nearly-tight characterization of the query complexity of
L1 monotonicity testing on the line, by a reduction to the classic problem of testing sortedness [EKKRV98].


                                                    27
3.5     Testing properties of parametric models
Motivated by linear regression and sparse recovery, [CDS20] studied the problem of deciding
whether a noisy linear model y = w · x + η is k-sparse, meaning that the unknown weight
vector w ∈ Rd is supported on at most k elements, or whether w is ε-far from every k-
sparse vector in normalized ℓ2 distance. The algorithm receives samples (x, y = w · x + η),
where x is distributed according to a fixed product distribution D = X d and the noise η
is sampled independently at random from a fixed distribution, and must make its decision
using only these samples (rather than arbitrary queries). The main result of [CDS20] is
an algorithm whose sample complexity depends only on the one-dimensional marginal X
of D and on the distribution of the noise η, but not on d.
    The main similarity between the work of [CDS20] and this thesis is that [CDS20] also
applied the framework of property testing in a fully real-valued setting, i.e. the “noisy
function” x ∈ Rd 7→ w · x + η ∈ R. Since the input is assumed to come from a linear
model with added noise, we may see their result as an application of property testing to
a parametric setting in the language of statistics. Accordingly, their notion of distance for
testing is defined in terms of the parameter vector w ∈ Rd . In contrast, in this thesis we
apply property testing to Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R with no parametric specifi-
cation, and in this setting the natural notion of distance is in terms of the outputs of f
instead. One interesting connection between these two settings is that, in certain para-
metric situations, the Lipschitz condition assumed in our work could be derived a priori or
otherwise approximately tested by using the model specification.




                                             28
Part I: Monotonicity testing in the
         analytic setting




                 29
Chapter 4

Testing with O(d) queries via
rearrangements

In this chapter, we begin our investigation of directed (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequalities by
studying the (L1 , ℓ1 ) setting, which we use to derive upper bounds for L1 monotonicity
testing of Lipschitz functions defined on the hypergrid [n]d and solid unit cube [0, 1]d .
The main results in this chapter are a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequality for Lipschitz
functions f : [0, 1]d → R (Theorem 4.1), and an L1 monotonicity tester for M -Lipschitz
functions f : [0, 1]d → R using O(dM/ε) queries (Theorem 4.3). We also give lower bounds
for these problems, derive tighter results for the special case of the discrete and continuous
lines (i.e. d = 1), and compare our results to what can be derived from prior work on Lp
testing by Berman, Raskhodnikova and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14a].


4.1      Results and main ideas

4.1.1     Directed Poincaré-type inequalities
Our first result is a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequality for Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d →
R. This result may be seen as a continuous analogue of Theorem 1.1, which says that
Boolean functions which are far from monotone have many violating edges. Indeed, our
argument is inspired by the proof in [Ras99; GGLRS00], and the monotonicity tester we
will obtain from our inequality is a continuous analogue of the O(d) monotonicity tester
for Boolean functions.

                                                30
Theorem 4.1. Let f : [0, 1]d → R be a Lipschitz function with monotone rearrangement
f ∗ . Then
                      distmono (f ) ≍ E [|f − f ∗ |] ≲ E ∥∇− f ∥1 .
                                                                
                          1                                                     (4.1)

    As hinted in the statement, a crucial tool for this result is the monotone rearrangement
f ∗ of f . We construct f ∗ by a sequence of axis-aligned rearrangements R1 , . . . , Rn ; each Ri
is the non-symmetric monotone rearrangement operator along dimension i, which naturally
generalizes the sorting operator of [GGLRS00] to the continuous case. For each coordinate
i ∈ [d], the operator Ri takes f into an equimeasurable function Ri f that     is  monotone    in
                                                                                 −
                                                                                      
the i-th coordinate, at a “cost” E [|f − Ri f |] that is upper bounded by E |∂i f | . We show
that each application Ri can only decrease the “cost” associated with further applications
Rj , so that the total cost of obtaining f ∗ (i.e. the middle expression in (4.1)) may be upper
bounded, via the triangle inequality, by the sum of all directed partial derivatives, i.e. the
right-hand side of (4.1).
   A technically simpler version of this argument also yields a directed (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré
inequality for real-valued functions on the hypergrid. We also note that Theorems 4.1
and 4.2 are both tight up to constant factors.

Theorem 4.2. Let f : [n]d → R and let f ∗ be its monotone rearrangement. Then

                     distmono (f ) ≍ E [|f − f ∗ |] ≲ nE ∥∇− f ∥1 .
