                         1


    Table 3.1 in Chapter 3 shows these two results in the broader context of classical and
directed isoperimetric inequalities for continuous and discrete settings.


4.1.2     Testing monotonicity on the unit cube and hypergrid
Equipped with the results above, we give a monotonicity tester for Lipschitz functions
f : [0, 1]d → R, and the same technique yields a tester for functions on the hypergrid as
well. The testers are parameterized by an upper bound M on the Lipschitz constant of f
with respect to the ℓ1 geometry, in the terminology from Chapter 2.
   Both of our testers are partial derivative testers. These are algorithms which only have
access to a directional derivative oracle and, moreover, their queries are promised to be
axis-aligned vectors. In the discrete case, these are usually called edge testers [GGLRS00;
CS13].



                                               31
Theorem 4.3. There is a nonadaptive partial derivative L1 monotonicity tester for (ℓ1 , M )-
Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R with query complexity O dM
                                                               
                                                             ε
                                                                 and one-sided error.
   Similarly, there is a nonadaptive partial derivative L1 monotonicity tester for (ℓ1 , M )-
Lipschitz functions f : [n]d with query complexity O dnMε
                                                             and one-sided error.

    The testers work by sampling points x and coordinates i ∈ [d] uniformly at random,
and using directional derivative queries to reject if ∂i− f (x) < 0. Their correctness is shown
using Theorems 4.1 and 4.2, which imply that, when f is ε-far from monotone in L1 -
distance, the total magnitude of its negative partial derivatives must be large—and since
each partial derivative is at most M by assumption, the values ∂i− f (x) must be strictly
negative in a set of large measure, which the tester stands good chance of hitting with the
given query complexity.


4.1.3     Testing monotonicity on the line
The results above, linking a directed Poincaré inequality with a monotonicity tester that
uses partial derivative queries and has linear dependence on d, paint a close parallel between
our analytic setting and the case of the edge tester on the Boolean cube [GGLRS00]. On the
other hand, we also show a strong separation between Hamming and L1 testing. Focusing
on the simpler problem of monotonicity testing on the line, we show that the tight query
complexity of L1 monotonicity testing Lipschitz functions grows with the square root of
the size of the (continuous or discrete) domain.

Theorem 4.4. There exist nonadaptive L1 monotonicitytesters for
                                                               M -Lipschitz functions
                                                       p
f : [0, n] → R and f : [n] → R with query complexity O
                                                     e  nM/ε . The testers use value
queries and have one-sided error.

   This result (along with the near-tight lower bounds in Section 4.1.4) stands in contrast
with the case of Hamming testing functions f : [n] → R, which has sample complexity
Θ(log n) [EKKRV98; Fis04; BRY14b; Bel18]. Intuitively, this difference arises because an
M -Lipschitz function may violate monotonicity with rate of change M , so the area under
the curve may grow quadratically in violating regions. The proof is in fact a reduction to
the Hamming case, using the Lipschitz assumption to establish a connection between the
L1 and Hamming distances to monotonicity.




                                              32
4.1.4       Lower bounds
We give two types of lower bounds: under no assumptions about the tester and for constant
d, we show that the dependence of Theorem 4.3 on M/ε is close to optimal1. We give
stronger bounds for the special case of partial derivative testers (such as the ones from
Theorem 4.3), essentially showing that our analysis of the partial derivative tester is tight.
      We first state our general lower bound for constant dimension d.

Theorem 4.5. Let d be a constant. Any L1 monotonicity tester (with two-sided error,
and adaptive value and directional
                                  derivative
                                             queries) for (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz functions f :
                                       d
[0, 1]d → R requires at least Ω (M/ε) d+1 queries.

    Similarly, any L1 monotonicity tester (with two-sided errornand adaptive queries)
                                                                                 o     for
                                                                         d
  1                                 d                                          d
(ℓ , M )-Lipschitz functions f : [n] → R requires at least Ω min (nM/ε) d+1 , n     queries.

   Note that the bounds above cannot be improved beyond logarithmic factors, because
they nearly attain the upper bounds for the line from Theorem 4.4. It also follows that
adaptivity (essentially) does not help with L1 monotonicity testing on the line, matching
the situation for Hamming testing [Fis04; CS14; Bel18].
    Theorem 4.5 is obtained via a “hole” construction, which hides a non-monotone region
of f inside an ℓ1 -ball B of radius r. We choose r such the violations of monotonicity inside
B are large enough to make f ε-far from monotone, but at the same time, the ball B is
hard to find using few queries. However, this construction has poor dependence on d.
    To lower bound the query complexity of partial derivative testers with better depen-
dence on d, we employ a simpler “step” construction, which essentially chooses a coordinate
i and hides a small negative-slope region on every line along coordinate i. These functions
are far from monotone, but a partial derivative tester must correctly guess both i and
the negative-slope region to detect them. We conclude that Theorem 4.3 is optimal for
partial derivative testers on the unit cube, and optimal for edge testers on the hypergrid
for constant ε and M .

Theorem 4.6. Any partial derivative L1 monotonicity tester for (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz func-
tions f : [0, 1]d → R (with two-sided error and adaptive queries) requires at least Ω(dM/ε)
queries.
  1
    Note that one may always multiply the input values by 1/M to reduce the problem to the case with
Lipschitz constant 1 and proximity parameter ε/M , so this is the right ratio to look at.

Table 4.1: Query complexity bounds for testing monotonicity on the unit cube and hyper-
grid. Upper bounds are for nonadaptive (n.a.) algorithms with one-sided error (1-s.), and
lower bounds are for adaptive algorithms with two-sided error, unless stated otherwise. For

or for partial derivative testers (p.d.t.).

cube and hypergrid, along with the analogous Hamming testing results for intuition and
bounds for L1 testing from prior works. See Section 4.6 for a comparison of our upper
bounds and those given in [BRY14a] for L1 testing of monotonicity, and an explanation of
how we derive the upper bounds shown in the table from their results (which are stated
under incomparable assumptions, namely bounded range instead of Lipschitz continuity).
We sketch in Appendix A how to obtain the Ω(d log n) nonadaptive lower bound shown in
the table via the construction of Blais, Raskhodnikova and Yaroslavtsev [BRY14b] for the
Hamming setting.


Organization. The rest of this chapter is organized as follows. In Section 4.2, we prove
our directed Poincaré inequalities on the unit cube (Theorem 4.1) and hypergrid (Theo-

                                                            34
rem 4.2), and in Section 4.3 we give our L1 monotonicity testers for these domains (Theo-
rem 4.3). Section 4.4 gives the upper bound for testing functions on the line (Theorem 4.4),
and in Section 4.5 we prove our lower bounds (Theorems 4.5 and 4.6). In Section 4.6, we
discuss the relation between our results and those from prior work on Lp testing [BRY14a]
and sketch the upper bounds shown in Table 4.1 derived from their results. Section 4.7
concludes this chapter with a brief discussion of the results and some open questions.


4.2      Directed Poincaré inequalities
In this section, we establish Theorems 4.1 and 4.2. We start with the one-dimensional case,
i.e. functions on the line, and then generalize to higher dimensions. In each subsection, we
focus our presentation on the setting where the domain is continuous (corresponding to our
results for the unit cube), and then show how the same proof strategy (more easily) yields
analogous results for discrete domains (corresponding to our results for the hypergrid).


4.2.1     One-dimensional case
Let n > 0, let I := (0, n), and let f : I → R be a measurable function. We wish to show
that ∥f − f ∗ ∥L1 (I) ≲ n ∥∂ − f ∥L1 (I) , where f ∗ is the monotone rearrangement of f . We first
introduce the monotone rearrangement, and then show this inequality using an elementary
calculus argument.


Monotone rearrangement

Here, we introduce the (non-symmetric, non-decreasing) monotone rearrangement of a one-
dimensional function. We follow the definition of [Kaw85], with the slight modification
that we are interested in the non-decreasing rearrangement, whereas most of the literature
usually favours the non-increasing rearrangement. The difference is purely syntactic, and
our choice more conveniently matches the convention in the monotonicity testing literature.
Up to this choice, our definition also agrees with that of [BS88, Chapter 2], and we refer
the reader to these two texts for a comprehensive treatment.
   We define the (lower) level sets of f : I → R as the sets
                                       
                                 I c := x ∈ I : f (x) ≤ c


                                               35
for all c ∈ R. For nonempty measurable S ⊂ R of finite measure, the rearrangement of S
is the set
                                   S ∗ := [0, L(S)]
(recall that L denotes the Lebesgue measure), and we define ∅∗ := ∅. For a level set I c , we
        ∗              ∗
write I c to mean I c .

Definition 4.7. The monotone rearrangement of f is the function f ∗ : I → R given by
                                      n              ∗
                                                       o
                           ∗
                          f (x) := inf c ∈ R : x ∈ I c .                          (4.2)

   Note that f ∗ is always a non-decreasing function.
    We note two well-known properties of the monotone rearrangement: equimeasurability
and order preservation. Two functions f, g are called equimeasurable if L{f ≥ c} = L{g ≥
c} for every c ∈ R. A mapping u 7→ u∗ is called order preserving if f (x) ≤ g(x) for all
x ∈ I implies f ∗ (x) ≤ g ∗ (x) for all x ∈ I. See [BS88, Chapter 2, Proposition 1.7] for a
proof of the following facts.

Fact 4.8. Let f : I → R be a measurable function. Then f and f ∗ are equimeasurable.

Fact 4.9. The mapping f 7→ f ∗ is order preserving.


Absolutely continuous functions and the one-dimensional Poincaré inequality

Let f : I → R be absolutely continuous. It follows that f has a derivative ∂f almost
everywhere (i.e. outside a set of measure zero), ∂f ∈ L1 (I) (i.e. its derivative is Lebesgue
integrable), and                               Z          x
                                   f (x) = f (0) +            ∂f (t) dt
                                                      0

for all x ∈ I. It also follows that ∂ − f ∈ L1 (I).
   We may now show our one-dimensional inequality.

Lemma 4.10. Let f : I → R be absolutely continuous. Then ∥f − f ∗ ∥L1 (I) ≤ 2n ∥∂ − f ∥L1 (I) .

Discrete case

Let n ∈ N and let I := [n]. We may define the monotone rearrangement f ∗ : I → R of
f : I → R as in Definition 4.7 by identifying I with I and writing S ∗ := [|S|] for each
finite S ⊂ N. More directly, f ∗ is the function such that f ∗ (1) ≤ f ∗ (2) ≤ · · · ≤ f ∗ (n) is
the sorted sequence of the values f (1), f (2), . . . , f (n). It is easy to show that the directed
version of Lemma 4.10 holds, and in fact one may simply repeat the proof of that lemma.

Lemma 4.11. Let f : [n] → R. Then ∥f − f ∗ ∥L1 (I) ≤ 2n ∥∂ − f ∥L1 (I) .



                                                     37
4.2.2      Multidimensional case
In the continuous case, we ultimately only require an inequality on the unit cube [0, 1]n .
However, we will first work in slightly more generality and consider functions defined on a
box in Rd , defined below. This approach makes some of the steps more transparent, and
also gives intuition for the discrete case of the hypergrid.

Definition 4.12. Let a ∈ Rd>0 . The box of size a is the closure B ⊂ Rd of B = (0, a1 ) ×
· · · × (0, ad ).

For the rest of this section, B ⊂ Rd will always denote such a box.


Notation. For x ∈ Rd , y ∈ R and i ∈ [d], we will use the notation x−i to de-
note the vector in R[d]\{i} obtained by removing the i-th coordinate from x (note that
the indexing is not changed), and we will write (x−i , y) as a shorthand for the vector
(x1 , . . . , xi−1 , y, xi+1 , . . . , xd ) ∈ Rd . We will also write x−i directly to denote any vector in
R[d]\{i} . For function f : B → R and x−i ∈ R[d]\{i} , we will write fx−i for the function given
by fx−i (y) = f (x−i , y) for all (x−i , y) ∈ B. For any set D ∈ Rd , we will denote by D−i the
projection {x−i : x ∈ D}, and extend this notation in the natural way to more indices, e.g.
D−i−j .

Definition 4.13 (Rearrangement in direction i). Let f : B → R be a measurable function
and let i ∈ [d]. The rearrangement of f in direction i is the function Ri f : B → R given
by
                                  (Ri f )x−i := (fx−i )∗                            (4.3)
                  −i
for all x−i ∈ B . We call each Ri the rearrangement operator in direction i.
                 


    We may put (4.3) in words as follows: on each line in direction i determined by point
x−i , the restriction of Ri f to that line is the monotone rearrangement of the restriction of
f to that line.

Proposition 4.14. Let B be the box of size a ∈ Rd , and let f : B → R be Lipschitz. Then
for each i ∈ [d],
                         ∥f − Ri f ∥L1 (B) ≤ 2ai ∂i− f L1 (B) .

Proof. Since f is Lipschitz, each fx−i : [0, ai ] → R is Lipschitz and a fortiori absolutely
continuous. The result follows from Lemma 4.10, using Tonelli’s theorem to choose the

                                                   38
order of integration.

   A key ingredient in our multi-dimensional argument is that the rearrangement operator
preserves Lipschitz continuity.

Lemma 4.15 ([Kaw85, Lemma 2.12]). If f : B → R is Lipschitz (with Lipschitz constant
L), then Ri f is Lipschitz (with Lipschitz constant 2L).

   We are now ready to define the (multidimensional) monotone rearrangement f ∗ of f .

Definition 4.16. Let f : B → R be Lipschitz. The monotone rearrangement of f is the
function
                              f ∗ := Rd Rd−1 · · · R1 f .

   We first show that f ∗ is indeed a monotone function.

Proposition 4.17. Let f : B → R be Lipschitz. Then f ∗ is monotone.

Proof. Say that g : B → R is monotone in direction i if gx−i is non-decreasing for all
           −i
x−i ∈ B . Then g is monotone if and only if it is monotone in direction i for every
i ∈ [d]. Note that Ri f is monotone in direction i by definition of monotone rearrangement.
Therefore, it suffices to prove that if f is monotone in direction j, then Ri f is also monotone
in direction j.
   Suppose f is monotone in direction j, and suppose i < j without loss of generality. Let
                                       −j
a ∈ Rd be the size of B. Let x−j ∈ B       and 0 ≤ y1 < y2 ≤ aj , so that (x−j , y1 ), (x−j , y2 ) ∈
B. We need to show that (Ri f )(x−j , y1 ) ≤ (Ri f )(x−j , y2 ). Let I i := [0, ai ]. For each
k ∈ {1, 2}, let gk : I i → R be given by

                  gk (z) := f (x1 , . . . , xi−1 , z, xi+1 , . . . , xj−1 , yk , xj+1 , . . . , xn ) .

Note that
                gk∗ (z) = (Ri f )(x1 , . . . , xi−1 , z, xi+1 , . . . , xj−1 , yk , xj+1 , . . . , xn )
for every z ∈ I i , and therefore our goal is to show that g1∗ (xi ) ≤ g2∗ (xi ). But f being
monotone in direction j means that g1 (z) ≤ g2 (z) for all z ∈ I i , so by the order preserving
property (Fact 4.9) of the monotone rearrangement we get that g1∗ (xi ) ≤ g2∗ (xi ), which
concludes the proof.


                                                          39
    It is well-known that the monotone rearrangement is a nonexpansive operator. Actually
a stronger fact holds, as we note below.

Proposition 4.18 ([CT80]). Let n > 0 and let f, g ∈ L1 [0, n]. Then f ∗ , g ∗ satisfy
                      Z                     Z

                            (f − g ) dx ≤         (f − g)− dx
                            [0,n]                         [0,n]

and                         Z                         Z

                              [0,n]                   [0,n]


   The result above is stated for functions on the interval. Taking the integral over the
box B and repeating for each operator Ri yields the nonexpansiveness of our monotone
rearrangement operator, as also noted by [Kaw85].

Corollary 4.19. Let f, g ∈ L1 (B). Then ∥f ∗ − g ∗ ∥L1 (B) ≤ ∥f − g∥L1 (B) .

   We show that the rearrangement operator can only make the norm of the directed
partial derivatives smaller, i.e. decrease the violations of monotonicity, which is the key
step in this proof. This result may be see as a directed version of nonexpansiveness.

Proposition 4.20. Let f : B → R be Lipschitz and let i, j ∈ [d]. Then ∂j− (Ri f ) L1 (B) ≤
 ∂j− f L1 (B) .

Proof. We may assume that i ̸= j, since otherwise the left-hand side is zero. We will use the
following convention for variables names: w ∈ Rd will denote points in B; z ∈ R[d]\{i,j} will

and y ∈ R will denote points in (0, aj ) (indexing the j-th dimension). Recall that, for each
i ∈ [d], ei denotes the i-th basis vector.
   Since f is Lipschitz, so is Ri f by Lemma 4.15. By Rademacher’s theorem, these
functions are differentiable almost everywhere. Therefore, let D ⊆ B be a measurable

Equalities (BC1) and (BC2) hold by the bounded convergence theorem, which applies be-
cause the difference quotients are uniformly bounded by the Lipschitz constants of Ri f
and f (respectively), and because Ri f and f are differentiable in D (which gives pointwise
convergence of the limits). Equalities (D1) and (D2) hold again by the uniform bounded-
ness of the difference quotients, along with the fact that L(B \ D) = 0. Equalities (T1) and
(T2) hold by Tonelli’s theorem. Finally, the inequality holds by Proposition 4.18, since
(Ri f )(z, y + h, ·) is the monotone rearrangement of f (z, y + h, ·) and (Ri f )(z, y, ·) is the
monotone rearrangement of f (z, y, ·).

Corollary 4.22. Let B = (0, 1)d and let f : B → R be Lipschitz. Then
                                            Z

     To complete the proof of Theorem 4.1, we need to show that distmono
                                                                      1   (f ) ≍ E [|f − f ∗ |],
i.e. that the monotone rearrangement is “essentially optimal” as a target monotone function
for f . The inequality distmono
                            1   (f ) ≤ E [|f − f ∗ |] is immediate from the fact that f ∗ is
monotone. The inequality in the other direction follows from the nonexpansiveness of the
rearrangement operator, with essentially the same proof as that of [KMS18] for the Boolean
cube, as we now show.

Proposition 4.23. Let f : [0, 1]d → R be Lipschitz. Then E [|f − f ∗ |] ≤ 2distmono
                                                                               1    (f ).

Proof. Let B := (0, 1)d and let g ∈ L1 (B) be any monotone function. It follows that g ∗ = g
almost everywhere (that is, except perhaps at jump discontinuities of g). By Corollary 4.19,
we have that ∥f ∗ − g ∗ ∥L1 (B) ≤ ∥f − g∥L1 (B) . Using the triangle inequality, we obtain
             ∥f − f ∗ ∥L1 (B) ≤ ∥f − g∥L1 (B) + ∥g − f ∗ ∥L1 (B)
                               = ∥f − g∥L1 (B) + ∥f ∗ − g ∗ ∥L1 (B) ≤ 2 ∥f − g∥L1 (B) .
The claim follows by taking the infimum over the choice of g.

                                                    42
Tightness of the inequality. To check that Corollary 4.22 is tight up to constant
factors, it suffices to take the linear function f : [0, 1]d → R given by f (x) = 1 − x1 for all
x ∈ [0, 1]d . Then f ∗ is given by f ∗ (x) = x1 , so E [|f − f ∗ |] = 1/2 while E [∥∇− f ∥1 ] = 1, as
needed.

Discrete case

The proof above carries over to the case of the hypergrid almost unmodified, as we now
outline. We now consider functions f : [n]d → R, so the box B is replaced with [n]d
and its dimensions ai are all replaced with the length n of the hypergrid. We define
the rearrangement in direction i, Ri f , as in Definition 4.13 by sorting the restrictions of
f to each line along direction i. We also define f ∗ as in Definition 4.16 by subsequent
applications of each operator Ri . Then Proposition 4.14 carries over by applying the one-
dimensional Lemma 4.11, and the proof of Proposition 4.17 carries over unmodified.
   The nonexpansiveness properties Proposition 4.18 and Corollary 4.19 also carry over
unmodified, and the key Proposition 4.20 carries over with a more immediate proof: the
use of Proposition 4.18 remains the same, but rather than expanding the definition of
derivative and reasoning about the limit, the discrete argument boils down to showing the
inequality
           Z                                        Z
                                              −
               ((Ri f )(w + ej ) − (Ri f )(w)) dw ≤     (f (w + ej ) − f (w))− dw ,
            [n]d                                         [n]d

which follows immediately from the discrete version of Proposition 4.18 by summing over
all lines in direction i. Then, the hypergrid version of Theorem 4.21 follows by the same ap-
plication of the triangle inequality, and we conclude the inequality portion of Theorem 4.2.

Theorem 4.24. Let f : [n]d → R. Then E [|f − f ∗ |] ≤ 2nE [∥∇− f ∥1 ].

   The discrete version of Proposition 4.23 follows identically, and we state it here for
convenience.

Proposition 4.25. Let f : [n]d → R. Then E [|f − f ∗ |] ≤ 2distmono
                                                               1    (f ).

   Finally, the tightness of Theorem 4.24 is mostly easily verified via the following step
function: letting n be even for simplicity, define f : [n]d → R by
                                         (
                                            1 if x1 ≤ n/2 ,
                                 f (x) =
                                            0 if x1 > n/2 .

                                                 43
Then f ∗ is obtained by flipping this function along the first coordinate, or equivalently
swapping the values 1 and 0 in the definition above. Thus E [|f − f ∗ |] = 1. On the other
hand, ∥∇− f ∥1 takes value 1 on exactly one point in each line along the first coordinate,
and 0 elsewhere. Hence E [∥∇− f ∥1 ] = 1/n, as needed.


4.3     Monotonicity testers
In this section, we use the directed Poincaré inequalities on the unit cube and hypergrid
to show that the natural partial derivative tester (or edge tester) attains the upper bounds
from Theorem 4.3.
   Let Ω denote either [0, 1]d or [n]d , and let q(Ω, M, ε) denote the query complexity of
our testers for (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz functions on these domains, as follows:
                                                                             
                   d                dM                      d               dnM
           q([0, 1] , M, ε) := Θ              and      q([n] , M, ε) := Θ         .
                                     ε                                        ε

   The tester is given in Algorithm 1. It is clear that this algorithm is a nonadaptive
partial derivative tester, and that it always accepts monotone functions. It suffices to show
that it rejects with good probability when distmono
                                                 1   (f ) > ε.

Algorithm 1 L1 monotonicity tester for Lipschitz functions using partial derivative queries
   Input: Partial derivative oracle access to Lipschitz function f : Ω → R.
   Output: Accept if f is monotone, reject if distmono
                                                   1   (f ) > ε.
   Requirement: f is (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz.
 procedure PartialDerivativeTester(f, Ω, M, ε)
    repeat q(Ω, M, ε) times
        Sample x ∈ Ω uniformly at random.
        Sample i ∈ [d] uniformly at random.
        Reject if ∂i f (x) < 0.
    end repeat
    Accept.

Lemma 4.26. Let Ω be one of [0, 1]d or [n]d , and let f : Ω → R be an (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz
function. Suppose distmono
                      1    (f ) > ε. Then Algorithm 1 rejects with probability at least 2/3.



                                             44
Proof. Write µ for the uniform measure over Ω, which is the Lebesgue measure if Ω = [0, 1]d

4.4      L1-testing monotonicity on the line
In this section, we show the upper bounds for L1 monotonicity testing on the line from
Theorem 4.4. The main idea is to reduce from L1 testing to Hamming testing by using
the Lipschitz constant to show that, if the L1 distance to monotonicity is large, then the
Hamming distance to monotonicity must be somewhat large as well; combined with the
Hamming testers [EKKRV98; Bel18], this yields an L1 tester for the discrete line [n].
    To obtain a tester for the continuous line [0, n], we furthermore apply a discretization
strategy inspired by domain reduction and downsampling ideas from previous works on
property testing [BCS20; HY22]. The idea is that, given ε and M , we may impose a fine
enough grid on [0, n] such that the function defined on that grid preserves the L1 distance
to monotonicity compared to the continuous function; again, the Lipschitz assumption is
essential for this step.
    In this section, we will follow the convention of denoting functions on continuous do-
mains by f, g, and those on discrete domains by f , g. Depending on the context, it will
be clear whether f is an arbitrary function or one obtained by discretizing a particular
function f . We will also write “f is M -Lipschitz” without specifying the ℓq geometry, since
all choices are equivalent in one dimension.

Lemma 4.27 (Discretization preserves distance to monotonicity). Let n, M, ε > 0 and
let f : [0, n] → R be an M -Lipschitz function. Let the discretized function f : [n′ ] → R,
for suitable choice of n′ = Θ (nM/ε), be given by f (i) = f (δi) for each i ∈ [n′ ], where
δ := n/n′ . Then if distmono
                        1    (f ) > ε, we have distmono
                                                   1    (f ) > ε/4.

                                                    46

distmono
    1    (f ) > ε.
     Let g : [n′ ] → R be the monotone rearrangement of f . It is easy to check that g is
Lipschitz with at most the Lipschitz constant of f . Let g : [0, n] → R be the following
piecewise linear function whose discretization is g: for each i ∈ [n′ ] we set g(δi) = g(i),
and g is the linear spline induced by these points elsewhere (and constant in the segment
[0, δ]). Then clearly g is monotone, and thus E [|f − g|] > ε. Moreover, g is M -Lipschitz,
since its steepest slope is the same as that of g up to the coordinate change.3 Hence, we

4.5      Lower bounds
In this section, we prove our lower bounds for testing monotonicity on the unit cube and
on the hypergrid. We first show our general lower bounds based on a “hole” construction,
which hides a monotonicity violating region inside a randomly placed ℓ1 -ball; these bounds
imply near tightness of our upper bounds for testing on the line from Section 4.4. Then
we give our lower bounds for partial derivative testers, which show that the analysis of our
tester in Section 4.3 is tight.

Definition 4.32 (ℓ1 -ball). Let Ω be one of Rd or Zd , let x ∈ Ω and let r > 0 be a real
number. The ℓ1 -ball of radius r centered at x is the set B1d (r, x) := {y ∈ Ω : ∥x − y∥1 ≤ r}.
We also write B1d (r) := B1d (r, 0).

    It will be clear from context whether the domain should be taken to be continuous or
discrete, i.e. whether B1d (r, c) should be understood under Ω = Rd or Ω = Zd . Let ν denote
the Lebesgue measure when Ω = Rd , and the counting measure over Zd when Ω = Zd .
   We give the following simple bounds on the volume of continuous and discrete ℓ1 -balls.
Since we do not require particularly tight bounds, we opt for a simple formulation and
elementary proof.

d ∈ N. Let Ω be one of Rd or Zd . Let r ∈ R satisfy r > 0 if Ω = Rd , and r ≥ 1 if Ω = Zd .
Then

Remark 4.34. The constants c1 (d) and c2 (d) in Proposition 4.33 have poor dependence
on d, and this is unavoidable at least in the continuous case. This fact is essentially the
reason why our general lower bound construction is only efficient for constant d.

    We now prove our tester-independent lower bounds. Note that there exists a tester
for (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz functions with proximity parameter ε if and only if there exists a
tester for (ℓ1 , 1)-Lipschitz functions with proximity parameter ε/M (by rescaling the input).
Therefore it suffices to prove the theorems for the case M = 1. The following two theorems
establish the continuous and discrete cases of Theorem 4.5.

    The construction for the partial derivative tester lower bounds is simpler: we start with
a one-dimensional “step” construction which is flat everywhere except for a small region
of negative slope, and then copy this function onto every line along a randomly chosen
coordinate i. Then a partial derivative tester must correctly guess both i and the negative-
slope region to detect such functions. The following two theorems establish the continuous
and discrete cases of Theorem 4.6.

                                                  54
Theorem 4.37 (Lower bound for partial derivative testers on the unit cube). Any partial
derivative L1 monotonicity tester for (ℓ1 , 1)-Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R (with two-
sided error and adaptive queries) requires at least Ω(d/ε) queries.

Proof. Let ε ≤ 1/6. For any z ∈ 31 , 23 − ε , let gz : [0, 1] → R be the function given by
                                           

                                 ε
                                                  if x < z ,

                                 
                                   0               if x > z + ε .
                                 

Note that gz is 1-Lipschitz. Moreover, we claim that distmono    1     (gz ) ≳ ε. Indeed, for any
x ∈ [0, 1/3], we have that gz (x) = ε and gz (2/3 + x) = 0. On the other hand, for any
monotone function h : [0, 1] → R we must have h(x) ≤ h(2/3 + x). Thus, for any such h we
have |gz (x) − h(x)| + |gz (2/3 + x) − h(2/3 + x)| ≥ ε. Since this holds for all x ∈ [0, 1/3],
we conclude that for any such h we must have E [|gz − h|] ≥ ε/3, proving the claim.
    Now, for any i ∈ [d] and z ∈ 31 , 23 − ε , let fi,z : [0, 1]d → R be given by copying gz
                                               

onto f along every line in direction i, i.e. setting fi,z (x) = gz (xi ) for every x ∈ [0, 1]d . Note
that f is (ℓ1 , 1)-Lipschitz (since its partial derivatives are 0 along non-i coordinates), and
distmono
    1    (f ) ≳ ε (since the lines in direction i partition the domain).
   We construct a set of Ω(d/ε) functions fi,z as follows. First, i can be any of the
                                                               1
coordinates in [d]. Then
                      1 2let z1, . . . , zk be given by zj = 3 + kε for k = Ω(1/ε), such that for
each j we have zj ∈ 3 , 3 − ε and, moreover, for distinct j, ℓ ∈ [k], the regions where fi,zj
and fi,zℓ take non-zero slope are disjoint. It follows that each partial derivative query may
only rule out one such fi,z , so any partial derivative tester that distinguishes an fi,z chosen
uniformly at random from the constant-0 function must make at least Ω(d/ε) queries.

    The argument for the hypergrid is similar, except that the construction cannot be made
to occupy an arbitrarily small region of the domain when the domain is discrete. We opt
to keep the argument simple and give a proof for constant parameter ε.

Theorem 4.38 (Lower bound for edge testers on the hypergrid). For sufficiently small
constant ε, any partial derivative L1 monotonicity tester for (ℓ1 , 1)-Lipschitz functions
f : [n]d → R (with two-sided error and adaptive queries) requires at least Ω(dn) queries.

Proof. Let n be a multiple of 3 for simplicity. For each z ∈ n3 + 1, . . . , 2n

4.6       Comparison with prior work on Lp testing for
          bounded-range functions
[BRY14a] initiated the systematic study of Lp -testing and, most relevant to our results,
established the first (and, to our knowledge, only previous) results on Lp testing of mono-
tonicity on the hypergrid and on the discrete line. While our models are broadly compat-
ible, a subtle but crucial distinction must be explained.
    In [BRY14a], the authors focused their exposition on the case of functions f : Ω → [0, 1],
and in this regime, L1 testing is only be easier than Hamming testing—which they show
via a reduction based on Boolean threshold functions. On the other hand, for functions
with other ranges, say f : Ω → [a, b], their definition normalizes the notion of distance
                 1
by a factor of b−a  . In our terminology, letting r := b − a and g := f /r, it follows that
dist1 (g) = distmono
    mono
                   1    (f )/r, so testing f with proximity parameter ε reduces to testing
g with proximity parameter ε/r. For Hamming testers with query complexity that scale
linearly with 1/ε, this amounts to paying a factor of r in the reduction to the Boolean case5 .
This loss is indeed necessary, because by the same reasoning, testing g with proximity
parameter ε reduces to testing f with proximity parameter rε. Therefore the problems of
testing f with proximity parameter ε and testing f /r with proximity parameter ε/r have
the same query complexity.
   In this thesis, we do not normalize the distance metric by r; we would like to handle
functions f that may take large values as the dimension d grows, as long as f satisfies
a Lipschitz assumption, and our goal is to beat the query complexity afforded by the
reduction to the Boolean case. We derive such a benchmark in Section 4.6.1 by assuming
that the input f is Lipschitz, and inferring an upper bound for r based on the Lipschitz
constant and the size of the domain. Combining the hypergrid tester of [BRY14a] and
   5
    This factor can also be tracked explicitly in the characterization of the L1 distance to monotonicity of
[BRY14a]: it arises in Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2, where an integral from 0 to 1 must be changed to an integral
from a to b, so the best threshold function is only guaranteed to be ε/r-far from monotone.


                                                    56
a discretization argument for the unit cube inspired by [BCS20; HY22], we obtain upper
bounds for our setting.
    With the discussion above in mind, it is instructive to return to Table 4.1. We note that
our upper bounds have polynomially smaller dependence on d than the benchmarks, sug-
gesting that our use of the Lipschitz assumption—via our directed Poincaré inequalities—
exploits useful structure underlying the monotonicity testing problem (whereas the bench-
mark testers must work for every function with bounded range, not only the Lipschitz
ones). Our lower bounds feature an almost-linear dependence on the hypergrid length n;
intuitively, this dependence is not implied by previous works such as [BRY14a; BRY14b] be-
cause those works construct the violations of monotonicity via Boolean functions, whereas
our constructions exploit the fact that a Lipschitz function can vastly exceed the [0, 1]
range along a given direction, which exacerbates the L1 distance to monotonicity in the
region where that happens.

                               1

Proof. The algorithm simulates the tester from Theorem 4.39 with parameters r = M nd
and ε, which gives the announced query complexity. As for correctness, note that since
f is (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz, for any x, y ∈ [n]d we have |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M ∥x − y∥1 ≤ M nd.

                                             57
Thus f has range of size at most M nd, so the reduction to the tester from Theorem 4.39
is correct.

    We outline how this result implies a tester for Lipschitz functions on the unit cube as
well, via the domain reduction or downsampling principle of [BCS20; HY22], and similarly
to our work on the one-dimensional case in Section 4.4. Even though the tester above has
query complexity that depends on the hypergrid length n, the main observation is that,
given an (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz function f on [0, 1]d , we may discretize it into an (ℓ1 , M/n)-
Lipschitz function f ′ on an arbitrarily fine hypergrid [n]d by choosing n large enough.
Then the term n(M/n) = M remains fixed for any choice of n, so the complexity of the
tester above applied to f ′ does not depend on n. Then, by setting n large enough, we may
upper bound the error introduced by the discretization.

Corollary 4.41. Thereis an L1 monotonicity
                                               tester for (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz functions f :

uniformly on [0, 1]d . More precisely, we define a function f ′ : [n]d → R via f ′ (x) = f (x/n)
for all x ∈ [n]d . Note that f ′ is (ℓ1 , M/n)-Lipschitz. Then, the algorithm simulates the
tester from Corollary 4.40 on f ′ with proximity parameter Θ(ε), and returns its result.
Note that the query complexity is as desired, so it remains to show correctness. If f is
monotone so is f ′ , in which case the algorithm accepts. Now assume that distmono  1   (f ) > ε,

     Let g ′ : [n]d → R be any monotone function. We obtain a monotone function g :

Remark 4.42. One may wonder whether the reductions above could yield more efficient
testers if combined with Hamming testers for Boolean functions on the hypergrid with
better dependence on d (via the reduction from L1 testing to Hamming testing of [BRY14a],
which is behind Theorem 4.39), since e.g. the tester of [BCS23a] has query complexity
 e 1/2+o(1) /ε2 ). However, it seems like this cannot be the case, i.e. Corollary 4.40 has the

M nd. The reason is as follows: [KMS18] showed that any nonadaptive, one-sided pair tester
for the Boolean cube with query complexity O(dα /εβ ) must satisfy α + β2 ≥ 32 , so hypergrid
testers must also satisfy this as well as β ≥ 1, assuming query complexity independent of
n. Then, given a hypergrid tester for Boolean functions with query complexity O(d    e α /εβ ),

4.7      Discussion and open questions
In this chapter, we started the systematic study of directed Poincaré inequalities over con-
tinuous and discrete domains, and showed that the connection between directed isoperime-
try and monotonicity testing extends to the analytic setting by giving an L1 monotonicity
tester for Lipschitz functions over [0, 1]d via the corresponding directed Poincaré inequality.
We also showed that the one-dimensional case of the (discrete or continuous) line enjoys
a reduction to the Hamming setting; at the same time, the case of the discrete line [n]
shows a strong separation between Hamming testing (which has query complexity p       Θ(log n))
       1
and L testing of M -Lipschitz functions (which has query complexity Θ( nM/ε)). The
                                                                            e
one-dimensional
              setting also establishes
                                            (perhaps surprisingly) that our lower bounds of
            d                     d
Ω (M/ε) d+1 and Ω (nM/ε) d+1 for the unit cube and hypergrid, respectively, cannot
be improved for general constant dimension d.
    Comparing our tester for the solid cube [0, 1]d , which makes O(dM/ε)
                                                                       √ queries,   with
                                                                                 1/2+o(1)
the situation for monotonicity testing of Boolean functions, where O( d) and d
                                                                     e
query complexity bounds are achievable for the Boolean cube and hypergrid, respectively
[KMS18; BCS23a], the most immediate question is: is there an L1 tester for monotonicity
of Lipschitz functions with o(d) query complexity? We will begin to tackle this question
in the next chapter.

                                               59
A conjectured (L1 , ℓ2 )-inequality. Classical Poincaré inequalities are usually of the ℓ2
form, which seems natural e.g. due to basis independence. On the other hand, in the
directed setting, the weaker ℓ1 inequalities (as in [GGLRS00] and this chapter) have more
straightforward proofs than ℓ2 counterparts such as the inequality of [KMS18]. A perhaps
related observation is that monotonicity is not a basis-independent concept, since it is
defined in terms of the standard basis. In light of the parallels between the continuous and
discrete settings, we conjecture that the solid unit cube enjoys a directed (L1 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré
inequality, mirroring the situation for the Boolean cube [KMS18; PRW22; CCRSW25].

Conjecture 4.43. For every Lipschitz function f : [0, 1]d → R, it holds that
                                               −
                            distmono
                                                        
                                1    (f ) ≲ E  ∥∇ f ∥ 2   .

    While we originally made this conjecture in [Fer23] in hopes of establishing an o(d)
tester for the analytic√setting, in the rest of this thesis we will actually obtain such a
tester—indeed, an O(e d) tester—from an (L2 , ℓ2 )-inequality instead. Such an inequality
enjoys the more standard ℓ2 geometry as described above, and appears to be easier to
obtain compared to Conjecture 4.43 because L2 spaces enjoy more useful structure (for
example, they are Hilbert spaces) than L1 spaces. Nevertheless, we believe that a proof or
disproof of Conjecture 4.43 will further illuminate the nature of directed isoperimetry in
the analytic setting, and its connection to monotonicity.

   We make two remarks about this result.
    First, as observed in [BRY14a], for 1 ≤ p ≤ q and any (say) Lipschitz f and g,
Jensen’s inequality gives ∥f − g∥Lp ([0,1]d ) ≤ ∥f − g∥Lq ([0,1]d ) . Thus any Lq monotonicity

holds for Lp testing with p ∈ [1, 2]. At the same time, as remarked in Chapter 2, if

                                               61
f is M -Lipschitz (with respect to the standard ℓ2 geometry, which is the assumption in
Theorem 5.2), then f is also (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz. Thus the testers from both Theorem 4.3
from the previous chapter and Theorem 5.2 above are L1 monotonicity testers for M -
Lipschitz functions; and in this setting, ignoring logarithmic factors, Theorem 5.2 is better
than Theorem 4.3 when Mε > √1d and vice-versa. Curiously, something analogous is true
                                                                                      √
of the testers of [KMS18] and [GGLRS00] for the Boolean setting, which make O(      e d/ε2 )
and O(d/ε) queries respectively.
                  √
    Second, the d dependence in the query complexity is optimal for derivative-pair
testers, which are randomized nonadaptive testers that, at each step, either sample points
x ⪯ y and use value queries to reject if f (x) > f (y), or sample point x and direction v ⪰ 0
and use a directional derivative query to reject if v · ∇f (x) < 0. The proof is not difficult,
and is given in Section 5.2.


Idea of the algorithm. Our tester may be seen as the natural continuous analogue of
the path tester of [KMS18] for the Boolean cube, which samples points x ⪯ y ∈ {0, 1}d
connected by a path of length 2k , for k sampled uniformly from [log d], and rejects if
f (x) > f (y). In our case, a path is replaced by a direction v ∈ {0, 1}d , and the condition
f (x) > f (y) is replaced by the condition v · ∇f (x) < 0, for a uniformly random point
x ∈ [0, 1]d , via a directional derivative query. Such a tester accepts any monotone function,
so the challenge is to ensure that it detects the case when distmono
                                                                   2   (f ) ≥ ε.
   In this case, hiding constant factors, Theorem 5.1 gives that E [∥∇− f ∥22 ] ≳ ε2 . We
                                                                                  ∥∇− f (x)∥2
would like the distribution of v to be such that, for any x, P [v · ∇f (x) < 0] ≳ √d∥∇f (x)∥22 .

Definition 5.3 (Distribution of direction vector). For each p ∈ [0, 1], we define distribution

                                                          67
On simulating directional derivative queries using value queries. We remark
that the directional derivative queries, while convenient for the analysis and in our opinion
conceptually clean, can be replaced with value queries under reasonable smoothness as-
sumptions. First, in Remark 5.6 we observed that the rejection condition v·∇f (x) < 0 may
in fact be replaced with the more robust condition v · ∇f (x) < −Θ(ε/d). Now, suppose the
input function f is promised to be twice-differentiable with second derivatives bounded by
any constant β. We may then replace any directional derivative query v · ∇f (x) with the
value queries f (x) and f (y), where y = x + αv for sufficiently small α > 0 as a function of
β, ε and d, and reject if f (y) < f (x). This is possible because, if v · ∇f (x) < −Θ(ε/d) and
for such small α, the directional derivative v · ∇f (z) remains negative on all points z in
the line segment between x and y, and hence f (y) < f (x). Conceptually, we view this as
confirmation that directional derivative queries are not unreasonably powerful, but rather
a useful abstraction for the analytic setting.


5.2      Lower bound
Definition 5.7. A derivative-pair tester for monotonicity of Lipschitz functions f :
[0, 1]d → R is described by a distribution D over pair tests and directional derivative
tests, where

  1. a pair test is an operation that performs a pair of value queries f (x), f (y) for x ⪯ y,
     and rejects if and only if f (x) > f (y); and
  2. a directional derivative test is an operation that performs a directional derivative
     query at point x and direction v ⪰ 0, and rejects if and only if ∇f · v < 0.

The tester independently samples m tests from D, rejects if any of these tests rejects,
and accepts otherwise. By definition, every derivative-pair tester is nonadaptive and has
one-sided error.

                                                  70
Interlude




    71
Chapter 6

Directed isoperimetry on graphs via
the directed heat flow

In this chapter, we start working towards a proof of a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality
for the solid cube [0, 1]d (Theorem 5.1) by first showing such an inequality in the discrete
setting of functions f : {0, 1}d → R over the Boolean cube. By taking this intermediate
step, we untangle two aspects of our eventual proof of Theorem 5.1:

  1. A dynamical argument: instead of reasoning about distmono2   (f ) as a “static” com-
     parison between the object f and some monotone function f ∗ , we instead inspect a
     time-continuous process which smoothly takes initial state f to equilibrium state f ∗ .
     This idea can already be implemented in the case of functions f : {0, 1}d → R.
  2. The challenges of continuous space: when we implement this idea in the analytic
     setting of functions f : [0, 1]d → R, we will need to handle many complications of
     real- and functional-analytic flavour, such as ensuring regularity and integrability of
     solutions to partial differential equations. In contrast, the discrete study relies on
     the more well-behaved theory of finite-dimensional vector spaces, and thus makes for
     a more appropriate starting point.

    It turns out that the most natural way to show an (L2 , ℓ2 ) inequality for the Boolean
cube is to study the more general setting of directed graphs instead. In that setting, classical
Poincaré inequalities are intimately connected to the field of spectral graph theory, and our
argument will proceed by revisiting and extending a spectral theory for directed graphs
initiated by [Yos16] in the context of network analysis, via a dynamical system framework
introduced by work on the Paulsen problem from operator theory [KLLR18].

                                              72
   First, let us briefly recall some elements of spectral graph theory and their connection
to Poincaré inequalities. While our proof is mostly self-contained, we refer the reader to
[Han14; LP17; Lau25] for a thorough introduction to these ideas.
    Let G = (V, E) be an unweighted, undirected graph, with V = [n]. One of the central
objects in spectral graph theory is the Laplacian operator of G, which is the n × n matrix
L given as follows. Let A be the adjacency matrix of G, and let D be the diagonal matrix
such that Di is the degree of vertex i for each 1 ≤ i ≤ n. Then the Laplacian operator
is L := D − A. This matrix is symmetric and real-valued, and therefore enjoys a spectral
decomposition; we write λ1 ≤ λ2 ≤ · · · ≤ λn for its eigenvalues.
    Spectral graph theory shows that the spectrum of L is remarkably effective in capturing
the connectivity properties of G, roughly speaking. For example, the smallest eigenvalue
λ1 is always equal to zero, and the second eigenvalue λ2 is nonzero if and only if G is
connected. Graph partitioning algorithms work by putting the vertices of G into clusters
according to an ordering defined by the eigenvector corresponding to the second eigenvalue
λ2 . This eigenvalue is also related to properties of G such as vertex and edge expansion,
which are combinatorial measures of connectivity of G, via so-called Cheeger inequalities.
Graphs with large λ2 are expander graphs, which are graphs that behave in many ways like
random graphs and have far-reaching connections in theoretical computer science, spanning
areas such as error-correcting codes, pseudorandomness, probabilistically checkable proofs,
and Markov chain theory. Moreover, the action Lf of the Laplacian operator also induces
a dynamical process called the heat flow, which will be a crucial element of our proof.
   The relevance of the Laplacian operator to our goals is revealed by the quadratic form
on L: for each f ∈ Rn , we have
                                       X
                              f ⊤ Lf =    (f (x) − f (y))2 .                         (6.1)
                                          xy∈E

Note that the right-hand side above, which sums up the variation of f along each edge of G,
is an unnormalized version of the right-hand side E [∥∇f ∥22 ] of a classical (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré
inequality when G is the graph of the hypercube, with an edge between x, y ∈ {0, 1}d if
and only if ∥x − y∥1 = 1. Indeed, the Laplacian operator is intimately connected to the
Poincaré inequality on G, and this connection provides the starting point for our proof in
the next section.
    The rest of this chapter is organized as follows. In Section 6.1, we study a spectral
theory for directed graphs and prove a general directed Poincaré inequality for any weighted
directed graph G, mediated by a quantity called the dynamical spectral gap of G. In
Section 6.2, we compute the dynamical spectral gap of the hypercube graph Hn , which we

                                                 73
use to derive a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality for functions f : {0, 1}d → R. Finally,
in Section 6.3 we show a family of reverse Poincaré inequalities, showing in particular that
the directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality is tight up to a factor of d, and thus elucidating
the quality of the local-to-global relationship embodied by the directed Poincaré inequality.


6.1      Spectral theory and heat flow for directed graphs
We start by summarizing existing work on spectral theory for directed graphs, and pre-
viewing how we will employ and extend this theory to give a dynamical proof of a directed
Poincaré inequality for any weighted directed graph G.


Prior work on spectral theory for directed graphs. There has been extensive prior
work developing spectral graph theory beyond the classical setting of undirected graphs,
toward capturing directed graphs and hypergraphs. Early work of [Fil91; Chu05] associated
a certain Hermitian matrix with each directed graph, and showed a Cheeger-type inequality
based on the eigenvalues of that matrix, and many subsequent works have built upon that
foundation; we refer to the recent thesis [Tun25] for a thorough review, and here we mention
two recent lines of work that are closest to our setting. One line of works [LTW23; LTW24;
Tun25] has developed a theory of reweighted eigenvalues capturing expansion properties
of directed graphs and hypergraphs, proved Cheeger inequalities for these settings, and
devised efficient algorithms for graph partitioning. Another line of works [Yos16; Yos19;
FSY21; IMTY22] has pursued similar goals by analyzing a nonlinear Laplacian operator
and the heat equation associated with it.
    While our interest in a spectral theory for directed graphs is related to these previous
works (and indeed we will build upon the approach of [Yos16]), our focus is slightly different.
In a nutshell, while prior works have focused on spectral characterizations of good expansion
of a directed graph G as captured by directed versions of Cheeger inequalities (for edge
conductance or vertex expansion) and mixing time of random walks, our focus will be on
the quality of G as the “substrate” for a dynamical process; we will consider G a good
directed spectral expander if it affords fast convergence for that process. In particular, our
perspective allows for directed acyclic graphs to be considered good expanders, which is a
stark departure from prior perspectives – as we briefly explain next.
   Indeed, a central focus of prior works has been to establish Cheeger inequalities of the
type                                             q
                                    ⃗λ2 ≲ ϕ(G)
                                          ⃗    ≲ ⃗λ2 ,                                (6.2)

                                              74
where ⃗λ2 denotes a relevant second eigenvalue related to the directed weighted graph G,
                           ⃗
and the edge conductance ϕ(G)   of G is
                                                  +           +
                        ⃗             min {w(δ (S)), w(δ (V \ S))}
                        ϕ(G) := min                                 ,
                               ∅̸=S⊊V  min {volw (S), volw (V \ S)}

where δ + (S) denotes the outgoing edge boundary of S and volw (S) denotes the total
weighted degree of all vertices in S. Now, if G is not strongly connected, then in general
there exists a set S with positive volume but no outgoing edges, which makes ϕ(G)    ⃗     and
     ⃗
thus λ2 zero. In particular, this is the case for the directed hypercube graph which we are
interested in, so if we hope to show a non-trivial directed Poincaré inequality via a spectral
argument, such a quantity ⃗λ2 will not do.


Our approach. The type of spectral theory for directed graphs we study in this section
was first developed by [Yos16] in the context of network analysis. In that work, [Yos16]
defined a nonlinear Laplacian operator acting on real-valued functions defined on the ver-
tices of a directed graph, showed that this operator induces a dynamical process that is a
directed analogue of the classical heat flow on graphs, proved that this operator has non-
trivial eigenvalues, and established a Cheeger inequality like (6.2) for this setting. While
[Yos16] focused on the implications of directed spectral theory for graph partitioning and
related problems in network analysis, we focus on the dynamical properties of the heat
flow on directed graphs – namely its convergence to a monotone limit, and its connection
to the directed Poincaré inequality.
    Let us briefly motivate the main ideas in our argument by elaborating on the relevance
of spectral graph theory to Poincaré inequalities, as previewed in the introduction to this
chapter. Given a graph G = (V, E), the following four concepts play a central role:

   1. The Laplacian operator1 L , which acts on function f by outputting another function
      L f.

   2. The Dirichlet energy functional E, which associates with each f an energy E(f ) ≥ 0
      measuring the “local variance” of f along edges of G. (This is essentially the quadratic
      form shown in (6.1), appropriately normalized by 1/|V | and possibly the edge weights
      of G.)
  1
     We now write L as opposed to L because, going forward, we will see L as an operator acting on a
normalized inner product space, as opposed to a matrix acting on unnormalized Euclidean space. This
will be made formal in Section 6.1.1.


                                                75
  3. The heat flow semigroup Pet , which captures a dynamical process which starts at some
                                                                           d e
     initial state f and has its rate of change governed by the Laplacian: dt Pt f = L Pet f .
     The heat flow informally “sends mass” along each edge of G from the vertex with
     higher f -value to the vertex with lower f -value, causing the system to converge to
     an equilibrium state.

  4. The Poincaré inequality, which states that Var [f ] ≤ λ1 E(f ). The best constant λ is
     called the spectral gap of G.

    The appearance of the Poincaré inequality above hints at the relevance of this theory
to our goal of proving a directed Poincaré inequality, and we mentioned above that our
strategy toward this goal will be to define a directed version of the spectral gap. As a
motivation for this strategy, we observe that the spectral gap ties together essentially all
of the elements of the list above; indeed, as summarized in [Han14, Theorem 2.18], the
following are equivalent given a constant c ≥ 0:

  1. Poincaré inequality: Var [f ] ≤ cE(f ) for all f .
                           h    i
  2. Variance decay: Var Pt f ≤ e−2t/c Var [f ] for all f, t.
                             e

  3. Energy decay: E(Pet f ) ≤ e−2t/c E(f ) for all f, t.

    To prove a directed Poincaré inequality, we replace the Laplacian operator L with
an operator L − which, intuitively, only “sends mass” from vertex u to vertex v if (u, v)
is a directed edge and f (u) > f (v), i.e. f violates monotonicity along edge (u, v); the
dynamical system induced by L − is the directed heat flow on G, which was studied by
[Yos16]. The directed heat flow is precisely the gradient system for the directed energy
functional E − (f ), which is the directed version of E(f ) that only counts those edges along
which f violates monotonicity. Thus, this system intuitively “corrects” the local violations
of monotonicity as quickly as possible, and indeed it converges to a monotone function as
t → ∞.
    This directed theory cannot fully analogize the classical situation above; for example,
variance decay fails to hold, because non-constant monotone functions are (non-unique!)
stationary solutions. Instead, we will define the dynamical spectral gap of G as the best
constant characterizing the (directed) energy decay, as in Item 3 above, and then show
that (the directed version of) Item 3 implies (the directed version of) Item 1.



                                               76
Organization. The rest of this section is organized as follows. Sections 6.1.1 to 6.1.3
present the directed versions of the Laplacian operator, the Dirichlet energy functional,
and the heat flow, respectively. These subsections are largely an alternative exposition of
the ideas covered in [Yos16], but with a different emphasis tailored to our goals2. Then,
in Section 6.1.4 we define the dynamical spectral gap of a directed weighted graph, and
show via an energy decay argument that every directed weighted graph admits a directed
Poincaré inequality mediated by the dynamical spectral gap.


Notation. Given a discrete set V , we denote by L2 (V ) the Hilbert space obtained by
endowing the set of V → R functions with the inner product ⟨f, g⟩ := E [f (x)g(x)],
                                                                                        x∈V

                                 p respect to
                                            pthe uniform distribution over V . This inner
product induces the norm ∥f ∥ = ⟨f, f ⟩ = E [f 2 ].
    For each u ∈ V , we write eu ∈ L2 (V ) for the standard basis vector given by eu (v) :=
1 [u = v].


6.1.1      The directed Laplacian
Let G = (V, w) be a directed weighted graph, where w : V × V → [0, +∞) is function
specifying the weights of edges in G. By convention, we say that (u, v) ∈ V × V is an edge
in G when w(u, v) > 0. We say G is undirected if w is a symmetric function.

Definition 6.1 (Directed Laplacian [Yos16]). The directed Laplacian operator of G is the
operator L − = LG− : L2 (V ) → L2 (V ) given by
                                   1 X
                        L − f :=           w(u, v) (f (u) − f (v))+ (ev − eu )                     (6.3)
                                   2 u,v∈V

for each f ∈ L2 (V ).

    Given f ∈ L2 (V ), we say an edge (u, v) is f -monotone if f (u) ≤ f (v), and we say
it is f -antimonotone if f (u) > f (v). We say f is monotone if every edge (u, v) of G is
f -monotone. If we think of f as the distribution of “mass” over the vertices V and of L − f
as the rate of change of f = f (t) over time t, then Definition 6.1 posits that mass flows
along the f -antimonotone edges, from the heavier vertex to the lighter one. When G is
   2
    In particular, [Yos16] defined both normalized and unnormalized versions of their Laplacian operator,
and focused on the normalized one. We study a single definition for weighted graphs.


                                                   77
undirected, this process is the standard heat flow on G, and indeed Definition 6.1 recovers
the standard graph Laplacian in this case:

Observation 6.2. If G is undirected, then LG− is (half of ) the standard (unnormalized)
Laplacian operator LG of G. Indeed, we can see the action of LG− on f ∈ L2 (V ) as
follows: 1) remove the f -monotone edges (u, v) from G; 2) view the resulting graph G′ as
undirected; and 3) apply the standard Laplacian operator LG′ to f .

Remark 6.3. In spectral graph theory one typically defines the Laplacian operator of
an undirected graph as −LG in our notation, i.e. by replacing ev − eu with eu − ev in
Definition 6.1 (which yields the matrix L from the introduction to this chapter). Our
notation follows instead the tradition from probability theory (see e.g. [BGL14; Han14]),
which has the advantages 1) that L − itself, rather than −L − , will be the generator of
the heat semigroup – our main object of interest; and 2) of consistency with the analytic
setting, where we have the Laplacian operator ∆ for smooth functions in Euclidean space.

   We note that unlike the standard Laplacian operator, the operator L − is nonlinear and
not self-adjoint in general. Instead, one may think of L − as a “piecewise linear” operator
on L2 (V ), which is in particular Lipschitz continuous.

Lemma 6.4. The operator L − : L2 (V ) → L2 (V ) is Lipschitz continuous.

Proof. Since all norms are equivalent in a finite-dimensional space, it suffices to let
f, g ∈ L2 (V ) differ on a single point z ∈ V and show that, for all u ∈ V ,
|(L − f )(u) − (L − g)(u)| ≤ M |f (z) − g(z)| for some constant M > 0, which may depend
on the graph G but not on f or g.
   By Definition 6.1, we have

6.1.2    The energy functional
As in the case of the standard Laplacian operator, the directed Laplacian naturally in-
duces an energy functional (or Dirichlet form). The following definition corresponds to the
Rayleigh quotient defined in [Yos16].

Definition 6.5 (Energy functional). The directed Dirichlet energy functional E − :
L2 (V ) → R is given by
                             E − (f ) := − f, L − f .

   The directed Dirichlet energy measures the local violations of monotonicity along edges
of G, and it is indeed always non-negative, as shown in the following proposition (which is
similar to Lemma 4.3 of [Yos16] for the normalized nonlinear Laplacian).

Proposition 6.6 (Energy functional measures local violations). For each f ∈ L2 (V ), it

   Observe that since L − is “piecewise linear”, the energy functional E − is a “piecewise
quadratic” functional on L2 (V ). Furthermore, at each point f ∈ L2 (V ) the Laplacian L − f
points at the direction opposite to the gradient of E − . To formalize this viewpoint, we first
need to introduce some standard definitions to clarify what we mean by the gradient of a
functional on L2 (V ), such as E − . We recall the following standard definition.

Definition 6.7 (Fréchet derivative and gradient). We say that F : L2 (V ) → R is Fréchet
differentiable at f ∈ L2 (V ) if there exists a bounded linear operator A : L2 (V ) → R such
that
                                     |F (f + h) − F (f ) − Ah|
                               lim                             = 0.
                             ∥h∥2 →0           ∥h∥2
In this case, we call DF (f ) := A the Fréchet derivative of F at f . Moreover, by the Riesz
representation theorem there exists a unique vector v ∈ L2 (V ) satisfying

                                             Ah = ⟨h, v⟩                                           (6.4)

for all h ∈ L2 (V ). We call ∇F (f ) := v the gradient of F at f . Finally, we say that F is
(Fréchet) C 1 if it is Fréchet differentiable at every f ∈ L2 (V ) and the map f 7→ DF (f )
(from L2 (V ) to the space of bounded linear operators L2 (V ) → R) is continuous.

   The following proposition follows from standard real analysis.


                                                   80
Proposition 6.8. Suppose F : L2 (V ) → R has continuous partial derivatives with respect
to the standard basis {eu }u∈V , that is, for each u ∈ V the L2 (V ) → R function
                                                     F (f + teu ) − F (f )
                                   ∂u F (f ) = lim
                                              t→0             t
is continuous. Then F is (Fréchet) C 1 and its gradient ∇F : L2 (V ) → L2 (V ) is given by

                                      (∇F (f ))(u) = |V | · ∂u F (f )

for each f ∈ L2 (V ) and u ∈ V .

   We highlight that since the inner product on L2 (V ) used in (6.4) differs from the
standard inner product on the finite dimensional Euclidean space RV by a factor of |V |,
the gradient of F also differs from the partial derivatives of F by a factor of |V |.
  We are now equipped to show that E − is a convex C 1 functional, and that the Laplacian
L is (half of) the negative gradient of E − .
  −

6.1.3     Directed heat flow
As previewed in the Section 6.1.1, the directed Laplacian operator can be thought of as
the rate of mass transfer along f -antimonotone edges of G in a dynamical process. Let us
make this notion precise.
   Given any f ∈ L2 (V ), we define the directed heat flow on G with initial state f as the
dynamical system given by the initial value problem (IVP)

                          f ′ (t) = L − f (t) for all t ≥ 0 ,   f (0) = f .                 (6.6)
Since the operator L − is Lipschitz by Lemma 6.4, a standard existence and uniqueness
theorem for ordinary differential equations (ODEs) implies that this IVP has a unique
solution f : [0, +∞) → L2 (V ); see e.g. [Tes12, Corollary 2.6]. This can also be shown
using the theory of maximal monotone operators, as done by [IMTY22] for the heat flow

                                                  82
on hypergraphs, and by [Yos19] in a study that generalizes both the directed graph setting
of [Yos16] and the hypergraph setting of [IMTY22].
   Moreover, the directed heat flow enjoys the following semigroup structure. Define the
operator family (Pt )t≥0 , with Pt : L2 (V ) → L2 (V ) for each t ≥ 0, as follows: for each
f ∈ L2 (V ), let f : [0, +∞) → L2 (V ) be the solution to the IVP (6.6), and let

                                         Pt f := f (t) .

Then it immediately follows that Pt satisfies the properties of a semigroup, namely

  1. P0 is the identity operator.

  2. Ps Pt = Ps+t for all s, t ≥ 0.

  3. limt→0 Pt f = f for all f ∈ L2 (V ) (this follows from the differentiability of the solution
     f (t)).

We call Pt the directed heat semigroup operator. Note that Pt is a nonlinear operator.

Observation 6.11 (Monotone functions are stationary solutions). If f ∈ L2 (V ) is mono-
tone, then L − f = 0 and hence Pt f = f for all t ≥ 0.

     Since monotone functions are stationary solutions to the directed heat flow (Observa-
tion 6.11), while any non-monotone f has non-zero Laplacian L − f (see Proposition 6.6),
it is natural to expect that Pt f always converges to a monotone function as t → ∞. This is
indeed the case, because the directed heat flow is a gradient system for the convex energy
functional E − .

Proposition 6.12 (Directed heat flow is gradient system). For all f ∈ L2 (V ) and t ≥ 0,
we have
                              d           1
                                 Pt f = − ∇E − (Pt f ) .
                              dt          2
                                       d
Proof. By the definition of Pt we have dt Pt f = L − Pt f . The claim then follows from
Lemma 6.10.

Corollary 6.13 (Convergence to monotone equilibrium). For every f ∈ L2 (V ), there
exists a (unique) monotone f ∗ ∈ L2 (V ) such that Pt f → f ∗ as t → ∞.


                                               83
Proof. It is a standard fact that since E − is a convex differentiable function which attains
its minimum, its gradient system (which is given by Pt ) converges to a minimizer f ∗ ; see
e.g. [AC84, Chapter 3, Theorem 2]. By Proposition 6.6, such a minimizer is monotone.

   In light of Corollary 6.13, we may define the following limit operator.

Definition 6.14 (Monotone equilibrium). We define the operator P∞ : L2 (V ) → L2 (V )
by P∞ f := limt→∞ Pt f for each f ∈ L2 (V ), and call P∞ f the monotone equilibrium of f .

Remark 6.15. Since P∞ f is monotone, it is constant in each strongly connected compo-
nent of G.


6.1.4    Dynamical spectral gap
In this subsection, we associate with the directed Laplacian operator L − = LG− a quantity
λ− = λ− (G), the dynamical spectral gap of G, as a natural directed generalization of the
classical (undirected) case. In particular, λ− (G) characterizes the rate of energy decay in
the directed heat flow as Pt f converges to its monotone equilibrium, and this also implies a
directed Poincaré inequality linking the distance between the initial and equilibrium states
to the energy of the initial state (i.e. its violations of monotonicity).
   In the classical theory, the spectral gap λ(G) associated with the classical Laplacian
operator LG is
                                                      − ⟨f, LG f ⟩
                              λ(G) =        inf                    ,                (6.7)
                                      f ̸=⃗0: E[f ]=0    ∥f ∥22
which is the best constant for which the Poincaré inequality
                                                   1
                                    Var [f ] ≤        E(f )
                                                 λ(G)

holds, where the classical Dirichlet energy is E(f ) = − ⟨f, LG f ⟩. As mentioned at the
beginning of this section, the spectral gap also characterizes the rate of exponential decay
of the variance toward its minimum value under the heat flow. That is, λ(G) is the best
constant for which
                              d     h    i               h    i
                                 Var Pt f ≤ −2λ(G)Var Pt f
                                      e                    e                            (6.8)
                              dt
holds for all f ∈ L2 (V ) and t ≥ 0, where Pet is the undirected heat flow. It also similarly
characterizes the exponential decay of the Dirichlet energy, i.e. λ(G) is the best constant

                                             84
for which
                                d e
                                   E(Pt f ) ≤ −2λ(G)E(Pet f )                     (6.9)
                                dt
holds for all f ∈ L2 (V ) and t ≥ 0. We refer the reader to [Han14] for a comprehensive
reference.
    We wish to define a natural notion of spectral gap for the directed Laplacian L − ,
towards the goal of obtaining a directed Poincaré inequality. One attempt is to take the
directed analogue of the ratio in (6.7), namely
                                       E − (f )   − ⟨f, L − f ⟩
                                                =               .                                (6.10)
                                        ∥f ∥22       ∥f ∥22
(This would essentially be the unnormalized version of the spectral gap defined by [Yos16]
for the normalized nonlinear Laplacian.) However, this quantity faces the issue that any
monotone function f has E − (f ) = 0, which makes the ratio zero3. Even if we restricted
our attention to non-monotone f , in general ∥f ∥22 could still be made arbitrarily large
while keeping E − (f ) > 0 bounded, by putting large values of f (u) that do not introduce
violations of monotonicity. Therefore (6.10) does not seem like an informative quantity for
our purposes.
    Another way to see the conceptual issue is that, unlike in the classical case, in the
directed setting we cannot hope to have variance decay as in (6.8), since the monotone
equilibrium P∞ f may have strictly positive variance. However, as our next attempt will
reveal, we can establish the decay of Dirichlet energy as in (6.9), which will suffice to give
a directed Poincaré inequality for G.
       We start by observing that, in the classical case, the ratio
                                                     ∥L f ∥22
                                               inf                                               (6.11)
                                           f :E(f )>0 E(f )

equals the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of the classical Laplacian −LG , so for a connected
graph it also equals the spectral gap λ(G). Now, its directed analogue
                                                      ∥L − f ∥22
                                              inf                                                (6.12)
                                         f :E − (f )>0 E − (f )

seems like a reasonable candidate – intuitively, note that not only does the constraint
E − (f ) > 0 rule out monotone functions f , but also, f -monotone edges (u, v) with large
   3
    While for strongly connected directed graphs only constant functions are monotone, which renders the
objection void, this is not the case for general directed graphs.


                                                   85
f (v) − f (u) contribute nothing to the denominator, suggesting that (6.12) does not have
the same shortcoming as (6.10)4. Another way to see the intuition behind (6.12) is that,
while its classical counterpart (6.11) only differs from (6.7) by ignoring the zero eigenvalues
coming from the single pathological case of disconnected graphs, the directed (6.12) ignores
many more uninformative situations and captures the connectivity of G in the appropriate,
directed sense.
       Therefore, we define

Definition 6.16. The dynamical spectral gap of G is the quantity λ− (G) ∈ [0, +∞] given
by
                                ∥L − f ∥22
                                                                 
                  −                              2       −

                                 E − (f )

       The next proposition shows that every graph has a non-zero dynamical spectral gap.

Proposition 6.17. The dynamical spectral gap λ− (G) is strictly positive, and it is a real
number unless G consists of isolated vertices only.

Proof. If G contains at least one edge e = (u, v), then any function f ∈ L2 (V ) with
f (u) = 1 and f (v) = 0 has E − (f ) > 0, so λ− (G) < +∞.
   We now show that λ− (G) > 0. The idea is that, for each function f ∈ L2 (V ), the action
of L − on f is the same as the action of the standard Laplacian on a subgraph of G (made
undirected) that preserves only the f -antimonotone edges (as noted in Observation 6.2).
The number of such subgraphs is at most |V |!, i.e. the number of permutations induced
by f , so λ− (G) is lower bounded by the minimum non-zero eigenvalue out of finitely many
graphs.
   Formally, let n := |V |, fix any f ∈ L2 (V ) satisfying E − (f ) > 0, and let π : V → [n] be
a bijection such that fπ−1 (1) ≤ fπ−1 (2) ≤ · · · ≤ fπ−1 (n) . Let Gπ = (V, wπ ) be the undirected,
weighted graph defined as follows: for each u, v ∈ V ,

                                              w(v, u) otherwise.

with fi (u) = 0, fi (v) = i, f (u′ ) = 1, and f (v ′ ) = 0. Then E − (fi )/∥fi ∥22 → 0 as i → ∞, while
∥L − fi ∥22 /E − (fi ) remains constant.

Lemma 6.24. For any a, b, c, d ∈ R, we have

                  (a − b)+ − (c − d)+ (a − c)+ − (b − d)+ ≥ 0 .
                                                      


Proof. Let X := (a − b)+ − (c − d)+ and Y := (a − c)+ − (b − d)+ , so that our goal is to
show that XY ≥ 0, or equivalently that X > 0 =⇒ Y ≥ 0 and Y > 0 =⇒ X ≥ 0. By
switching the roles of b and c, it suffices to prove the first implication, i.e. that X > 0 =⇒
Y ≥ 0.
   Suppose X > 0. Then a − b > 0, since otherwise we would have (a − b)+ = 0 and hence
X ≤ 0. Therefore a − b = (a − b)+ > (c − d)+ ≥ c − d. Moreover, if (b − d)+ = 0 then
Y ≥ 0 and we are done, so we may assume that b − d = (b − d)+ > 0. We conclude that

                 Y = (a − c)+ − (b − d)+ ≥ (a − c) − (b − d) = (a − b) − (c − d) ≥ 0 ,

where the first inequality holds since (a − c)+ ≥ a − c while (b − d)+ = b − d, and the
second inequality holds since a − b ≥ c − d.
