Part II: Continuous setting via PDEs
        and optimal transport




                 96
Chapter 7

An L2 directed Poincaré inequality
via the directed heat equation

The main result of this chapter is a directed (L2 , ℓ2 )-Poincaré inequality for sufficiently reg-
ular functions f : [0, 1]d → R (in particular, Lipschitz continuity suffices; see Section 7.2.1).

Theorem 7.1 (Directed Poincaré inequality). There exists a universal constant C > 0
such that, for all f ∈ H 1 ((0, 1)d ),
                                 distmono (f )2 ≤ C E ∥∇− f ∥22 .
                                                              
                                       2                                        (7.1)

    As in the previous chapter, we will prove Theorem 7.1 via a dynamical argument. The
two areas of mathematics underpinning our proof are partial differential equations [Eva10;
Bre11] and the theory of optimal transport [Vil09; San15]. Our guiding vision is to identify
a robust approach to the classical Poincaré inequality such that, by “toggling” a single
aspect of the approach, we can transform a proof of the classical statement into a proof
of its directed counterpart. It turns out that the heat equation is the crucial ingredient to
implement this vision, as we now outline.


7.1      Proof ideas
7.1.1     Starting point: the heat equation.
The first step is to identify the right classical starting point. For example, the Poincaré
inequality can be proved using Fourier analysis, but since the directed problem is highly

                                                97
nonlinear (due to the ∇− operator), this approach does not seem suitable. Instead, we
take a physically-motivated approach. Let u = u(t, x) where we think of the first variable
as time, with each f = u(t) = u(t, ·) a function over space. A remarkable property (see
e.g. [BGL14, Chapter 4]) of the Poincaré inequality

                                 Var [f ] ≤ C E ∥∇f ∥22
                                                       

(where we have written Var [f ] in the place of distconst
                                                    2     (f )2 , which is the same thing) is that
it is equivalent to exponential decay of variance in the heat equation

                                           ∂t u = ∆u ,                                      (7.2)

where ∆ denotes the Laplacian operator, given appropriate “no-flux” boundary conditions
which we do not expand Ron at the moment.
                                       R       To see this equivalence, assume for simplicity
the mean zero condition u(0) dx = u(0, x) dx = 0 (which is then preserved over time),
compute ∂t Var [u(t)], differentiate under the integral, apply (7.2) and integrate by parts to
obtain
                  Z               Z                   Z                      Z
                        2
∂t Var [u(t)] = ∂t u(t) dx = 2 u(t)∂t u(t) dx = 2 u(t)∆u(t) dx = −2 ∇u(t)·∇u(t) dx .

This means that ∂t Var [u(t)] ≤ −C Var [u(t)] if and only if Var [u(t)] ≤ C2 ∥∇u(t)∥22 dx,
                                                                              R

i.e. exponential decay of the variance is equivalent to the Poincaré inequality.
    We observe that solutions to the heat equation converge to a constant equilibrium
(taking the average value of u), and the associated Poincaré inequality is a bound on the
distance to a constant function. Accordingly, it seems intuitive that if we replace (7.2)
with a PDE that converges to a monotone function, we might learn something about the
distance to a monotone function instead.


7.1.2     The directed heat equation
The preceding observations suggest that we should study directed analogues of (7.2). How-
ever, in the multidimensional setting, the nonlinearities that seem inherent to any such
directed analogue prove challenging to analyze. Hence, let us focus on the one-dimensional
case for now. In one dimension, the heat equation is

                                         ∂t u = ∂x ∂x u .

The surface reading of this PDE is that, if we focus on the value of u at a single point x,
the PDE tells us how this value changes over time. Another useful perspective is to think

                                               98
of u(t, x) as the density of “particles” at each point, and ask about how these particles are
“moving” over time; this idea can be made formal via the so-called continuity equation.
Under this view, it turns out that the inner expression ∂x u represents (up to a sign change)
the flux (or momentum) field, i.e. the total rate of particle movement at each point, and
by taking the derivative of this field, i.e. ∂x ∂x u, we determine whether, on the balance,
there are more “incoming” or “outgoing” particles—quantitatively, the rate of change ∂t u.
    With this perspective, a natural candidate PDE—which we call the directed heat equa-
tion—is
                                       ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u ,                            (7.3)
where ∂x− u := min{0, ∂x u}. The idea is that the new flux ∂x− u is always nonpositive,
which, up to a required sign change, means that particles are only allowed to “move to the
right”. Observe also that the RHS of (7.3) is zero for any monotone function, so monotone
functions are stationary solutions, while for decreasing functions, this PDE behaves exactly
as the heat equation. Thus, intuitively, this PDE seems to take a function u(0) and move
it toward a monotone limit over time.
    We explained above that the Poincaré inequality is intimately connected to convergence
of the heat equation, and we would like to show a similar property in the directed case.
One option would be to study the rate of decay of the distance to monotonicity, distmono   2 .
Although this is possible, it does not seem to lead to a proof strategy; also note that, while
in the case of the variance we also have tools such as Fourier analysis to reason about
the rate of decay directly, here we only have the PDE to work with. Fortunately, there is
another relevant quantity which decays exponentially under the heat equation, namely the
Dirichlet energy                              Z
                                            1
                                    E(f ) =     (∂x f )2 dx .
                                            2
Indeed, another formal computation shows that by differentiating under the integral,
switching the partial derivatives, applying (7.2) and integrating by parts, we get
                     Z                 Z                          Z
                 1              2
    ∂t E(u(t)) = ∂t (∂x u(t)) dx = (∂x u(t))(∂t ∂x u(t)) dx = (∂x u(t))(∂x ∂t u(t)) dx
                 2
                 Z                               Z                        Z
                                                              2         1
               = (∂x u(t))(∂x ∂x ∂x u(t)) dx = − (∂x ∂x u(t)) dx ≤ −         (∂x u(t))2 dx
                                                                       C
                   2
               = − E(u(t)) ,
                   C
where the inequality is an application of a version of the Poincaré inequality for functions
that are zero on the boundary, which will be the case for ∂x u(t) by the no-flux boundary
conditions.

                                             99
   Now, it seems reasonable to propose the following directed analogue of the Dirichlet
energy:                                   Z
                                −       1
                               E (f ) =     (∂x− f )2 dx .
                                        2
If E measures the “locally non-constant” activity of a function, E − measures the “locally
non-monotone” activity. Using E − to recast the directed heat equation in the language of
gradient flows and maximal monotone operators [Bre73], we can show that

   1. this PDE has a solution (Proposition 7.20 and Corollary 7.23);

   2. the directed Dirichlet energy decays exponentially in time (Proposition 7.25);

   3. the solution converges to a monotone equilibrium as t → ∞ (Proposition 7.68); and

   4. the solution, including up to the limit above, satisfies several other essential ana-
      lytic properties, such as nonexpansiveness (Proposition 7.72) and order preservation
      (Corollary 7.71).


7.1.3      Transport-energy inequality in one dimension
It is tempting, now, to use these results to conclude the one-dimensional directed Poincaré
inequality. The problem is that this seems to lead to a dead end, because we do not know
how to tensorize the one-dimensional inequality into a multidimensional one1.
     Instead, we keep leaning on what the PDEs naturally tell us. It turns out that there
is a different notion of distance, the Wasserstein distance, which is much more closely
connected to the theory of evolution equations and better suited to the dynamical approach
we are undertaking. Informally, the squared Wasserstein distance W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) between
probability measures ϱ0 and ϱ1 is the minimum total cost of a “transport plan” (coupling)
moving particles from ϱ0 to ϱ1 , where the cost of moving a particle from point x to point y
is |x − y|2 . The connection to PDEs is via the Benamou-Brenier formula, which informally
says that
                    Z 1                                                                           
   2                          2
W2 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) = min      ∥vt ∥L2 (ϱt ) dt : vt is a velocity field taking ϱ0 to ϱ1 from time 0 to 1 .
                         0
   1
     In the classical case, the law of total variance does allow such tensorization [BGL14, Chapter 4.3], but
this aspect of the problem does not seem to be robust to passing to the directed setting. Also note that, if
we only desired a one-dimensional directed Poincaré inequality, then we may obtain a much shorter proof
by following the argument from Chapter 4.


                                                    100
Without going into details, it follows that if we can upper bound the directed Dirichlet
energy E − (u(t)), then we can upper bound the total (weighted) magnitude of the velocity
field, i.e. ∥vt ∥2L2 (ϱt ) (recall that the momentum of particles in the directed heat equation
is essentially ∂x− u, so this connection is not arbitrary), and therefore upper bound the
Wasserstein distance between the initial state ϱ0 —which informally corresponds to the
function f = u(0)—and the final state ϱ1 —which informally corresponds to the monotone
equilibrium f ∗ = limt→∞ u(t).
    This strategy yields, at least for bounded functions, the following result. Let U be a
class of “reasonable” initial states which we do not define for now, and let the operator P∞
map each initial state to its monotone equilibrium according to the directed heat equation.
Then

Theorem 7.2 (Transport-energy inequality in one dimension). There exists a constant
C > 0 such
       R      that the following holds. Let u ∈ U be positive, bounded away from zero, and
satisfy (0,1) u dx = 1. Define the measures dµ := u dx and dµ∞ := (P∞ u) dx. Then
                                                    C −
                                 W22 (µ, µ∞ ) ≤         E (u) .
                                                  inf u

7.1.4     Tensorizing the transport-energy inequality
The advantage of making this detour through the Wasserstein distance is that, modulo
the required technical work, tensorization becomes relatively straightforward. Indeed, it
is well-known in the theory of optimal transport (c.f. [Vil09, Remark 6.6]) that the W2
distance scales well with the dimension and reflects useful geometric content. Here the
main idea is that, since the cost function for the W2 distance is the squared Euclidean
distance |x − y|2 between points x, y ∈ [0, 1]d , one way to extend the one-dimensional
result above to, say, the unit square [0, 1]2 is to

  1. apply the one-dimensional result to each row, making the row restrictions monotone
     while paying cost W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ1 ) = a2 ; and then
  2. apply the one-dimensional result to each column, making the column restrictions
     monotone while paying cost W22 (ϱ1 , ϱ2 ) = b2 .

Then, by combining the transport of particles along rows and columns into a single trans-
port plan, we obtain via the Pythagorean theorem a plan with cost a2 + b2 , so this quantity
upper bounds the overall squared distance W22 (ϱ0 , ϱ2 ). The same idea extends to higher
dimensions, and we prove:

                                             101
Lemma 7.3 (Pythagorean composition of transport plans). Let I, J ⊆ [d] be nonempty
disjoint sets. Let µ, ϱ, ν ∈ P (Rd ) be supported in bounded sets, and let γ + ∈ ΠI (µ → ϱ)
and γ − ∈ ΠJ (ϱ → ν). Then there exists γ ∈ ΠI∪J (µ → ν) satisfying C2 (γ)2 = C2 (γ + )2 +
C2 (γ − )2 .

    In this statement, C2 (γ)2 is the cost of transport plan γ, and ΠI (µ → ν) denotes the set
of transport plans between probability measures µ to ν, under two additional restrictions:
1) particles can only move along “I-aligned” lines, e.g. along rows in the example; and
2) particles can only move “up” in the partial order on Rd , i.e. a particle can move from
x to y only if x ⪯ y—we call such a transport plan directed. The second restriction is
important because, when we finally recover a Poincaré inequality from a transport-energy
inequality, the information that particles only moved up in the partial order will be reflected
in the appearance of the directed gradient ∇− .
   Now, informally, Theorem 7.2 tells us that each step of the above strategy (e.g. moving
particles
R − 2 along rows) incurs cost bounded by the directed Dirichlet energy, i.e. the integral
 (∂i f ) dx of the partial derivatives along the (say) rows. Repeating for every i ∈ [d] via
Lemma 7.3, we obtain the directed, multidimensional transport-energy inequality:

Theorem 7.4 (Transport-energy inequality). There exists a universal constant C > 0
such     that the following holds. Let a ∈ (0, 1), and let f ∈ Lip satisfy 1 − a ≤ f ≤ 1 + a and
         f dx = 1. Define the probability measures dµ := f dx and dµ∗ := f ∗ dx on [0, 1]d .

                                             (1 − a)     [0,1]d


    Moreover, by the order preservation property of our solution to our PDE, each operation
along direction i preserves the monotonicity along the directions j < i, so the final function
f ∗ in the statement above, which is the result after all d steps, is indeed monotone. The
notation W22 (µ → µ∗ ) indicates that this bound holds even for directed transport plans, as
explained above.


7.1.5     Recovering a Poincaré inequality via optimal transport du-
          ality
The final step is to recover, from the multidimensional transport-energy inequality, our
desired directed Poincaré inequality. Fortunately, there is an intimate connection between


                                              102
transport and Poincaré inequalities (as well as Sobolev, logarithmic Sobolev, and other
isoperimetric and concentration inequalities), and this theory is well established in the
classical case (c.f. [Vil09, Chapters 21 and 22]). Closest to the present approach, [Liu20]
proved, in the classical setting, the equivalence between transport-energy, Poincaré, and
other related inequalities. Our task, therefore, is to obtain at least an implication in the
directed setting.
    The main ingredient toward this goal is the notion of Kantorovich duality for the
Wasserstein distance. In the classical setting, the weak Kantorovich duality says that,
given two probability measures µ and ν, if we can find “certificate functions” ϕ and ψ
satisfying
                    ϕ(y) − ψ(x) ≤ |x − y|2     for all points x and y,            (7.4)
then                                   Z                      Z
                        W22 (µ, ν) ≥           ϕ(y) dν(y) −           ψ(x) dµ(x) .      (7.5)

Villani recounts the following analogy by Caffarelli: suppose you operate a consortium of
bakeries and cafes in a city, and that µ(dx) is the supply of bread by a bakery at point
x while ν(dy) is the demand by a cafe at point y. Then if transporting a unit of bread
from x to y costs |x − y|2 , the minimum cost for transporting all the bread is by definition
W22 (µ, ν). Suppose a transportation company offers to take over the transportation job by
buying bread from the bakeries at price ψ(x) and selling it to cafes at price ϕ(y). If (7.4)
holds then, for each x and y, hiring the transportation company is no more expensive than
handling the transportation yourself. Therefore the cost W22 (µ, ν) is at least as large as
how much the bakery-cafe consortium pays the company to fulfill the supply and demand,
which is the RHS of (7.5).
    In the directed setting, where we can only transport mass from x to y if x ⪯ y, we
expect the transport to be in general more expensive, so “more” certificates should be
valid. Indeed, it is not difficult to obtain the following natural directed version of weak
duality (Lemma 7.126): if

                      ϕ(y) − ψ(x) ≤ |x − y|2             for all points x ⪯ y,

then                                       Z                      Z
                       W22 (µ → ν) ≥            ϕ(y) dν(y) −           ψ(x) dµ(x) .

   The final main ingredient is an operator which, given a candidate function ϕ, produces
the best possible ψ for duality. This is accomplished by a directed analogue of the so-called


                                                   103
Hamilton-Jacobi operator, defined as follows: for each function h on the unit cube,

    At this point, instead of trying to reproduce the calculations from Section 7.4.2, let us
give some intuition for how the pieces above recover a Poincaré inequality. By homogeneity,
it suffices to consider mean-zero functions h. The key idea is to fix a small t > 0 and let h
play two roles at the same time: 1) as the building block for measures dµ(x) = (1 + th) dx
and dµ∗ (x) = (1 + th∗ ) dx, where h∗ is the monotone function obtained via the coordinate-

function −th for duality via (7.6). That is, after building measures µ and µ∗ from h and
h∗ (as perturbations of the uniform measure, scaled by the parameter t), we also employ
h to build certificate functions ϕ = −th and ψ = H     ⃗ 1 (−th). This yields, via (7.6), a bound
on the Wasserstein distance to monotonicity of (the measure µ produced by) h which is
itself written in terms of h.

                                               104
Organization. The rest of this chapter is organized as follows. In Section 7.2, we study
the directed heat equation, showing that this PDE is well-posed and satisfies the properties
listed in Section 7.1.2. In Section 7.3, we introduce optimal transport and establish the
transport-energy inequality (Theorem 7.4). Finally, in Section 7.4 we recover the directed
Poincaré inequality (Theorem 7.1) from the transport-energy inequality.


7.2      Directed heat semigroup
In this section, we make concrete the ideas presented in Section 7.1.2 of the proof overview.
Recall that we wish to analyze the directed heat equation, which we informally specified in
(7.3) as
                                       ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u .                                (7.8)
We require a more rigorous definition before we can study this PDE, since e.g. the quantity
∂x− u = min{0, ∂x u} is not in general differentiable even if u is smooth. Indeed, while the
classical heat equation has a smoothing effect, we expect any solution to (7.8) (in the
appropriate sense) to have non-differentiable points, so a more technical treatment of this
nonlinear PDE is unavoidable.
    The first step is to replace the operator ∂ − u with something more amenable to anal-
ysis. In Definition 7.11 we define a canonical representation u = u↑ + u↓ such that u↓
is nonincreasing and differentiable (more precisely, it is in the Sobolev space H 1 ), while
u↑ is nondecreasing and need not be differentiable (it only needs to be in the space L2 of
square-integrable functions). While a priori such a representation need not be unique, we
show in Proposition 7.10 that thereR is a unique   representation (after fixing the shift by a
                                           2
constant) minimizing the energy (∂x u↓) dx; intuitively, this means that we pack all the
“decreasing behaviour” of u into u↓, and all the “increasing behaviour” of u into u↑. Note
that u↑ may even contain jump discontinuities, which our theory must withstand, so this
decomposition captures extremely relevant properties of our problem.
   Then, we are able to restate our PDE more rigorously in Definitions 7.14 and 7.18.
Essentially, the “Neumann problem”
                               (
                                 z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in (0, 1)
                                 ∂x u↓ = 0    on {0, 1}

given in (7.9) (where z will take the role of ∂t u in Definition 7.18) replaces ∂x− u with ∂x u↓ in
(7.8), and then places a “Neumann boundary condition” ∂x u↓ = 0 which, as is standard in

                                               105
PDEs, means that there will be no mass flux at the boundaries of the domain. For technical
reasons that are common to most study of PDEs, we need to give in (7.10) a notion of
“weak solution”, which is essentially a solution which may not be regular enough to satisfy
the specification of the PDE (i.e. it may not be twice-differentiable), but which behaves
(via integration by parts) just like a “strong solution” for many analytical purposes, and
can sometimes be “promoted”: a weak solution that is regular enough is usually also a
strong solution (in fact, our problem automatically enjoys such a promotion thanks to
“elliptic regularity”, see Lemma 7.24).
    To study our PDE, we bring in the theory of gradient flows and maximal monotone
operators [Bre73] by recasting the PDE as the problem of finding the direction of steepest
descent2 (in the space of L2 functions) of the directed Dirichlet energy E − (u), which our
formalism allows us to define in terms of u↓ in Definition 7.12. The main results in
Section 7.2.2 are Proposition 7.20 on the existence of a unique solution to the gradient flow
problem – which allows us to define a semigroup operator Pt , the directed heat semigroup,
describing the evolution of this solution – and Corollary 7.23, which shows that this solution
is also a solution to the Neumann problem (i.e. our PDE of interest).
    To conclude the basic study of our PDE, Section 7.2.3 shows, via the theory of gradient
flows, that the directed Dirichlet energy E − (Pt u) decays exponentially in time (Propo-
sition 7.25), which allows us to conclude that Pt u converges to some limit as t → ∞
(Proposition 7.27).
    Sections 7.2.4 to 7.2.6 are concerned with showing that regular initial states u yield
solutions Pt u that are also regular for all times t > 0, where “regular” can mean Lipschitz
or Sobolev class H 1 . The main result in Section 7.2.4 is Lemma 7.28, a technical lemma
which says that for solutions to our PDE, if ∂x u↓ < 0 in an interval (a, b) (which we wish
to call “u is decreasing”), it must be the case that u↑ is constant in (a, b) (legitimizing
that wish). This result is far from being vacuous or tautological – it implies that in regions
where our PDE is “active” (because ∂x u↓ is nonzero), the other component u↑ of u is
“inactive” (constant), which means that solutions to our PDE avoid a host of pathological
scenarios and allows crucial technical arguments to go through. Then, using technical
arguments and tools from the theory of maximal monotone operators, Section 7.2.5 builds
toward Corollary 7.41 – H 1 regularity is preserved over time – and Section 7.2.6 builds
toward Corollary 7.48 – Lipschitz regularity is preserved over time.
   In Section 7.2.7, we turn our attention to nonexpansiveness and order preservation
properties. The main result, Proposition 7.57, is a quantitative order preservation result
which implies that if initial states u, v satisfy u ≤ v, then Pt u ≤ Pt v for all times t > 0
  2
      This is analogous to our analysis of the heat flow on directed graphs as a gradient system in Chapter 6.


                                                     106
(Corollary 7.59). We conclude Section 7.2.7 with some useful basic observations about the
semigroup Pt ; in particular, Proposition 7.66 shows that Pt behaves nicely under certain
affine transformations, namely Pt (αu + β) = αPt u + β when α > 0 and β ∈ R.
    Finally, Section 7.2.8 studies the convergence of solutions Pt u as t → ∞. The main
result is Proposition 7.68, which shows that Pt u converges to a monotone function, which
we define as P∞ u in Definition 7.69. The remainder of Section 7.2.8 shows that the main
properties of Pt shown in the previous subsections – preservation of regularity, nonexpan-
siveness, behaviour under affine transformations – are also satisfied by P∞ .


7.2.1     Preliminaries for PDE
We briefly outline some of the main concepts required to study our PDE, and refer the
reader to e.g. [Eva10; Bre11] for detailed expositions.
    Let J = (a, b) be an open interval. The absolutely continuous (AC) functions f : J → R
                                                                1
                 R for which there exists a function g ∈ L (J) such that, for all x ∈ J,
are precisely those
f (x) = f (a) + (a,x) g dx. We call g a weak derivative of f , and write ∂x f for any weak
derivative of f . The weak derivative is almost everywhere (a.e.) uniquely determined,
and moreover, f is classically differentiable a.e. and its classical derivative agrees with ∂x f
almost everywhere.
    Let Ω := J d . Let k ∈ Z≥0 and p ∈ [1, +∞]. The Sobolev space W k,p (Ω) is the space of
functions f : Ω → R which have weak derivatives up to order k in Lp (Ω). The definition of
weak derivative in the multidimensional case is more involved than in the one-dimensional
case, but the details are not relevant here. In one dimension, we may recursively define
W 0,p (J) := Lp (J) and, for k ≥ 1, W k,p (J) as the set of functions f ∈ W k−1,p (J) such that
∂x f ∈ W k−1,p (J).
    We identify a.e. equal functions into equivalence classes in W k,p (Ω), and we write “f = g
a.e.” and “f = g in W k,p (Ω)” interchangeably. Often, we are interested in a continuous
representative of f , which is unique when it exists, and by abuse of notation write f for
its continuous representative as well. With this interpretation, a standard fact is that
W 1,∞ (Ω) is precisely the class of Lipschitz functions on Ω. If 1 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ +∞, then
W k,q (Ω) ⊂ W k,p (Ω).
   The case p = 2 is special because then H k (Ω) := W k,2 (Ω) is a Hilbert space. In one
dimension, the inner product in H k (J) is
                                                k
                                                X
                             ⟨f, g⟩H k (J) :=         Di f, Di g L2 (J) ,
                                                i=0


                                                 107
where Di above denotes the i-th weak derivative. When the space of integration is clear,
we drop the subscript from the inner product notation. The inner product above also
induces the norm                          v

                                                 i=0


   For a sequence (fn )n∈N ⊂ H k (Ω) and f ∈ H k (Ω), the notation “fn → f in H k (Ω)”
means convergence in norm, i.e. ∥fn − f ∥H k (Ω) → 0 as n → ∞. We write “fn ⇀ f weakly
in H k (Ω)” to denote weak convergence in this space, which means that φ(fn ) → φ(f ) as
n → ∞ for every φ in the dual space of H k (Ω). The dual space of L2 (Ω) is L2 (Ω) itself
with the L2 (Ω) inner product action φ(f ) = ⟨φ, f ⟩. The dual space of H 1 (Ω) is larger, but
contains L2 (Ω) with the same action.
    It is often useful to approximate a function by a sequence of smooth functions, and
for that we will use mollification. The following comes from [Eva10, Appendix C]. The
standard mollifier η ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) is given by

We then have the following properties (see Theorem 7 of [Eva10, Appendix C]):

  1. f ε ∈ C ∞ (Uε ).

  3. If f ∈ C(U ), then f ε → f uniformly on compact subsets of U .

  4. If 1 ≤ p < ∞ and f ∈ Lploc (U ), then f ε → f in Lploc (U ),

where Lploc (U ) is the space of measurable functions whose every restriction to compact
K ⊂ U is in Lp (K), and convergence in Lploc (U ) means convergence in Lp (K) for every
compact K ⊂ U .
    We also use the following abstract spaces. Let X be a real Banach space (for us,
typically L2 (J)), let 1 ≤ p ≤ +∞ and let T ∈ (0, +∞]. Then the Bochner space Lp (0, T ; X)

As usual, C([0, T ]; X) denotes the set of continuous functions u : [0, T ] → X. When
X possesses the so-called Radon-Nikodym property, which in particular is the case for
X = L2 (J), and for T < +∞, the characterization of absolutely continuous functions
as those possessing an integrable weak derivative introduced earlier extends to functions
u : [0, T ] → X (see [DU77, pp. 217–219]).
    Above and hereafter, we use boldface symbols, e.g. u, to denote Banach-valued func-
tions whose domain we think of as the time in an evolution equation.
   Hereafter, we write I for the unit interval (0, 1) unless otherwise specified, which will
always be clearly indicated.


7.2.2    Neumann and gradient flow problems
The next definition captures the “natural” class of functions we need to handle in our PDE
study; very informally, those whose “decreasing regions are differentiable”.

Definition 7.5 (Down-H 1 functions). We define the set U of down-H 1 functions as

                              U := {u ∈ L2 (I) : R(u) ̸= ∅} ,

where R(u) is the set of admissible representations for u as follows:
                        n

Remark 7.6. The properties “nondecreasing” and “nonincreasing” in the definition above
technically do not apply directly to members of L2 (I) and H 1 (I), which are equivalence
classes of a.e. equal functions. Rather, we mean that u(1) is a member of L2 (I) which has a
representative function I → R that is nondecreasing, and likewise for u(2) . More generally,
when f is an object of any space that identifies a.e. equal functions, we abuse language
and write “f is nonincreasing” (resp. nondecreasing) to mean that f has a nonincreasing
(resp. nondecreasing) representative.

Remark 7.7. It is easy to check that H 1 (I) ⊂ U, since for any u ∈ H 1 (I) we may separate
the positive and negative parts of ∂x u into u(1) and u(2) , respectively, and shift u(1) and
u(2) by appropriate constants so that they satisfy the conditions of Definition 7.5. Then,
since H 1 (I) is dense in L2 (I), so is U.

                                            110
Definition 7.11 (Canonical representation). For each u ∈ U, the canonical representation

L2 (I) by assigning +∞ to functions outside of U, will enable us to find and study a solution
to our PDE using tools from the theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows.
Our main reference for this theory is [Bre73].

Definition 7.16 (Nice evolution function). Let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) and u′ : (0, +∞) →

                                                 111
Remark 7.17. Definition 7.16 captures functions that are Lipschitz away from zero, and
absolutely continuous with square-integrable weak derivative near zero.

Definition 7.18 (Neumann evolution problem). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I))
be a nice evolution function. We say u is a weak solution to the Neumann evolution
problem with initial state (or initial data) u0 if 1) u(0) = u0 ; 2) u(t) ∈ U for all t > 0; and
3) u(t), u′ (t) form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem for a.e. t > 0.

    Before introducing the gradient flow problem, we need some notation. Let φ : L2 (I) →
[0, +∞] be a convex, proper, lower semicontinuous function. We write D(φ) := {u ∈
L2 (I) : φ(u) < +∞} for the domain of φ. For any u ∈ L2 (I), we define the subdifferential
of φ at u as
               ∂φ(u) := {z ∈ L2 (I) : ∀v ∈ L2 (I) . φ(v) ≥ φ(u) + ⟨z, v − u⟩} ,
and we write D(∂φ) := {u ∈ L2 (I) : ∂φ(u) ̸= ∅} for the domain of ∂φ. Using the fact that
φ is proper, it is easy to check that D(∂φ) ⊆ D(φ). It is standard that ∂φ is a maximal
monotone operator ; we will not use (the definition of) this property explicitly, but rather
rely on the theory of such operators as presented in [Bre73].
   We can now define the gradient flow problem:

Definition 7.19 (Gradient flow problem). Let u0 ∈ U and let u be a nice evolution
function. We say u is a solution to the gradient flow problem with initial state (or initial
data) u0 if 1) u(0) = u0 ; 2) u(t) ∈ D(∂E − ) for all t > 0; and 3) u′ (t) ∈ −∂E − (u(t)) for
a.e. t > 0.

   Note that in both Definitions 7.18 and 7.19, the pointwise condition at t = 0 makes
sense by the requirement that u be continuous.
   The theory of maximal monotone operators and gradient flows immediately yields that
the gradient flow problem has a unique solution, as follows:

Proposition 7.20. Let u0 ∈ U. Then there exists a unique solution u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I))
to the gradient flow problem. Moreover, for all t > 0 we have

                        E (u0 ) − E (u(t)) =       ∥u′ (s)∥2L2 (I) ds .

for all t > 0.

Proof. This is a direct application of [Bre73, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 and Proposition 3.1].

Directed heat semigroup. As described in [Bre73], the nonexpansive property of so-
lutions u allows us to define an operator Pt : L2 (I) → L2 (I) such that Pt u = u(t) for
each initial state u ∈ U with corresponding solution u, and with Pt u defined by continuous
extension when u ∈ L2 (I) \ U (recall that U is dense in L2 (I)). Then for every u ∈ L2 (I)
and t > 0 it holds that Pt u ∈ D(∂E − ), and (Pt )t≥0 forms a nonexpansive semigroup in
L2 (I), i.e.

  1. Pt+s = Pt Ps for all t, s ∈ R≥0 , with P0 the identity;
  2. Pt u → u in L2 (I) as t ↓ 0, for all u ∈ L2 (I); and
  3. ∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥u − v∥L2 (I) for all u, v ∈ L2 (I) and t ∈ R≥0 .

The last property also implies that, for each t ≥ 0, Pt : L2 (I) → L2 (I) is continuous. We
say that Pt is the semigroup generated by −∂E − .
   It turns out that the solution to the gradient flow problem is also a weak solution to
the Neumann evolution problem, as the following results show.

Proposition 7.21. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ) and z ∈ −∂E − (u). Then u, z form a weak solution
to the static Neumann problem.

Proof. Note that we have u ∈ D(∂E − ) ⊆ D(E − ) = U. Let ϕ ∈ H 1 (I); we will prove that
(7.10) holds. Let α ̸= 0, let ψα := αϕ and let vα := u + ψα . Since −z ∈ ∂E − (u), we have

which will imply that E − (vα ) = D− (vα ). In fact, we establish the following:

Corollary 7.23. Let u0 ∈ U and suppose u is a solution to the gradient flow problem.
Then u is also a weak solution to the Neumann evolution problem.

Proof. The condition u(0) = u0 holds by definition of solution to the gradient flow problem,
and for all t > 0 we have u(t) ∈ D(∂E − ) ⊆ D(E − ) = U. Finally, for a.e. t > 0 we have
u′ (t) ∈ −∂E − (u(t)), which by Proposition 7.21 implies that u(t), u′ (t) form a solution
to the static Neumann problem. Hence u is a weak solution to the Neumann evolution
problem.

                                                114
7.2.3     Exponential decay of directed Dirichlet energy
The following elliptic regularity result is standard, and essentially shows that weak solutions
to the static Neumann problem are in fact strong solutions whose regularity is two degrees
higher than that of z; in particular, even if we only have z ∈ L2 (I), we gain one degree of
regularity by obtaining u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) when we only assumed u↓ ∈ H 1 (I). Recall that H 0 (I)
is the same as L2 (I).

Lemma 7.24 (Elliptic regularity; see [Mik78, Chapter IV Section 2, Theorems 3 and 4]).
Let k ≥ 0 be an integer and let u ∈ U, z ∈ H k (I) form a weak solution to the static
Neumann problem               (
                                z = ∂x ∂x u↓ in I
                                ∂x u↓ = 0    on {0, 1} .
Then u↓ ∈ H k+2 (I), and moreover ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓ a.e. in I.

   We now give our energy decay result.

Proposition 7.25. There exists a constant K > 0 such that the following holds. Let
u ∈ U. Then for all t > 0,
                              E − (Pt u) ≤ e−Kt E − (u) .

Proof. Let u ∈ U and let u(t) = Pt u be the corresponding solution to the gradient flow
problem. Recall that Proposition 7.20 gives, for all t > 0,
                                            Z

                      E (u(t)) = E (u) +         −∥u′ (s)∥2L2 (I) ds .
                                                 (0,t)

It follows that t 7→ E − (u(t)) is absolutely continuous on every interval [0, T ] with weak
derivative ∂t E − (u(t)) = −∥u′ (t)∥2L2 (I) . Moreover for a.e. t > 0 we have that u(t), u′ (t)
form a weak solution to the static Neumann problem, and thus u′ (t) = ∂x ∂x u(t)↓ a.e.
in I and ∂x u(t)↓ = 0 on {0, 1} by Lemma 7.24. Therefore the Poincaré inequality (for
zero-on-the-boundary functions) yields

                       2                   2                       2
for some constant C > 0. It follows that for a.e. t > 0, we have
                                                            2

   The exponential decay of t 7→ E − (Pt u) allows us to find a Cauchy sequence in (Pt u)t≥0 ,
and thus establish its strong convergence to some limit in L2 (I). Later on, we will say
more about this limit by reasoning about the weak convergence of Pt u (to the same limit).

Lemma 7.26 (Cauchy sequence). Let u ∈ U, and let (uk )k∈N be given by uk := Pk u. Then
(uk )k is Cauchy as a sequence in L2 (I). As a consequence, uk → u∗ in L2 (I) for some
u∗ ∈ L2 (I).

Proposition 7.27 (Strong convergence). Let u ∈ U. Then Pt u converges in L2 (I) to some
u∗ ∈ L2 (I) as t → ∞.

Proof. Let u∗ be such that (Pk u)k∈N converges to u∗ in L2 (I) as k → ∞, as given by
Lemma 7.26. We claim that Pt u → u∗ . Let ε > 0, and let N ∈ N be such that ∥Pk u −
u∗ ∥L2 (I) < ε for all k ≥ N , as given by the convergence of the sequence (Pk u)k . Then for
any t ≥ N , letting j := ⌊t⌋, we have

                   ∥Pt u − u∗ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥Pj u − u∗ ∥L2 (I) + ∥Pt u − Pj u∥L2 (I)

by the triangle inequality. We have ∥Pj u − u∗ ∥L2 (I) < ε by the choice of N , and on the
other hand,
                       Z

                       Z

                                   1/2               ′

                                          (j,t)
                                           1/2
                  ≤ E − (Pj u) − E − (Pt u)                                  (t − j ∈ [0, 1), Proposition 7.20)
                  ≤ e−Kj/2 E − (u)1/2                                            (E − ≥ 0, Proposition 7.25) .

Thus by letting N be large enough, we can ensure that

                ∥Pt u − Pj u∥L2 (I) ≤ e−Kj/2 E − (u)1/2 ≤ e−KN/2 E − (u)1/2 < ε ,

and hence ∥Pt u − u∗ ∥L2 (I) < 2ε. Thus Pt u → u∗ in L2 (I).


7.2.4     Auxiliary results for studying regularity of solutions
The following lemma establishes the intuitive property that, if ∂x u↓ < 0 in a certain region,
then u↑ is constant in that region, i.e. u has no increasing component.

Lemma 7.28 (Functions in D(∂E − ) are well-behaved). Suppose u ∈ D(∂E − ). Let (a, b) ∈
I be a nonempty interval and suppose that ∂x u↓(x) < 0 for all x ∈ (a, b). Then u↑ is
constant in (a, b).

                                                          117

justifies writing the condition that ∂x u↓ < 0 on (a, b), which we take to mean in terms of
the continuous representative of ∂x u↓ ∈ H 1 (I)).

follows from Lemma 7.24), we may assume without loss of generality (by extending the
interval (a, b) if necessary) that ∂x u↓(a) = ∂x u↓(b) = 0.

    We relied on the following technical lemma, which intuitively says that if f is nonde-
creasing on an interval and g ≤ 0 attains 0 at the boundaries of that interval, then f and
∂x g are positively correlated.

Lemma 7.29. Let J ⊂ R be a finite, nonempty open interval. Let f ∈ L2 (J) be nonde-
creasing and let g ∈ H 1 (J) be such that g < 0 in J and g = 0 on ∂J, i.e. the endpoints of
J. Let [a, b] ⊂ J, let δ := f (b) − f (a) and let α := − sup(a,b) g. Then ⟨f, ∂x g⟩L2 (J) ≥ αδ.

Proof. See Appendix B.

   The following auxiliary result confirms the natural expectation that, if u has a first
derivative and some degree of regularity, then its components u↑ and u↓ enjoy the same
regularity and, moreover, can be obtained by taking the positive and negative parts of the
derivative ∂x u, respectively.

Proposition 7.30. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞, and let u ∈ U ∩ W 1,p (I). Then u↑, u↓ ∈ W 1,p (I) and,
moreover, we have ∂x u↑ = ∂x+ u and ∂x u↓ = ∂x− u a.e. in I.

                                               120
Proof. Let u(1) , u(2) : I → R be given by
                             Z                                                      Z
     (1)
   u (y) := u(0) + C +           ∂x+ u(x) dx              and     (2)

                                (0,y)                                                 (0,y)

for each y ∈ I, where C ∈ R is implicitly defined so as to satisfy
                                     Z
                                         u(2) dx = 0 .
                                              I

∂x u(1) = ∂x+ u and ∂x u(2) = ∂x− u a.e. in I, and
                                             Z
                   (1)     (2)
                                                   ∂x+ u(x) + ∂x− u(x) dx = u(x)
                                                                      
                 (u + u )(y) = u(0) +
                                                  (0,y)

for all y ∈ I by the absolute continuity of u. It is also clear that u(1) , u(2) ∈ L∞ (I) ⊂ Lp (I),
since they are pointwise upper bounded in magnitude by |u(0)| + |C| + ∥∂x u∥L1 (I) < +∞.
Moreover, since u ∈ W 1,p (I), we have ∂x u ∈ Lp (I) and hence ∂x+ u, ∂x− u ∈ Lp (I), yielding
that u(1) , u(2) ∈ W 1,p (I).
     The proof will be concluded if we show that (u↑, u↓) = (u(1) , u(2) ). However, at this
point we cannot even state that (u(1) , u(2) ) ∈ R(u) because we have not established that
u(2) ∈ H 1 (I) = W 1,2 (I) (unless p ≥ 2, of course). However, if we can show that any
(v (1) , v (2) ) ∈ R(u) satisfies
                                  Z              Z
                                               ?
                                    (∂x v ) dx ≥ (∂x u(2) )2 dx ,
                                         (2) 2
                                                                                      (7.12)
                                    I                      I

then using the assumption that u ∈ U and the definition of u↑, u↓, we will conclude that
indeed (u↑, u↓) = (u(1) , u(2) ), as needed.
    Let (v (1) , v (2) ) ∈ R(u). We claim that ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) for a.e. x ∈ I, which
will imply (7.12). Recall that v (2) ∈ H 1 (I) ⊂ W 1,1 (I). Since u = v (1) + v (2) a.e. and
u ∈ W 1,p (I) ⊂ W 1,1 (I), we conclude that v (1) ∈ W 1,1 (I). Hence u, u(1) , u(2) , v (1) , v (2)
are all absolutely continuous. The fundamental theorem of calculus for the Lebesgue
integral implies that, almost everywhere in I, these functions are all differentiable, and
their classical and weak derivatives agree with ∂x u = ∂x u(1) + ∂x u(2) = ∂x v (1) + ∂x v (2) . On
any such point x, the monotonicity of u(1) , u(2) , v (1) , v (2) implies that ∂x u(1) (x), ∂x v (1) (x) ≥ 0
and ∂x u(2) (x), ∂x v (2) (x) ≤ 0. Therefore
    ∂x v (2) (x) = −∂x v (2) (x) = −∂x u(x) + ∂x v (1) (x) = −∂x u(2) (x) − ∂x u(1) (x) + ∂x v (1) (x)
                = ∂x u(2) (x) − ∂x u(1) (x) + ∂x v (1) (x) .

                                                    121
Now, if ∂x u(x) ≥ 0, then ∂x u(2) (x) = 0 by definition, so ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) holds
trivially. Otherwise, we conversely have ∂x u(1) (x) = 0 while ∂x v (1) ≥ 0, and hence, by the
above,
                                   ∂x v (2) (x) ≥ ∂x u(2) (x) ,
which concludes the proof.


7.2.5      Preservation of H 1 regularity
We wish to show that if the initial state u is in H 1 (I), then Pt u remains in H 1 (I) for all
times t > 0. To that end, define φ : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] by
                                    (
                                      ∥∂x u∥2L2 (I) if u ∈ H 1 (I)
                           φ(u) :=
                                      +∞            otherwise.

   The theory of maximal monotone operators gives us a recipe to establish that t 7→
φ(Pt u) is nonincreasing. The key ingredients are Claim 7.31 and Lemma 7.33.

Claim 7.31. The functional φ is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof. Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicon-
tinuity. Since L2 (I) is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity.
Let (un )n∈N be a sequence in L2 (I) such that un → u in L2 (I). We need to show that
                                                  ?
                                           φ(u) ≤ lim inf φ(un ) .
                                                      n→∞

The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subse-
quence (unk )k∈N such that limk→∞ φ(unk ) = A < +∞. By extracting a subsequence if
necessary, we may assume that φ(unk ) < +∞, and thus unk ∈ H 1 (I), for every k.
    We claim that (unk )k is bounded in H 1 (I). Indeed suppose this is not the case. Then
since ∥unk ∥2H 1 (I) = ∥unk ∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x unk ∥2L2 (I) and ∥∂x unk ∥2L2 (I) remains bounded due to the
fact that φ(unk ) → A, we conclude that ∥unk ∥L2 (I) gets arbitrarily large as k → ∞. But
this contradicts the fact that unk → u in L2 (I), so the claim holds.
   It follows that we may extract a weakly convergent subsequence (unkℓ )ℓ∈N , and by
uniqueness of weak limits, we obtain that u ∈ H 1 (I) and unkℓ ⇀ u weakly in H 1 (I). By
weak lower semicontinuity the norm in H 1 (I),
∥u∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x u∥2L2 (I) = ∥u∥2H 1 (I) ≤ lim inf ∥unkℓ ∥2H 1 (I) = lim inf ∥unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) + ∥∂x unkℓ ∥2L2 (I) .

Fact 7.32 (See e.g. [EG15, Theorem 4.4]). Let 1 ≤ p < ∞ and let f ∈ W 1,p (I). Then
∂x f = 0 a.e. on {f = 0}.

Fact 7.34. Let f ∈ H 2 (I). Then f ∈ C 1,1/2 (I). In particular, f is continuously differen-

Lemma 7.35. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), suppose u is continuous, and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Let λ > 0
and let v := u − λz. Let 0 < a < b < 1 and suppose u(a) < u(b). Then for all ε > 0, there

   We first observe that it cannot be the case that ∂x u↓(x) < 0 for all x ∈ (a, b), since

Lemma 7.36. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), suppose u is not continuous, and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Let
λ > 0. Then u − λz is not continuous.

Proof. Recall that, by Proposition 7.21, u, z form a weak solution to the static Neumann
problem, and by Lemma 7.24 we have u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) with ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓
in L2 (I). In particular, u↓ is continuously differentiable by Fact 7.34.
   Since u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) is continuous while u is not continuous, we conclude that u↑ is not
continuous and, since it is monotone, it contains only jump discontinuities. Let x0 ∈ I be
a point of jump discontinuity of u↑, i.e. a point such that L− < L+ where

                      L− := lim− u↑(x)        and      L+ := lim+ u↑(x) .
                             x→x0                             x→x0


We first claim that ∂x u↓(x0 ) = 0. Indeed, it is clear that ∂x u↓ ≤ 0 since u↓ is nonincreasing
and continuously differentiable. If we had ∂x u↓(x0 ) < 0, then by continuity ∂x u↓ would
be strictly negative in a neighbourhood of x0 , in which case Lemma 7.28 would imply that
u↑ is constant in a neighbourhood of x0 , contradicting the fact that L− < L+ . Hence the
claim holds.
    We now claim that, for all δ > 0, there exists x ∈ (x0 , x0 + δ) such that z(x) ≤ 0.
Suppose for a contradiction that there exists δ > 0 such that for all x ∈ (x0 , x0 + δ),
z(x) > 0. Then, since z = ∂x ∂x u↓ a.e. and ∂x u↓(x0 ) = 0, we conclude that ∂x u↓ > 0 in
(x0 , x0 + δ), contradicting the fact that ∂x u↓ ≤ 0. Hence the claim holds. By the same
reasoning, we conclude that for all δ > 0 there exists x ∈ (x0 − δ, x0 ) such that z(x) ≥ 0.
   Thus, for all δ > 0 there exist points x− ∈ (x0 − δ, x0 ), x+ ∈ (x0 , x0 + δ) such that
z(x+ ) − z(x− ) ≤ 0 and hence
                               h                 i h                    i    h             i
(u − λz)(x+ ) − (u − λz)(x− ) = u↑(x+ ) − u↑(x− ) + u↓(x+ ) − u↓(x− ) − λ z(x+ ) − z(x− )
                               h                 i h                    i
                              ≥ u↑(x+ ) − u↑(x− ) + u↓(x+ ) − u↓(x− ) .

Since u↑ → L+ as x ↓ x0 and u↑ → L− as x ↑ x0 and u↑ is nondecreasing, we have
u↑(x+ ) − u↑(x− ) ≥ L+ − L− . By the continuity of u↓, we can let δ > 0 be small enough so

                                                 128

                                    . We conclude that, for all sufficiently small δ > 0, there

and thus u − λz is not continuous.

   We are ready to obtain our preservation of regularity result. We will use the following
tool from the theory of maximal monotone operators.

Lemma 7.37 (Specialization of [Bre73, Theorem 4.4]). Let H be a Hilbert space and let
τ : H → [0, +∞] be a convex, proper, and lower semicontinuous functional such that
τ (ProjD(A) x) ≤ τ (x) for all x ∈ H. Let A : H → 2H be a maximal monotone operator and
let St be the semigroup generated by −A. Then the following are equivalent:

  1. τ ((I + λA)−1 x) ≤ τ (x) for all x ∈ H and λ > 0; and

  2. τ (St x) ≤ τ (x) for all x ∈ D(A) and t ≥ 0.

     In the statement above, (I + λA)−1 : H → D(A) is the resolvent of A (also denoted by
Jλ , see e.g. [Eva10, p. 563]).
    We will need the following fact and observation about the density of our domains of
interest in L2 (I).

Fact 7.38 (See e.g. [Bar76, Chapter II, Corollary 2.1]). The set D(∂E − ) is a dense subset
of D(E − ) = U.

Observation 7.39. Since U contains H 1 (I), which is dense in L2 (I), Fact 7.38 implies
that
                         D(∂E − ) = D(E − ) = U = L2 (I) .

Proposition 7.40 (φ-monotonicity of solutions). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈
C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then
for all 0 ≤ t1 ≤ t2 < +∞, we have φ(u(t1 )) ≥ φ(u(t2 )).

Proof. This is a direct consequence of Lemma 7.37. Indeed, letting H := L2 (I) and
A := ∂E − (which is maximal monotone as remarked earlier), we first observe that indeed


                                             129
φ(ProjD(A) f ) ≤ φ(f ) for all f ∈ L2 (I), since by Observation 7.39 we have ProjD(A) f = f .
Thus it suffices to show that for all f ∈ L2 (I) and all λ > 0,
                                                        ?
                                  φ((I + λA)−1 f ) ≤ φ(f ) .

But this is equivalent to showing that, for all u ∈ D(A), −z ∈ A(u) and λ > 0,
                                              ?
                                     φ(u) ≤ φ(u − λz) ,

which is precisely Lemma 7.33.

Corollary 7.41 (Preservation of H 1 regularity). Suppose u0 ∈ H 1 (I), and let u ∈
C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then
u(t) ∈ H 1 (I) for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 7.40 and the definition of φ.


7.2.6     Preservation of Lipschitz regularity
It will also be useful to control the Lipschitz regularity of solutions. At a high level, we
follow a similar strategy as in Section 7.2.5. Recall that W 1,∞ (I) is equivalent to the space
of Lipschitz real-valued functions on I, up to identification of almost everywhere equal
functions. Define ψ : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] by
                                    (
                                      ∥∂x u∥L∞ (I) if u ∈ W 1,∞ (I)
                           ψ(u) :=
                                      +∞           otherwise.

Fact 7.42. Let u ∈ L2 (I) and let M ∈ R≥0 . Then ψ(u) ≤ M if and only if u = f a.e. for
some M -Lipschitz function f : I → R. Moreover, in this case f is the (unique) continuous
representative of u and ψ(u) is the Lipschitz constant of f .

Claim 7.43. The functional ψ is convex, proper and lower semicontinuous.

Proof. Convexity and properness are straightforward; it remains to verify lower semicon-
tinuity. Since L2 (I) is a metric space, it suffices to check sequential lower semicontinuity.
Let (un )n∈N be a sequence in L2 (I) such that un → u in L2 (I). We need to show that
                                          ?
                                    ψ(u) ≤ lim inf ψ(un ) .
                                                  n→∞


                                                  130
The only relevant case is when the RHS above is finite, so suppose there exists a subse-
quence (unk )k∈N such that limk→∞ ψ(unk ) = M < +∞. By extracting a subsequence if
necessary, we may assume that ψ(unk ) < +∞, and thus unk ∈ W 1,∞ (I), for every k. For
simplicity and using Fact 7.42, fix for each nk the continuous representative of unk , which
we denote by the same name. Then each unk is Mk -Lipschitz with Mk → M .
    Since unk → u in L2 (I), it is standard that we may extract a subsequence that converges
to u pointwise almost everywhere. Denote this further subsequence again by (unk )k , and
let N ⊂ I be a measure zero set such that unk → u pointwise in I \ N . Then for each
x ̸= y in I \ N , we have

           |u(x) − u(y)| = lim |unk (x) − unk (y)| ≤ lim Mk |x − y| = M |x − y| .
                            k→∞                         k→∞

By Lemma 7.44 below, u is a.e. equal to an M -Lipschitz function, so ψ(u) ≤ M by
Fact 7.42, as needed.

   We used the following simple lemma.

Lemma 7.44 (From “almost Lipschitz” to Lipschitz). Let M ∈ R≥0 and let N ⊂ I be a
measure zero set. Suppose f : I → R satisfies |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M |x − y| for all x, y ∈ I \ N .
Then there exists a M -Lipschitz function g : I → R such that f = g in I \ N .

Proof. See Appendix B.

Definition 7.45. For any w : I → R and distinct x, y ∈ I, let
                                                   w(y) − w(x)
                                slopew (x, y) :=               .
                                                      y−x
Lemma 7.46. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ) and let z ∈ −∂E − (u). Then for all λ > 0, ψ(u − λz) ≥
ψ(u).

Proof. We follow the proof outline from Lemma 7.33, but some of the technical details are
different. As in that proof, we have u↓ ∈ H 2 (I) with ∂x u↓ = 0 on {0, 1} and z = ∂x ∂x u↓
in L2 (I).
   We may assume that u − λz ∈ W 1,∞ (I), since otherwise ψ(u − λz) = +∞ and there is
nothing to prove. In particular, this establishes that u − λz is continuous.

Proposition 7.47 (ψ-monotonicity of solutions). Let u0 ∈ U and let u ∈
C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 . Then
for all 0 ≤ t1 ≤ t2 < +∞, we have ψ(u(t1 )) ≥ ψ(u(t2 )).

Proof. As in the proof of Proposition 7.40, this follows from Lemmas 7.37 and 7.46.

Corollary 7.48 (Preservation of Lipschitz regularity). Suppose u0 ∈ W 1,∞ (I), and let
u ∈ C([0, +∞); L2 (I)) be the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial data u0 .
Then u(t) ∈ W 1,∞ (I) for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 7.47 and the definition of ψ.




                                                   135
7.2.7     Nonexpansiveness and order preservation
Our goal in this section is to establish that the semigroup Pt is nonexpansive and order
preserving. These properties will help us show that applying Pt to line restrictions in the
multidimensional setting behaves as expected by “making progress” toward monotonicity
with each application.
   In the context of PDEs, one desirable way to show that a property is preserved through
time is to differentiate in time, and then pass the derivative inside the integral to exploit
the definition of the PDE. However, we need to establish some technical results before we
can justify such calculations.
    We start with the following lemma, slightly adapted from [CT80], which reveals a
close connection between order preservation and nonexpansiveness (here, in the supremum
norm) of operators. Hence our strategy will be to establish order preservation, and conclude
nonexpansiveness.

Lemma 7.49. Let (Ω, Σ, µ) be a finite measure space, and let 1 ≤ p ≤ +∞. Let L∞ (Ω) ⊂
C ⊂ Lp (Ω) have the property that for all f ∈ C and r ∈ R, f + r ∈ C. Let T : C → Lp (Ω)
be continuous as a map from Lp (Ω) to Lp (Ω) (the continuity requirement may be dropped
if p = ∞) satisfying
                               T (f + r) = T (f ) + r a.e.                        (7.21)
for every f ∈ C and r ∈ R. Then the following are equivalent:

 (a) For all f, g ∈ C, if f ≤ g a.e. then T f ≤ T g a.e. .

 (b) For all f, g ∈ C, (T f − T g)+ ≤ ess sup(f − g)+ a.e. .

 (c) For all f, g ∈ C, |T f − T g| ≤ ess sup|f − g| a.e. .

Proof. We show (a) =⇒ (b) =⇒ (c) =⇒ (a). First, assume (a) holds, and let f, g ∈ C.
Suppose r := ess sup(f − g)+ < +∞, since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Note that
g + r ≥ f and g + r ≥ g both a.e. . Thus by (a) and (7.21), we have

                 T g + (T f − T g)+ = T f ∨ T g ≤ T (g + r) = T g + r a.e. ,

so (T f − T g)+ ≤ r a.e. as needed. Next, suppose (b) holds, and let f, g ∈ C. Let
r1 := ess sup(f − g)+ and r2 := ess sup(g − f )+ ; note that ess sup|f − g| = max{r1 , r2 }, so



                                             136
we may assume that r1 , r2 < +∞, since otherwise there is nothing to show. Then indeed,
using (b),

    |T f − T g| = max (T f − T g)+ , (T g − T f )+ ≤ max{r1 , r2 } = ess sup|f − g| a.e. ,
                     


as needed. Finally, suppose (c) holds, and let f, g ∈ C satisfy f ≤ g a.e. . First, suppose
f, g ∈ L∞ (Ω). Let r := ess sup(g − f ), so that 0 ≤ r < +∞ by assumption. Then, by (c)
and (7.21),

        ess sup{T f − T g + r} = ess sup{T (f + r) − T g} ≤ ess sup|T (f + r) − T g|
                               ≤ ess sup|f − g + r| ≤ r ,

so T f ≤ T g a.e. as needed.
    Now, for general f, g ∈ C with f ≤ g a.e. , we may approximate them by sequences
(fn )n , (gn )n in L∞ (Ω) ⊂ C such that fn → f and gn → g in Lp (Ω) and moreover fn ≤
gn a.e. for each n; indeed, letting fn := max{−n, min{n, f }} and likewise for gn , it is
immediate that fn , gn ∈ L∞ (Ω) with fn ≤ gn a.e. , and the convergences fn → f and
                                                                                    p
gn → g in Lp (Ω) follow from the dominated convergence theorem:    R since |fp− fn | → 0
pointwise and |f − fn | ≤ |f | pointwise for each n, we have limn→∞ Ω |fn − f | dµ = 0.
    The previous case yields T fn ≤ T gn a.e. for each n, and we claim that the continuity
of T implies that T f ≤ T g a.e. as well. Indeed, we have T fn → T f an T gn → T g in Lp (Ω)
and thus

      (T f − T g)+ − (T fn − T gn )+ Lp (Ω) ≤ ∥(T f − T g) − (T fn − T gn )∥Lp (Ω)
                                            ≤ ∥T f − T fn ∥Lp (Ω) + ∥T g − T gn ∥Lp (Ω) → 0 ,

which means that (T fn − T gn )+ → (T f − T g)+ in Lp (Ω). But since (T fn − T gn )+ = 0 a.e.
and hence in Lp (Ω) for each n, we conclude that (T f − T g)+ = 0 a.e. , i.e. T f ≤ T g a.e.
as needed.

   The following lemma is a specific formulation of the well-known Leibniz rule for differ-
entiating under the integral sign. It is a specialization of the version stated in [Che], and
can be proved by a standard argument using the Fubini-Tonelli theorem.

Lemma 7.50 (Differentiating under the integral sign). Let (Ω, Σ, µ) be a measure space
and let [a, b] ⊂ R. Let f : [a, b] × Ω → R be a jointly measurable function satisfying the
following:


                                              137
  1. f (t, ·) ∈ L1 (Ω) for a.e. t ∈ (a, b).

  2. f (·, x) is AC for a.e. x ∈ Ω. (Its a.e. defined partial derivative is denoted by ∂t f as
     usual.)

  3. It holds that                 Z         Z
                                                  |∂t f (t, x)| dµ dt < +∞ .
                                     (a,b)    Ω

                      R
Then the function t 7→ Ω f (t, x) dµ is AC and
                    Z               Z
                 ∂t f (t, x) dµ =       ∂t f (t, x) dµ            for a.e. t ∈ (a, b).
                       Ω                 Ω


   The following fact is a standard consequence of the definition of absolute continuity.

Fact 7.51. Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. If f, g : J → R are AC, then f ∨ g is AC.

   We will also use the following standard fact about the weak derivative of the positive
part of a function.

Fact 7.52 (See e.g. [EG15, Theorem 4.4]). Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. Let 1 ≤ p <
∞ and let f ∈ W 1,p (J). Then f + ∈ W 1,p (J) and ∂x (f + ) = χ{f >0} ∂x f a.e. in J.

     We also use the following standard formulation of a chain rule for Sobolev functions.
This version follows e.g. from [EG15, Theorem 4.4], which is stated for globally Lipschitz
functions F , by using the fact that the image of f on J is bounded, so that the local
Lipschitz condition suffices (e.g. extend F linearly outside the image of f to obtain a
C 1 (R), globally Lipschitz function F̃ ).

Fact 7.53. Let J ⊂ R be a compact interval. Let 1 ≤ p < ∞, let f ∈ W 1,p (J), and let
F ∈ C 1 (R) be locally Lipschitz. Then F ◦ f ∈ W 1,p (J) and ∂x (F ◦ f )(x) = F ′ (f (x))∂x f (x)
for a. e. x ∈ J.

   The following lemma essentially says that, given an element of a Bochner space, we
can get a handle on a concrete jointly measurable function “representing” that element in
a precise sense. This makes concrete the intuitive expectation that a solution u to our
PDEs, which maps each point in time to an element of L2 (I), should also give us a specific
value at each point in time and space “u(t, x)”.

                                                     138
Lemma 7.54. [DS58, Theorem 17, p. 198] Let (S, ΣS , µ) and (T, ΣT , λ) be measure

product. Let 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and let F be a µ-integrable function on S to Lp (T, ΣT , λ, X ) where
 X is a real or complex Banach space. Then there is a ρ-measurable function f on R to X ,
which is uniquely determined except for a set of ρ-measure zero, such that f (s, ·) = F (s) for
Rµ-almost all s in S. Moreover f (·, t) is µ-integrable on S for
                                                              R λ-almost all t and

    f (s, t)µ(ds), as a function of t is equal to the element S F (s)µ(ds) of L (T, ΣT , λ, X ).

Proposition 7.57. Let u0 , v0 ∈ H 1 (I), and let u, v be the solutions to the gradient flow
problem with initial data u0 , v0 respectively. Then the function ∆ : [0, +∞) → [0, +∞)

Corollary 7.58 (“Directed nonexpansiveness” of Pt ). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). Then for all t > 0,
