Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 7.57 when u, v ∈ H 1 (I), and the
general case follows by approximating u and v by H 1 (I) functions and using the continuity
of the map w 7→ Pt w from L2 (I) to L2 (I).

Corollary 7.59 (Pt is order preserving). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I), and suppose u ≤ v a.e. . Then
Pt u ≤ Pt v a.e. for all t > 0.

Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Corollary 7.58.

   We also note below the that the semigroup Pt has no effect on monotone functions.

                                               143
Lemma 7.60. Every nondecreasing u ∈ L2 (I) is a stationary point of Pt , i.e. Pt u = u for
all t.

Proof. By definition, u = u↑ + u↓ with u↑ = u and u↓ = 0, so E − (u) = 0. Thus for every
v ∈ L2 (I),
                            E − (v) ≥ 0 = E − (u) + ⟨0, v − u⟩ ,
so 0 ∈ ∂E − (u). Thus u(t) = u is the solution to the gradient flow problem with initial
state u.

Observation 7.61. Corollary 7.59 in particular implies that if u ≥ a a.e. for some a ∈ R,
then Pt u ≥ Pt a = a a.e. for all t > 0, the equality by Lemma 7.60.

  The following results show that Pt is degree one positively homogeneous and additive
when one argument is a constant function.

Lemma 7.62. Let u ∈ U, β ∈ R, and v := u + β. Then v ∈ U with v↑ = u↑ + β and
v↓ = u↓, and thus E − (v) = E − (u).

Proof. This is a straightforward consequence of the definition of v↑, v↓.

Lemma 7.63. Let u ∈ U, α > 0, and v := αu. Then v ∈ U with v↑ = αu↑ and v↓ = αu↓,
and thus E − (v) = α2 E − (u).

Proof. This is a straightforward consequence of the definitions of v↑, v↓ and E − .

Lemma 7.64. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), β ∈ R, and v := u + β. Then ∂E − (u) = ∂E − (v).

Proof. By symmetry, it suffices to prove that ∂E − (u) ⊆ ∂E − (v). Let z ∈ ∂E − (u). Then
for all w ∈ U, using Lemma 7.62 twice and the definition of subdifferential,

          E − (w) = E − (w − β) ≥ E − (u) + ⟨z, w − β − u⟩ = E − (v) + ⟨z, w − v⟩ ,

and hence z ∈ ∂E − (v) as desired.

Lemma 7.65. Let u ∈ D(∂E − ), α > 0, and v := αu. Then ∂E − (v) = α∂E − (u).




                                            144
Proof. By symmetry, it suffices to prove that α∂E − (u) ⊆ ∂E − (v). Let z ∈ ∂E − (u); we
claim that αz ∈ ∂E − (v). Indeed for all w ∈ U, using Lemma 7.63 twice and the definition
of subdifferential,

        E − (w) = α2 E − α1 w ≥ α2 E − (u) + z, α1 w − u = E − (v) + ⟨αz, w − v⟩ ,
                                                       

and hence αz ∈ ∂E − (v) as desired.

Proposition 7.66 (Effect of certain affine transformations on Pt ). Let u ∈ L2 (I), α > 0,
and β ∈ R. Then Pt (αu + β) = αPt u + β.

Proof. We suffices to prove the statement for u ∈ U; the general case then follows by
approximating U by H 1 (I) functions and the continuity of Pt from L2 (I) to L2 (I). For
v := u + β, we observe that v(t) := u(t) + β with v ′ (t) := u′ (t) is a solution to the gradient
flow problem with initial state v, since for each t > 0 for which −u′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (u(t)),
Lemma 7.64 implies that −v ′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (v(t)).
    Similarly, let w := αu. Then w(t) := αu(t) with w′ (t) := αu′ (t) is the solution to
the gradient flow problem with initial state w, since for each t > 0 for which −u′ (t) ∈
∂E − (u(t)), Lemma 7.65 implies that −w′ (t) ∈ ∂E − (w).


7.2.8     Convergence to monotone equilibrium
Since the directed Dirichlet energy E − (Pt u) decays over time and Pt u converges to some
limit as t → ∞ (by Proposition 7.27), we expect this limit to be a monotone function. Let
us establish this fact and other properties of that limit.

Lemma 7.67. Let u ∈ U satisfy E − (u) = 0. Then u is nondecreasing.

Proof. Since E − (u) = 0, we have ∂x u↓ = 0 a.e. in I. Hence u↓ is a constant function, while
u↑ is nondecreasing by definition.

Proposition 7.68. Let u ∈ L2 (I). Then there exists a nondecreasing u∗ ∈ U, unique as
an element of L2 (I), such that Pt u → u∗ in L2 (I) as t → ∞.

Proof. Since E − achieves its minimum (namely 0, on e.g. constant functions) and Pt u ∈
D(∂E − ) for all t > 0, [AC84, Theorem 2, p. 160] implies that there exists a minimizer u∗
of E − such that Pt u ⇀ u∗ weakly in L2 (I). This means that u∗ ∈ U with E − (u∗ ) = 0, so
by Lemma 7.67 u∗ is nondecreasing. By Proposition 7.27 (which we may apply because,

                                              145
fixing any t0 > 0, we have Pt0 u ∈ D(∂E − ) ⊆ D(E − ) = U), Pt u also converges strongly
in L2 (I), and it is standard that the weak and strong limits agree and that this limit is
unique.

   Therefore the following definition is justified.

Definition 7.69 (Monotone equilibrium). Let P∞ : L2 (I) → U be the operator mapping
each u ∈ L2 (I) to the unique (as an element of L2 (I)) nondecreasing u∗ ∈ U such that
Pt u → u∗ in L2 (I) as t → ∞. We call P∞ u the monotone equilibrium of u.

   We now pass to the limit P∞ some useful properties of Pt .

Proposition 7.70 (“Directed nonexpansiveness” of P∞ ). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). Then
                   Z                          Z
                                     + 2
                                                         2
                                                 (u − v)+ dx .
                                              
                        (P∞ u − P∞ v)    dx ≤
                          I                            I

Proof. This follows by a standard limit argument as follows. Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). By Corol-
lary 7.58, ∥(Pt u − Pt v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(u − v)+ ∥L2 (I) for all t > 0. By definition of P∞ , we have
Pt u → P∞ u and Pt v → P∞ v in L2 (I) as t → ∞. Using the triangle inequality, we obtain
   ∥(Pt u − Pt v)+ − (P∞ u − P∞ v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(Pt u − Pt v) − (P∞ u − P∞ v)∥L2 (I)
                                            ≤ ∥Pt u − P∞ u∥L( I) + ∥Pt v − P∞ v∥L2 (I) → 0 ,
so (Pt u − Pt v)+ → (P∞ u − P∞ v)+ in L2 (I) as t → ∞. Since ∥(Pt u − Pt v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤
∥(u − v)+ ∥L2 (I) for all t > 0, we conclude that ∥(P∞ u − P∞ v)+ ∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥(u − v)+ ∥L2 (I) ,
which gives the conclusion.

Corollary 7.71 (P∞ is order preserving). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I), and suppose u ≤ v a.e. . Then
P∞ u ≤ P∞ v a.e. .

Proof. This is a direct consequence of Proposition 7.70.

Proposition 7.72. P∞ is nonexpansive, and therefore continuous, as an L2 (I) → L2 (I)
map.

Proof. Let u, v ∈ L2 (I). In the proof of Proposition 7.70, we showed that ∥(Pt u − Pt v) −
(P∞ u − P∞ v)∥L2 (I) → 0, which implies that ∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) → ∥P∞ u − P∞ v∥L2 (I) . But
∥Pt u − Pt v∥L2 (I) ≤ ∥u − v∥L2 (I) for all t > 0 since (Pt )t≥0 is a nonexpansive semigroup, so
the conclusion follows.

                                               146
Lemma 7.73. For every nondecreasing u ∈ L2 (I), we have P∞ u = u.

Proof. This follows from Lemma 7.60 along with the convergence Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I).

   Strong convergence to the monotone equilibrium, together with the preservation of reg-
ularity results from Section 7.2.5, allows us to obtain regularity of the monotone equilibrium
as well:

Proposition 7.74 (H 1 regularity of the monotone equilibrium). Let u ∈ H 1 (I). Then
P∞ u ∈ H 1 (I) with φ(P∞ u) ≤ φ(u).

Proof. Recall the functional φ from Section 7.2.5. Since u ∈ H 1 (I), φ(u) < +∞. By
Proposition 7.40, we conclude that φ(Pt u) ≤ φ(u) < +∞ for all t > 0. Since φ is lower
semicontinuous by Claim 7.31 and Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I) by Proposition 7.27, we conclude
that φ(P∞ u) ≤ φ(u) < +∞. Hence P∞ u ∈ H 1 (I).

Proposition 7.75 (Lipschitz regularity of the monotone equilibrium). Let u ∈ W 1,∞ (I).
Then P∞ u ∈ W 1,∞ (I) with ψ(P∞ u) ≤ ψ(u).

Proof. Recall the functional ψ from Section 7.2.6. Since u ∈ W 1,∞ (I), ψ(u) < +∞. By
Proposition 7.47, we conclude that ψ(Pt u) ≤ ψ(u) < +∞ for all t > 0. Since ψ is lower
semicontinuous by Claim 7.43 and Pt u → P∞ u in L2 (I) by Proposition 7.27, we conclude
that ψ(P∞ u) ≤ ψ(u) < +∞. Hence P∞ u ∈ W 1,∞ (I).

  We observe that P∞ also behaves nicely under the appropriate class of affine transfor-
mations.

Proposition 7.76 (Effect of certain affine transformations on P∞ ). Let u ∈ L2 (I), α > 0,
and β ∈ R. Then P∞ (αu + β) = αP∞ u + β.

Proof. Since Pt v → P∞ v as t → ∞ for each v ∈ L2 (I), applying Proposition 7.66 yields
                                                         h       i
   P∞ (αu + β) = lim Pt (αu + β) = lim [αPt u + β] = α lim Pt u + β = αP∞ u + β .
                    t→∞                t→∞                   t→∞


   We can finally conclude, via Lemma 7.49, that P∞ is nonexpansive in the L∞ norm.

Proposition 7.77 (P∞ is nonexpansive in L∞ norm). Let u, v ∈ L2 (I).                    Then
|P∞ u − P∞ v| ≤ ess sup|u − v| a.e. in I.

                                             147
Proof. We verify the conditions of Lemma 7.49 with (Ω, Σ, µ) the set I endowed with the
Lebesgue measure, p = 2, C = L2 (I), and T = P∞ . It is clear that for all f ∈ L2 (I)
and r ∈ R, f + r ∈ L2 (I). By Proposition 7.72, P∞ is a continuous L2 (I) → L2 (I) map.
Moreover, for all f ∈ L2 (I) and r ∈ R, the condition P∞ (f + r) = P∞ f + r holds by
Proposition 7.76. Finally, for all f, g ∈ L2 (I) with f ≤ g a.e. , we have that P∞ f ≤ P∞ g
a.e. by Corollary 7.71. The conclusion follows.


7.3      Directed transport-energy inequality
In this section, we establish a connection between the PDE studied above and the Wasser-
stein distance between the initial state u and its monotone equilibrium P∞ u, via the dynam-
ical approach embodied by the Benamou-Brenier formula, and then tensorize this result
from one dimension to [0, 1]d . For optimal transport concepts, we follow the presentation
and formalism of [San15], and also refer to [AGS05; Vil09].
    This section is organized as follows. After preliminary considerations in Section 7.3.1,
Section 7.3.2 shows that the PDE results from Section 7.2 imply a result about optimal
transport (Wasserstein) distance, namely a one-dimensional transport-energy inequality,
thereby implementing the ideas described in Section 7.1.3 of the proof overview. The idea
is to show that our solution to the directed heat equation
                                         ∂t u = ∂x ∂x− u
satisfies the so-called continuity equation
                                     ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0 ,
which prescribes that “particles” (distributed according to measure ϱt ) move according to
the velocity field vt ; in our setting, we have dϱt = u dx and vt = − ∂xuu↓ (Proposition 7.85),
so that indeed ∂x (ϱt vt ) = −∂x ∂x u↓ = −∂t ϱt . For functions u bounded close to 1, we can
informally disregard the denominator in vt and imagine, for simplicity, that vt ≈ −∂x u↓.
Then thanks to the exponential decay of
                                                Z
                                       −      1
                                     E (u) =      (∂x u↓)2 dx
                                              2
from the previous section, we conclude that the right-hand side of the Benamou-Brenier
formula
                         Z 1                                                             
           2                        2
         W2 (µ, ν) = min       ∥vt ∥L2 (ϱt ) dt : ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0, ϱ0 = µ, ϱ1 = ν
                              0


                                               148
from Proposition 7.83 is roughly upper bounded by E − (u), where (informally) the measures
dµ = u dx and dν = (P∞ u) dx correspond to the initial state and monotone equilibrium of
the directed heat equation, as probability measures4. Therefore we conclude in Theorem 7.2
the transport-energy inequality in one dimension,

                                       W22 (µ, µ∞ ) ≲ E − (u) .

Finally, the continuity equation also lets us conclude natural and useful properties of solu-
tions of the directed heat equation, such as mass conservation (Corollaries 7.86 and 7.87)
and the property that solutions can only ever lose mass in any given prefix of the interval
[0, 1] (Corollary 7.91).
    The next goal is to establish the tensorized (multidimensional) transport-energy in-
equality, thereby implementing the ideas described in Section 7.1.4 of the proof overview.
In Section 7.3.3, we develop the definitions and technical tools required to show our
“Pythagorean composition” result (Lemma 7.3), which yields a recipe for proving mul-
tidimensional transport-energy inequalities (with respect to the “directed Wasserstein dis-
tance”) by induction (Lemma 7.110).
    Finally, Section 7.3.4 ties together the tensorization recipe described above with the
one-dimensional results specific to our PDE from the previous sections. We show that, by
taking an initial state f : [0, 1]d → R with corresponding measure dµ = f dx, applying the
directed heat semigroup one coordinate at a time, and putting together the one-dimensional
transport-energy inequalities at each of these steps via properties of our PDE such as order
preservation and nonexpansiveness (which enable the induction to go through), we indeed
obtain a monotone final state f ∗ : [0, 1]d → R with corresponding measure dµ∗ = f ∗ dx for
which the multidimensional transport-energy inequality applies (Theorem 7.4):
                                               Z
                                           ∗               2
                                   2
                                W2 (µ → µ ) ≲        ∇− f dx .
                                                    [0,1]d



7.3.1      Preliminaries for optimal transport
We start by introducing notation and definitions relevant to the theory of optimal trans-
port.
   4
     More formally, we decompose the evolution from time t = 0 to time t → ∞ into intervals of unit time
length, but by the exponential energy decay, this summation is dominated by the initial term E − (u).




                                                  149
Projections. Say X × Y is a product space and z = (x, y) is an element in this space.
Then the projection operator onto the first coordinate, denoted interchangeably by π1 or
πx depending on the context, is given by π1 (z) = πx (z) = x. Similarly, π2 (z) = πy (z) = y.
We extend this definition in the natural way to projections from larger product spaces onto
smaller product spaces. For example, given the product space X1 × · · · × Xd and index set
I ⊂ [d], I = {i1 , . . . , in }, the operator πI projects any element in this space down to an
element of Xi1 × · · · × Xin . We also use the shorthand π−I := π[d]\I .
    Now, let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set and let I ⊆ [d]. For a point x ∈ Ω, we write the
projections xI := πI (x) and x−I := π−I (x), and we also write x = (xI , x−I ). Then, for the
set Ω, we write the projections ΩI := {xI : x ∈ Ω} and Ω−I := {x−I : x ∈ Ω}. For small
sets I or [d] \ I, we also use shorthand notation such as xi := x{i} and Ω−i−j := Ω−{i,j} .


Pushforward measure. Given measurable spaces (X, ΣX ) and (Y, ΣY ), measure µ on
X, and measurable map T : X → Y , the pushforward measure T# µ on Y is the measure
satisfying

                 (T# µ)(B) = µ(T −1 (B))              for all B ∈ ΣY , or equivalently,
       Z                      Z
            ϕ(y) d(T# µ)(y) =   ϕ(T (x)) dµ(x)         for all measurable ϕ : Y → R.
        Y                     X


  For a measure γ on product space X × Y , we say that (π1 )# γ and (π2 )# γ, which are
measures on X and Y respectively, are the first and second marginals of γ, respectively.
   If (Ω3 , Σ3 ) is another measurable space and S : Y → Z a measurable map, then it holds
that (S ◦ T )# µ = S# (T# µ).


Transport plans. For two probability spaces (Ω1 , Σ1 , µ1 ) and (Ω2 , Σ2 , µ2 ), we write
Π(µ1 , µ2 ) for the set of couplings, or transport plans, between µ1 and µ2 , namely prob-
ability measures γ on the product space Ω1 × Ω2 whose first and second marginals are µ1
and µ2 , respectively.


Space of probability measures. For any Borel set Ω ⊂ Rd , let P (Ω) denote the space
of all (Borel) probability measures on Ω. We endow P (Ω) with the weak topology, which is
the topology of weak convergence with respect to bounded continuous
                                                               R    functionals.
                                                                           R     Namely,
we say µn converges weakly to µ in P (Ω), and write µn ⇀ µ, if Ω ϕ dµn → Ω ϕ dµ for all
bounded continuous ϕ : Ω → R.

                                             150
Wasserstein distances. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and let p ∈ [1, +∞). Let
µ, ν ∈ P (Ω). Given transport plan γ ∈ Π(µ, ν), we define the cost Cp (γ) by
                                        Z                       1/p
                                                            p
                            Cp (γ) :=            |x − y| dγ(x, y)     .
                                           Ω×Ω

We then define the p-Wasserstein distance between µ and ν by

                                  Wp (µ, ν) :=        inf       Cp (γ) ,
                                                   γ∈Π(µ,ν)


and we also often refer to the quantity Wpp (µ, ν) := Wp (µ, ν)p . It is standard that Wp (·, ·)
is a distance metric on P (Ω).

Fact 7.78 (Wasserstein distance metrizes weak convergence; see e.g. [San15, Theo-
rems 5.10 and 5.11]). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set, and let p ∈ [1, ∞). Then
for a sequence (µn )n in P (Ω) and µ ∈ P (Ω), we have µn ⇀ µ if and only if Wp (µn , µ) → 0.

Corollary 7.79. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set, and let p ∈ [1, ∞). Then for
sequences (µn )n and (νn )n in P (Ω) with µn ⇀ µ and νn ⇀ ν for µ, ν ∈ P (Ω), we have
Wp (µn , νn ) → Wp (µ, ν). In other words, the p-Wasserstein distance is continuous in (the
weak topology on) P (Ω).

Proof. By the triangle inequality, we have

  Wp (µ, ν) − Wp (µ, µn ) − Wp (νn , ν) ≤ Wp (µn , νn ) ≤ Wp (µn , µ) + Wp (µ, ν) + Wp (ν, νn ) .

By Fact 7.78, the LHS and RHS converge to Wp (µ, ν) as n → ∞, so the conclusion
follows.


7.3.2     Optimal transport via Benamou-Brenier
Let {ϱt : t ∈ [0, T ]} be a family of measures on I and {vt : t ∈ [0, T ]} be a family of velocity
fields such that vt ∈ L1 (ϱt ) for each t. We start by defining what it means for the family
(ϱt , vt ) to solve the continuity equation

                                        ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0 .




                                                  151
Definition 7.80 (Weak solution; see [San15, Section 4.1.2]). Let (ϱt , vt ) be a family of
measure/velocity field pairs indexed by t ∈ [0, T ] such that vt ∈ L1 (ϱ) for each t. We
say that (ϱt , vt ) is a weak solution
                                R      to the continuity equation if, for every test function
ψ ∈ C 1 (I), the function t 7→ I ψ dϱt is absolutely continuous in t and, for a.e. t, we have
                                     Z         Z
                                  ∂t ψ dϱt = (∂x ψ)vt dϱt .                             (7.23)
                                     I          I

In this case, we call ϱ0 and ϱT the initial and final states of the solution, respectively (this
makes sense because the above implies that t 7→ ϱt is continuous for the weak convergence
of measures).

Remark 7.81. We will only work with absolutely continuous measures dϱt = u dx, u ∈
L2 (I).

Remark 7.82. Applying (7.23) with a constant test function ψ shows that every solution
to the continuity equation is mass conserving. In particular, Proposition 7.85 below implies
that this is true of the semigroup Pt ; see Corollary 7.86.

   The continuity equation is connected to Wasserstein distance via the Benamou-Brenier
formula:

Proposition 7.83 (Benamou-Brenier formula; see e.g. [San15, Theorem 5.28]). Let µ, ν
be probability measures on I. Then
                         Z 1                                                           
            2                      2
         W2 (µ, ν) = min      ∥vt ∥L2 (ϱt ) dt : ∂t ϱt + ∂x (ϱt vt ) = 0, ϱ0 = µ, ϱ1 = ν ,
                              0

where the constraint above means that (ϱt , vt )t∈[0,1] is a weak solution to the continuity
equation with initial state µ and final state ν.

   We now show our solution to the directed heat equation yields a solution to the conti-
nuity equation. This allows us to conclude several nice properties of this solution, such as
mass conservation, as well as apply the Benamou-Brenier formula to obtain a transport-
energy inequality.

Definition 7.90. Let µ and ν be probability measures on R. We say µ dominates ν, and
write µ ⪰ ν, if for every x ∈ R we have µ(−∞, x) ≥ ν(−∞, x).

Corollary 7.87.


7.3.3    Directed optimal transport and Pythagorean composition
We now introduce directed versions of basic optimal transport concepts and theory, with the
goal of combining a directed version of weak Kantorovich duality with a multidimensional
directed version of the transport-energy inequality from Theorem 7.2 to obtain our desired
directed Poincaré inequality via a perturbation argument. In the interest of space, we
refrain starting a systematic study of directed optimal transport, but rather limit ourselves
to the results we require.

Definition 7.92 (Quasimetric). Let X be a set. An extended real-valued function d :
X × X → [0, +∞], not necessarily symmetric, is called a quasimetric on X if it satisfies
the following:

  1. For all x, y ∈ X, d(x, y) ≥ 0 with d(x, y) = 0 if and only if x = y.
  2. For all x, y, z ∈ X, d(x, z) ≤ d(x, y) + d(y, z).

Definition 7.93. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set, and let µ and ν be probability measures on
Ω. We define the set Π(µ → ν) of directed couplings, or directed transport plans, from µ to
ν as                                        Z                         
                Π(µ → ν) := γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) :         χ{x̸⪯y} dγ(x, y) = 0 ,
                                                   Ω×Ω
where the integral is well-defined because the set {(x, y) ∈ Rd × Rd : x ̸⪯ y} is open and
hence Borel measurable. Note that the condition could also be written as γ ({x ̸⪯ y}) = 0,
or γ ({x ⪯ y}) = 1.

Definition 7.94 (Directed Wasserstein distance). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set
and let p ∈ [1, ∞). Given two probability distributions µ, ν over Ω, we define the directed
p-Wasserstein distance from µ to ν by
                               Wp (µ → ν) :=       inf    Cp (γ) ,
                                               γ∈Π(µ→ν)


                                             157
and we write Wpp (µ → ν) := Wp (µ → ν)p . Note that we may have Wp (µ → ν) = +∞, and
that Wp (· → ·) is not symmetric in general.

Observation 7.95. For all measures µ and ν, we have Π(µ → ν) ⊂ Π(µ, ν) and hence
Wp (µ, ν) ≤ Wp (µ → ν).

Lemma 7.96 (Gluing lemma; see e.g. [San15, Lemma 5.5]). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a Borel set.
Let µ, ϱ, ν be probability measures on Ω, and let γ + ∈ Π(µ, ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ, ν). Then there
exists a probability measure σ on Ω × Ω × Ω such that (πx,y )# σ = γ + and (πy,z )# σ = γ − .

    The proof of that Wp (· → ·) is a quasimetric follows the presentation of [San15, Lemma
5.4], with a simple additional argument to handle directed couplings.

Lemma 7.97 (Composition of directed transport plans). In Lemma 7.96, if γ + ∈ Π(µ →
ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν), then (πx,z )# σ ∈ Π(µ → ν).

Proof. Let γ := (πx,z )# σ. First, since (πx )# γ = (πx ◦ πx,z )# σ = (πx ◦ πx,y )# σ = (πx )# γ + =
µ, and similarly (πz )# γ = ν, we have γ ∈ Π(µ, ν). Moreover, by definition of pushforward
measure we have
 Z                        Z                               Z
                                                                                      
       χ{x̸⪯z} dγ(x, z) =           χ{x̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z) ≤           χ{x̸⪯y} + χ{y̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z)
   Ω×Ω
                          ZΩ×Ω×Ω                          Z Ω×Ω×Ω
                        =           χ{x̸⪯y} dσ(x, y, z) +          χ{y̸⪯z} dσ(x, y, z)

                          Z                           Z

                            Ω×Ω                        Ω×Ω

the last equality since γ + and γ − are directed couplings. Hence γ ∈ Π(µ → ν) as claimed.


Proposition 7.98. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and p ∈ [1, ∞). Then Wp (· → ·) is
a quasimetric on P (Ω).

Proof. It is clear that Wp (µ → ν) ≥ 0 always. If µ = ν then the identity coupling shows
that Wp (µ → ν) = 0, and conversely if Wp (µ → ν) = 0 then, by Observation 7.95,
Wp (µ, ν) = 0 and hence µ = ν. It remains to show that Wp (· → ·) satisfies the triangle
inequality.
  Let µ, ϱ, ν be probability measures on Ω. Let γ + ∈ Π(µ → ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν).
Then since we also have γ + ∈ Π(µ, ϱ) and γ − ∈ Π(ϱ, ν), apply Lemma 7.96 to obtain a

                                                158
probability measure σ on Ω × Ω × Ω such that (πx,y )# σ = γ + and (πy,z )# σ = γ − . Let
γ := (πx,z )# σ, so that γ ∈ Π(µ → ν) by Lemma 7.97. Then
                  Z                            1/p       Z                             1/p
                                   p                                            p
  Wp (µ → ν) ≤               |x − z| dγ(x, z)          =               |x − z| dσ(x, y, z)

Since γ + ∈ Π(µ → ϱ), γ − ∈ Π(ϱ → ν) were arbitrary, Wp (µ → ν) ≤ Wp (µ → ϱ) + Wp (ϱ →
ν).

   The directed Wasserstein distance arises naturally in the one-dimensional case when
one probability measure dominates the other in the sense of Definition 7.90, as we now
show.

Proposition 7.99 (Specialization of [San15, Theorem 2.9]). Let p ∈ (1, +∞). Let µ and ν
be two absolutely continuous probability measures on I with strictly positive densities. Then
there exists a unique γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) attaining Cp (γ) = Wp (µ, ν), and in fact γ = (id, Tmon )# µ
where Tmon : I → I is a nondecreasing function that is a.e. uniquely determined, and id
denotes the identity map.

Corollary 7.100. If in Proposition 7.99 we have µ ⪰ ν, then in fact γ ∈ Π(µ → ν). As
a consequence, Wp (µ → ν) = Wp (µ, ν).

Proof. We claim that Tmon (x) ≥ x for every x ∈ I. Indeed, suppose Tmon (x) < x for some
x ∈ I. Then
                                                       −1
          ν(0, Tmon (x)) = (Tmon# µ)(0, Tmon (x)) = µ(Tmon (0, Tmon (x))) ≥ µ(0, x) ,
                                            −1
the inequality because certainly (0, x) ⊆ Tmon (0, Tmon (x)), but we do not rule out at this
point that T remains constant for a while after x. But since Tmon (x) < x and ν has strictly
positive density by assumption, we conclude that

                                 ν(0, x) > ν(0, Tmon (x)) ≥ µ(0, x) ,


                                                   159
contradicting the assumption that µ ⪰ ν. Hence Tmon (x) ≥ x for every x ∈ I as claimed.
We now show that γ ∈ Π(µ → ν). Let S := {(x, y) ∈ I ×I : x ≤ y}. Since γ = (id, Tmon )# µ,
we have

            γ(S) = µ((id, Tmon )−1 (S)) = µ({x ∈ I : x ≤ Tmon (x)}) = µ(I) = 1 ,

so γ ∈ Π(µ → ν) as claimed.

    We now introduce the formal language for two related operations: constructing a prob-
ability measure by its marginal and conditional distributions, and the inverse process of
extracting marginal and conditionals from a probability measure, which is also called dis-
integration. We refer the reader to [AGS05, Section 5.3] for an overview of these ideas from
a measure-theoretic perspective.
    We use the following definition from [AGS05]. For X and Y separable metric spaces
and x ∈ X 7→ µx ∈ P (Y ) a measure-valued map, we say µx is a Borel map if x 7→ µx (B) is
a Borel map for any Borel set B ⊂ Y , or equivalently if this holds for any open set A ⊂ Y .
In this case we also have that
                                       Z
                                  x 7→    f (x, y) dµx (y)
                                            Y

is Borel for any bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function f : X × Y → R.

Definition 7.101 (Construction and disintegration). Let S be a finite set and let I ⊂ S.
Let xS\I ∈ RS\I 7→ µ|xS\I ∈ P (RI ) be a Borel map (the conditionals). Then for any
bounded (or nonnegative) Borel function f : RS → R, the RS\I → R map
                                  Z
                          xS\I 7→     f (xS\I , xI ) dµ|xS\I (xI )                 (7.24)
                                       RI

is Borel. Therefore, for any µS\I ∈ P (RS\I ) (the marginal ), we define µ ∈ P (RS ) implicitly
by                Z           Z                  Z
                    f dµ =       dµS\I (xS\I )             f (xS\I , xI ) dµ|xS\I (xI ) ,   (7.25)
                   RS       RS\I                      RI
                         R
and we formally write µ = RS\I µ|xS\I dµS\I .
    Conversely, given µ ∈ P (RS ), we let µS\I := (πS\I )# µ ∈ P (RS\I ) and write
(µ|xS\I )xS\I ∈RS\I ⊂ P (RI ) for the µS\I -a.e. uniquely determined Borel family of probability
                             R
measures such that µ = RS\I µ|xS\I dµS\I , and we call this decomposition a disintegration
of µ.

        −i


  2. For a transport plan γ ∈ P (Rd × Rd ), we write the index set as [2d] = S = Sx ∪ Sy
     for Sx := [d] and Sy := [2d] \ [d], so we write each element in the support of γ as
     a tuple (x, y) with x ∈ RSx and y ∈ RSy . Then, given index set I ⊂ [d], we may
     disintegrate γ into a marginal on index set SM := (Sx \ Ix ) ∪ (Sy \ Iy ) for Ix := I and
     Iy := {d + i : i ∈ I}, so that we think of the marginal as determining all but the I-
     indexed coordinates of the points x and y; and we view Rthe conditionals as supported
     along the Ix ∪ Iy directions. This disintegration is γ = RSM γ|xSM dγSM . To be clear,
     here we have γSM ∈ P (RSM ) and γ|xSM ∈ P (RIx ∪Iy ) for each xSM ∈ RSM . We make
                                                                 R
     the notation somewhat more mnemonic by writing γ = Rd ×Rd γ|x−I ,y−I dγ−Ix −Iy .
                                                                   −I   −I
     When we are considering a singleton I = {i}, we further simplify the notation by
     directly writing i in the place of I in this formula.

   For absolutely continuous probability measures on [0, 1]d , the following more familiar
characterization of disintegrations will be useful:

Proof. First, note that it is standard that u−i is a Borel map since it is the integral of
the section u(x−i , ·) at each point x−i . It is hence immediate that each u|x−i is also Borel.
Thus µ−i and all µ|x−i are indeed absolutely continuous probability measures.
    We now check the conditions of Definition 7.101. We claim that the map x−i ∈ Rd−i 7→
µ|x−i ∈ P (R) is Borel. Let B ⊂ R be a Borel set; we may assume that B ⊂ [0, 1]
without loss of generality, since all measures assign zero outside this interval. Then the
map x−i ∈ Rd−i 7→ µ|x−i (B) takes value zero outside [0, 1]d−i , and for x−i ∈ [0, 1]d−i , it is

This is again, at each point, the integral of a section over the subspace B, so it is standard
that this map is Borel. Hence the claim holds.
   It remains to verify (7.25). Let f : Rd → [0, +∞) be a nonnegative Borel function (and

Definition 7.103 (Aligned transport plans). Let I ⊊ [d]R be nonempty and let µ, ν ∈
P (Rd ). Let γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) and write its disintegration γ = Rd ×Rd γ|x−I ,y−I dγ−Ix −Iy . We
                                                                                 −I       −I
say γ is I-aligned if its marginal γ−Ix −Iy ∈ P (Rd−I × Rd−I ) satisfies the following:

                   γ−Ix −Iy (x−I , y−I ) ∈ Rd−I × Rd−I : x−I ̸= y−I = 0 .
                                                                      


                                                         162
Note that this condition is well-defined because the set being measured is open and hence
Borel. By convention, we say that every γ ∈ Π(µ, ν) is [d]-aligned. For any nonempty
I ⊆ [d], we denote the set of I-aligned transport plans by ΠI (µ, ν). When we have a
singleton I = {i}, we write i directly in the place of I in this definition.

Definition 7.104. For nonempty I ⊆ [d] and µ, ν ∈ P (Rd ), write ΠI (µ → ν) := Π(µ →
ν) ∩ ΠI (µ, ν).

   The following results let us find aligned transport plans by transporting mass only

Cp (γa ) = Wp (µa , νa ).

    Now, we show that the directed, aligned 2-Wasserstein distance enjoys a nice
“Pythagorean” composition property: composing I-aligned and J-aligned directed trans-
port plans, for I and J disjoint, yields an I ∪ J-aligned directed transport plan whose cost
is obtained from the costs of the two other plans via the Pythagorean theorem. Arguments
of this nature are well-known in the undirected case, e.g. a similar statement appears in
[Vil09, p. 572].

7.3.4    From one-dimensional PDE to optimal transport in the
         cube
We now tie most of the foregoing theory together by showing how to inductively transform
a function on the unit cube into a monotone function, one coordinate at a time via the
directed heat semigroup machinery, keeping track of the cost using the directed optimal
transport machinery.
    For this part of the proof, it is convenient to restrict our attention to Lipschitz func-
tions. As we will see, the Lipschitz property of f : [0, 1]d → R is preserved under taking
the monotone equilibrium along each axis-aligned direction, which enables an inductive
argument over the coordinates.
   Denote the set of Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R by Lip (the dimension d will always
be clear from context).

                                                     169
Observation 7.111. A function f : [0, 1]d → R is Lipschitz if and only if its restriction
to every axis-aligned line is uniformly Lipschitz, i.e. there exists some M > 0 such that,
for every i ∈ [d] and x−i ∈ [0, 1]d−i , f (x−i , ·) : [0, 1] → R is M -Lipschitz.

Definition 7.112 (i- and I-monotonicity). Let i ∈ [d]. We say f : [0, 1]d → R is i-
monotone if its every restriction along direction i is nondecreasing, i.e. if for every x−i ∈
[0, 1]d−i , f (x−i , ·) is nondecreasing. For I ⊆ [d], we say f is I-monotone if it is i-monotone
for every i ∈ I. We write MonI for the set of I-monotone functions.

Observation 7.113. Mon[d] is simply the set of monotone functions on [0, 1]d .

    The following useful characterization of i-monotone functions in terms of k-aligned lines
follows by definition:

Observation 7.114. Let i, k ∈ [d] be distinct. Then for all f : [0, 1]d → R, f is i-monotone
if and only if the following holds: for every z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k , and for all xi ≤ yi in [0, 1],
f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤ f (z−i−k , yi , ·).

Definition 7.115 (Monotone equilibrium operator). Let k ∈ [d]. We define the operator
Mk : Lip → ([0, 1]d → R) as follows: for each function f ∈ Lip, x−k ∈ [0, 1]d−k and
xk ∈ [0, 1], the function Mk f : [0, 1]d → R satisfies

                            (Mk f )(x−k , xk ) := (P∞ f (x−k , ·))(xk ) ,

where the application of P∞ is well-defined because each f (x−k , ·) is Lipschitz and hence
in L2 (I).

   The following lemma shows that Mk preserves Lipschitzness, confirming that Mk f is
well-defined pointwise as a real-valued function, as opposed to only defined up to sets of
measure zero.

Lemma 7.116. Let k ∈ [d]. Let f ∈ Lip. Then Mk f ∈ Lip.

Proof. Let M > 0 be such that every axis-aligned line restriction of f is M -Lipschitz. We
claim that every axis-aligned line restriction of Mk f is M -Lipschitz. We first check the
k-aligned lines. For any x−k ∈ [0, 1]d−k Proposition 7.75 implies that

                 ψ((Mk f )(x−k , ·)) = ψ(P∞ f (x−k , ·)) ≤ ψ(f (x−k , ·)) ≤ M ,

the last inequality and the conclusion that (Mk f )(x−k , ·) is M -Lipschitz by Fact 7.42.

                                                170
    It remains to check the other line restrictions. Let i ∈ [d]\{k} and let z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k .
Let xi ̸= yi ∈ [0, 1]. Since the axis-aligned line restrictions of f (in particular, in direction
i) are M -Lipschitz, we have

                       sup |f (z−i−k , xi , wk ) − f (z−i−k , yi , wk )| ≤ M |xi − yi | .
                     wk ∈[0,1]


Proposition 7.77 implies that

     |P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) − P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·)| ≤ ess sup|f (z−i−k , xi , wk ) − f (z−i−k , yi , wk )|
                                                       wk ∈[0,1]

                                                    ≤ M |xi − yi |                                       (7.28)

a.e. in I, and since P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) and P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·) are (Lipschitz) continuous as
observed above, we conclude that (7.28) holds pointwise. Since (7.28) holds for every
i ∈ [d] \ {k} and z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k , and since

    |(Mk f )(z−i−k , xi , ·) − (Mk f )(z−i−k , yi , ·)| = |P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) − P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·)|

pointwise, we get that every axis-aligned line restriction of Mk f is M -Lipschitz, and
Mk f ∈ Lip.

Lemma 7.117 (Mk application makes progress). Let k ∈ [d], and let I ⊆ [d] \ {k}. Let
f ∈ Lip ∩ MonI . Then Mk f ∈ Lip ∩ MonI∪{k} .

Proof. The fact that Mk f ∈ Lip is given by Lemma 7.116, and the fact that Mk f is
k-monotone follows from the definition of Mk and the fact that the monotone equilib-
rium P∞ f (x−k , ·) is nondecreasing. Now, let i ∈ I, so that f is i-monotone. By Ob-
servation 7.114, we have that for every z−i−k ∈ [0, 1]d−i−k , and for all xi ≤ yi in [0, 1],
f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤ f (z−i−k , yi , ·). By Corollary 7.71, we obtain that P∞ f (z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤
P∞ f (z−i−k , yi , ·) a.e. in I, and since these two functions are (Lipschitz) continuous, this
inequality holds pointwise. Thus (Mk f )(z−i−k , xi , ·) ≤ (Mk f )(z−i−k , yi , ·), and again by
Observation 7.114, Mk f is i-monotone. This concludes the proof.

Lemma 7.118 (Mk preserves bounds). Let f ∈ Lip. Let a ≤ b be real numbers and
suppose a ≤ f ≤ b. Then a ≤ Mk f ≤ b.

Proof. This follows by the definition of Mk together with Corollary 7.71 and Lemma 7.73.

Definition 7.122 (Coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium). For each f ∈ Lip, define f ∗ ∈
Lip, the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium of f , by

                                             f ∗ := Md Md−1 · · · M1 f .

   Note that indeed f ∗ is indeed Lipschitz by Lemma 7.116, and it is monotone:

Proposition 7.123. Let f ∈ Lip. Then f ∗ is monotone.

Proof. This follows by repeatedly applying Lemma 7.117, and recalling that Mon[d] is the
set of monotone functions on [0, 1]d .

  By Proposition 7.76 and the definition of Mk , we also observe that the coordinate-wise
monotone equilibrium behaves nicely with respect to certain affine transformations:


                                                        174
Observation 7.124. Let f ∈ Lip. Let α > 0 and β ∈ R. Then (αf + β)∗ = αf ∗ + β.

    Similarly, Lemma 7.119 implies that taking the coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium
is a nonexpansive operation:

Observation 7.125. Let f, g ∈ Lip. Then ∥f ∗ − g ∗ ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) ≤ ∥f − g∥L2 ((0,1)d ) . Since
0∗ = 0 by Lemma 7.73 (where we write 0 for the constant zero function), we conclude in
particular that ∥f ∗ ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) ≤ ∥f ∥L2 ((0,1)d ) .

    We are now ready to combine the ingredients from the previous sections and estab-
lish the transport-energy inequality. Note that this inequality is most effective when the
function f is pointwise bounded close to 1.

7.4       Directed Poincaré from Wasserstein and Kan-
          torovich
In this section, we establish our directed Poincaré inequality (Theorem 7.1) by combin-
ing the transport-energy inequality from Theorem 7.4 with a directed version of (weak)
Kantorovich duality via a perturbation argument, thereby implementing the ideas from
Section 7.1.5 of the proof overview. The general theme of going between transport inequal-
ities and Poincaré inequalities, as well as the spirit of the technical arguments presented
here, are well-known in the undirected case; we refer the reader to [Vil09, Chapters 7 and

                                                       177
22] for a comprehensive presentation and primary references, and to [Liu20] for an undi-
rected version of the precise reduction we instantiate here (that is, from a transport-energy
inequality to a Poincaré inequality).
    For simplicity, we do not attempt to initiate a systematic study of duality in directed
optimal transport, or to state results in the broadest possible generality, but rather limit
ourselves to what is required for the aforementioned goal. In Section 7.4.1, we introduce
the notion of directed weak Kantorovich duality and establish some of its properties. The
main results are Proposition 7.129, which gives a lower bound on the directed Wasserstein
distance between two measures in terms the so-called (directed) Hamilton-Jacobi operator
H⃗ (recall that the previous section gave an upper bound on this distance via the transport-
energy inequality), and Proposition 7.131, which relates the action H ⃗ t h(x) of this operator
                                 +
back to the directed gradient ∇ h(x) (which, recall, we wish to have in the right-hand side
of our Poincaré inequality). Then, in Section 7.4.2, we apply these tools via a careful choice
of dual function (a “perturbation argument”) to conclude the directed Poincaré inequality,
first for continuously differentiable functions (Theorem 7.133) and then, via a standard
approximation argument, for all H 1 functions (Theorem 7.1).


7.4.1     Directed weak Kantorovich duality and Hamilton-Jacobi
          operator
Kantorovich duality plays a fundamental role in the study of Wasserstein distance. The
weak part of this duality allows one to lower bound the Wasserstein distance between
two distributions by means of “test functions” satisfying a certain constraint. Here, we
translate a small part of this rich theory to the directed setting.

Lemma 7.126 (Weak duality). Let Ω ⊂ Rd be a bounded Borel set and let µ, ν ∈ P (Ω).

{y ∈ Rd : 0 < |x − y| < r} is the open ball of radius r centered at x with x itself excluded,
and let BΩ+ (x, r) := BΩ◦ (x, r) ∩ {y ∈ Rd : y ⪰ x}.
   The following result gives a directed analogue to some of the properties of the Hamilton-
Jacobi semigroup, as presented in [Vil09, Theorem 22.16].5

that y ∈ XN (resp. YN ) if and only if z ∈ XN (resp. YN ). Moreover, since ∂i h(w) > 0 for
all w in the line segment connecting y and z (which is contained in BΩ◦ (x, r0 ) ∪ {x}), the
fundamental theorem of calculus implies that h(z) > h(y).

7.4.2     Perturbation argument
We can now obtain the main result, first for C 1 ([0, 1]d ) functions and then for all of
H 1 ((0, 1)d ) by an approximation argument. The key idea of the perturbation argument is
to use the same (mean zero) function h in two different roles: 1) to construct absolutely
continuous probability measures from h and its coordinate-wise monotone equilibrium,
namely dµ = (1 + th) dx and dµ∗ = (1 + th∗ ) dx, for small t > 0; and 2) in the “test
function” −th for weak duality on W22 (µ → µ∗ ) via the directed Hamilton-Jacobi operator.

                                                            189
Chapter 8

Conclusion

In this thesis, we initiated the study of property testing in the analytic setting, where the
input is a continuous real-valued function and the goal is to test with respect to the Lp
distance. This research direction builds upon the current state of the art in property testing
in two ways: it extends the applicability of that paradigm to settings that are inherently
continuous in nature, and it reveals new conceptual and technical facets of long-studied
problems, hence contributing to a more comprehensive understanding of the mathematical
underpinnings of approximate decision making.
    We took the first steps toward a theory of analytic property testing by focusing on
the fundamental problem of monotonicity testing, and in particular its connection to the
phenomenon of directed isoperimetry. We gave a systematic account of directed isoperime-
try in continuous and discrete domains as captured by (Lp , ℓq )-Poincaré inequalities, and
showed that these inequalities imply efficient monotonicity testers for real-valued func-
tions f : [0, 1]d → R defined on the solid unit cube. While our initial study in Chapter 4,
where we obtained an O(d) query tester, leveraged the intuition from the classic edge tester
[GGLRS00] by defining and analyzing the appropriate continuous analogues
                                                                      √       of the discrete
arguments (namely via monotone rearrangements), our improved O( d) query tester relies
                                                                   e
on a new perspective on directed isoperimetry: its relationship with the convergence prop-
erties of the directed heat equation. We showed that the connection between isoperimetry
and the heat equation, which dates back to the work of Poincaré in mathematical physics
[Poi90], enjoys a natural directed analogue whose convergence properties we studied using
the theories of nonlinear partial differential equations and optimal transport.
   Naturally, there is no lack of open questions and exciting avenues for future research,
as we now outline. We divide our discussion in two parts: questions about monotonicity

                                             190
testing, and proposed directions for analytic property testing.


8.1      Monotonicity testing
Within monotonicity testing, the first open question that emerges from this thesis is that
of lower bounds for Lp monotonicity testing of Lipschitz functions. This thesis gave two
types of lower bounds:

  1. Strong lower bounds for specific classes of testers. In Chapters 4 and 5, we showed
     that the query complexity upper bounds given in Theorems 1.2 and 1.5 are essen-
     tially optimal for the type of tester given by those theorems, √ i.e. partial derivative
     testers and directional derivative testers. In particular, the O( d) bound from The-
                                                                    e
     orem 1.5 cannot be improved by any pair tester, i.e. an algorithm that repeatedly
     samples points x ⪯ y and rejects if f (x) > f (y), or any tester using the directional
     derivative analogue of such a pair test. In the discrete regimes of the Boolean cube
     and hypergrid, the best known monotonicity testers are indeed pair testers [KMS18;
     BCS23a].

  2. Lower bounds for constant dimensiond. In Chapter
                                                     4, we gave lower bounds showing
                                                  d
     that, for each constant d, at least Ω (M/ε) d+1 queries are required to test mono-
      tonicity of M -Lipschitz functions on [0, 1]d , and we gave similar results for hypergrid
      domains. These bounds show that the dependence of our upper bounds on M/ε is
                                                         d
      close to correct. Interestingly, the exponent d+1    in the lower bound cannot be im-
      proved in general, because in Chapter 4 we also gave a nearly-matching upper bound
      for the special case of the line (Theorem 4.4).

   The immediate open problem, then, is to give general lower bounds
                                                               √     in this model
with good dependence on the dimension d. We believe that the d dependence from
Theorem 1.5 is optimal.

Problem 8.1. Give a dΩ(1) lower bound for Lp monotonicity testing of 1-Lipschitz func-
tions f : [0, 1]d → R. As an easier problem, can we show such a bound for nonadaptive
testers?

    We may also ask about tolerant testing [PRR06]. In tolerant testing, we require the
tester to accept not only when the input f is monotone, but also when it is close to a

                                             191
monotone function. A closely related formulation refers to distance approximation: given
an input function f , and approximation parameters α > 0 (corresponding to additive
error) and ν > 1 (corresponding to multiplicative error), a (ν, α)-approximation algorithm
for monotonicity (with respect to some fixed distance metric dist) is an algorithm whose
output εb satisfies, with probability at least 2/3,
                            distmono (f ) − α ≤ εb ≤ ν · distmono (f ) .
Early works on monotonicity testing gave or implied (O(d), α)-approximation algorithms
with poly(d, 1/α) query complexity for Boolean functions on hypergrid domains, as well
as similar bounds with a dependence on log n for real-valued functions on the hypergrid
domains [DGLRRS99; GGLRS00;√FR10]. In more recent progress, [PRW22] extended the
work of [KMS18] to obtain an (O(e d), α)-approximation algorithm with poly(d, 1/α) query
                                           √
complexity, and they showed that the O(  e d) multiplicative factor is essentially optimal.
In the L1 setting, [BRY14a] gave distance approximation algorithms with a purely additive
error guarantee for the cases of the discrete line and the 2-dimensional grid.
    For the special case of Boolean functions f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1}, a simple variant of
the edge tester of [GGLRS00] suffices to obtain an (O(d), α)-approximation: instead of
rejecting whenever a violating edge is observed, compute εb by estimating the fraction
of violating edges in the hypercube. This works thanks to a reverse directed Poincaré
inequality which, together with Theorem 1.1, implies that the (relative Hamming) distance
distmono
    H    (f ) of f to monotonicity and its number V (f ) of violating edges are related by
the inequalities distmono
                      H   (f ) · 2d ≤ V (f ) ≤ distmono
                                                   H    (f ) · d · 2d . Hence a constant-factor
approximation to V (f ) yields an O(d)-approximation to distmono   H   (f ).
    Indeed, since we also showed in Section 6.3 reverse (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequalities for the
hypergrid [n]d up to a factor of d (which is the maximum degree of the directed hypergrid
graph), while our (L1 , ℓ1 )-Poincaré inequality from Theorem 4.2 takes a factor of n, a similar
argument gives an (O(nd), α)-approximation algorithm for the L1 distance to monotonicity
of (ℓ1 , M )-Lipschitz functions f : [n]d → R using poly(n, d, M, 1/α) queries. However, we
do not know how to implement this idea in the continuous case. Hence, we leave the
following open question:

Question 8.2. Is there a poly(d, 1/α)-query (O(d), α)-approximation algorithm for the Lp
distance to monotonicity of 1-Lipschitz functions f : [0, 1]d → R? How about (o(d), α)-
approximation?

   Finally, we may ask about the power of adaptivity for testing monotonicity in the
analytic setting. Indeed, whether adaptivity helps with testing monotonicity of Boolean

                                               192
functions is a notable open question in monotonicity testing – the best known
                                                                           √ 2 tester of
[KMS18] is nonadaptive, and indeed achieves optimal query complexity O( d/ε ) for non-
                                                                         e
                                                                     e 1/3 ) [CWX17]. On
adaptive testers, while the best lower bound for adaptive testers is Ω(d
the other hand, it is known that adaptivity does not help with testing monotonicity of
functions f : [n]d → R with respect to the relative Hamming distance [CS14]. In the
analytic setting, we ask the following question, whose resolution would most likely also
require progress on Problem 8.1.

Question 8.3. Does adaptivity help with Lp monotonicity testing of M -Lipschitz functions
f : [0, 1]d → R?


8.2      Further directions
Expanding our scope from monotonicity testing to the broader landscape of property test-
ing in the analytic setting, there are many interesting directions for future exploration.
Many of the classic properties which have been widely studied by the property testing
community over the past decades also have natural formulations in the analytic setting,
and here we list a few of them. Specifically, we ask about query-efficient property testers
for the following properties in the analytic setting. We believe that some of these properties
may be especially interesting to study in the tolerant regime.


Convexity. Two natural settings for testing convexity of functions are the unit cube
setting f : [0, 1]d → R under the uniform measure, and the setting of functions f : Rd →
R under the Gaussian measure—in particular, the rotation-invariance of the Gaussian
measure seems to be a natural fit for the rotation-invariance of the convexity property.
We remark that, in the discrete setting of functions f : [n]d → R, there is an exponential
Ω((n/d)d/2 ) nonadaptive lower bound for testing convexity with respect to the relative
Hamming distance [BBB20].


Unateness. A function f : [n]d → R is unate if it is either monotone or anti-monotone
along each direction i ∈ [d]. In the case of Boolean or real-valued functions over the
hypergrid [n]d or Boolean cube {0, 1}d , it is known that the query complexity of unateness
testing (with respect to the relative Hamming distance) is polynomial in d, 1/ε, and log n
[CWX17; CW19; BCPRS20].



                                             193
Juntas. A function f : {0, 1}d → {0, 1} on n variables is a k-junta if it depends on at
most k input variables. The query complexity of k-junta testing is Θ(k log k) for constant
proximity parameter ε [Bla09; Sag18]. In another relevant line of work, [DMN21] defined
a rotation-invariant notion of k-juntas, namely linear k-juntas, for the case of Boolean
functions f : Rd → {±1} over Euclidean space endowed with the Gaussian measure. They
showed that the class of linear k-juntas is not testable without further assumptions, but
it becomes testable if one assumes that f satisfies a “bounded surface area” assumption.
Here, we ask about testing k-juntas (or linear k-juntas) of real-valued f : [0, 1]d → R (or
f : Rd → R under the Gaussian measure), under a Lipschitz regularity assumption.


The dynamical approach. Finally, on a more conceptual note, we may ask about
further applications of the dynamical approach embodied by our proof of Theorem 1.4
in Chapter 7. In property testing, the idea of comparing the input object f to some
“ideal” object f ∗ that satisfies the property of interest is very natural, and we showed that
techniques from partial differential equations and optimal transport can be useful tools for
reasoning about this comparison smoothly in time. Therefore, it is plausible that these tools
may have something to say about other property testing problems of continuous nature.
In particular, we could attempt to apply this perspective for testing geometric properties
of sets in Rd , e.g. convexity. Testing convexity of sets S ⊂ Rd with respect to the Gaussian
measure is a notoriously√challenging problem: the best known query complexity upper
bound complexity is 2O( d)·poly(1/ε) [CFSS17], whereas the best known lower bounds are
                          e

poly(d) bounds for either nonadaptive testers, or testers with one-sided error [CDNSW25].
Is there a natural dynamical process by which an initial set S ⊂ Rd converges over time to
a convex set, and could the properties of such a process inspire new testers?

Appendix A

Nonadaptive lower bound from prior
work on Hamming testing

We briefly explain how the Ω(d log n) nonadaptive lower bound of [BRY14b] for Hamming
testing monotonicity of functions f : [n]d → R (with sufficiently small constant ε) also
applies to L1 -testing of (ℓ1 , O(1))-Lipschitz functions. The construction of [BRY14b] relies
on two main ingredients: step functions and Walsh functions.
    Let n = 2ℓ for simplicity. For each i ∈ {0, . . . , n} the i-th step function si : [2ℓ ] → [2ℓ−i ]
is given by                                            
                                               x−1
                                    si (x) =              + 1.
                                                 2i
In words, si (x) increases by 1 after every 2i consecutive elements (called a block of size 2i ).
  The Walsh functions are defined as follows. For each i ∈ [ℓ], the function wi : [2ℓ ] →
{±1} is given by
                               wi (x) = (−1)biti (x−1) ,
where the operator biti extracts the i-th bit of its input (indexed from least to most
significant). Then for each S ⊆ [ℓ], the function wS : [2ℓ ] → {±1} is given by
                                              Y
                                     wS (x) =    wi (x) .
                                                  i∈S


  These two types of functions are defined on the line, and they are extended into a
multidimensional construction on the hypergrid as follows. Given a vector i ∈ [ℓ]d , the

                                                 211
step function si : [2ℓ ]d → [d2ℓ ] is given by
                                                               d
                                                               X
                                     si (x1 , . . . , xd ) =         sij (xj ) ,
                                                               j=1

and given a vector S = (S1 , . . . , Sd ) of subsets of [ℓ], the Walsh function wS : [2ℓ ]d → {±1}
is given by
                                                         d
                                                         Y
                                 wS (x1 , . . . , xd ) =   wSj (xj ) .
                                                               j=1

    Then, [BRY14b] use a communication complexity argument (namely a reduction from
the AugmentIndex problem) to show that (nonadaptive) Hamming testing monotonicity
of functions hi,S : [2ℓ ]d → N of the form
                                      hi,S (x) = 2si (x) + wS (x) ,
for appropriate choices of i and S, requires at least Ω(d log n) queries. Therefore, to show
that (nonadaptive) L1 testing monotonicity of (ℓ1 , O(1))-Lipschitz functions also requires
at least this number of queries, it suffices to show that every such function h = hi,S satisfies

   1. h is (ℓ1 , O(1))-Lipschitz; and
   2. If distmono
             H    (h) ≥ ε, then distmono
                                    1    (h) ≳ ε.

    The first property follows from the definitions of the step and Walsh functions: let
x, y ∈ [2ℓ ]d be such that ∥x − y∥1 = 1. Then let j ∈ [d] be the coordinate such that
|xj − yj | = 1 and xk = yk for k ̸= j. Then
          |hi,S (x) − hi,S (y)| ≤ 2 sij (xj ) − sij (yj ) + |wS (x) − wS (y)| ≤ 2 + 2 = 4 ,
the first inequality because x and y agree on every coordinate except for j, and the second
inequality because the step function sij changes by at most 1 on adjacent inputs, and the
Walsh functions only take values ±1. Thus the first property holds.
    As for the second property, note that if distmono      H    (h) ≥ ε, then there exists a matching
of the form (xi , y i )i where for each i we have xi ⪯ y i and h(xi ) > h(y i ) (i.e. xi , y i form a
violating pair), such that at least an ε-fraction of the points [n]d belong to this matching
(see [FLNRRS02]). Now, for each such violating pair xi , y i , it follows that h(xi )−h(y i ) ≥ 1,
since h is integer-valued. Therefore for any monotone function h′ : [n]d → R, it must be
the case that |h(xi ) − h′ (xi )| + |h(y i ) − h′ (y i )| ≥ 1. Since this is true for disjoint pairs xi , y i
covering an ε-fraction of the domain, it follows that E [|h − h′ |] ≥ ε/2 for any monotone
function h′ . Hence distmono
                           1   (h) ≥ ε/2, and we are done.

Appendix B

Missing proofs from Chapter 7

We require the following standard result from convex optimization.

Lemma B.1 (See e.g. [Bre11, Corollary 3.23]). Let E be a reflexive Banach space and
let C ⊆ E be nonempty, closed and convex. Suppose f : C → (−∞, +∞] is convex,
proper, lower semicontinuous, and coercive in the sense that for every sequence (xn )n∈N
in C with ∥xn ∥E → ∞, we have f (xn ) → +∞. Then there exists x∗ ∈ C satisfying
f (x∗ ) = inf x∈C f (x).

Lemma B.2. Define Ω := (0, 1)d . Suppose fn ⇀ f weakly in L2 (Ω), and each fn is mono-
tone nondecreasing (resp. monotone nonincreasing). Then f is monotone nondecreasing
(resp. monotone nonincreasing).

Proof. Recall that for each ε > 0 and g ∈ L2 (Ω), g ε = ηε ∗ g is the mollification of g,
defined on Ωε = (ε, 1 − ε)d .
    Let ε ∈ (0, 1/3). We claim that fnε → f ε pointwise in Ωε . Indeed, fix any x ∈ Ωε and
define ηε,x ∈ C ∞ (Rd ) by ηε,x (y) := ηε (x − y). Since fn ⇀ f weakly in L2 (Ω), we obtain
         Z                                                          Z

f (x) =     ηε (x−y)f (y) dy = ⟨ηε,x , f ⟩ = lim ⟨ηε,x , fn ⟩ = lim   ηε (x−y)fn (y) dy = lim fnε (x) .
          Ω                                 n→∞              n→∞    Ω                     n→∞


    Now, suppose without loss of generality that each fn is monotone nondecreasing. Let
ε ∈ (0, 1/3). It is immediate that each fnε is also monotone nondecreasing (in Ωε ). Since
fnε → f ε pointwise in Ωε , we conclude that f ε is monotone nondecreasing as well.
   The conclusion follows the fact that f ε → f almost everywhere as ε → 0.

Proposition 7.13. The functional E − : L2 (I) → [0, +∞] is convex, proper and lower
semicontinuous.

Proof. Properness is trivial, since e.g. 0 ∈ U and E − (0) = 0. Convexity also follows easily
from the convexity of U and D− .

to show sequential lower semicontinuity. Let u ∈ L2 (I), and let (un )n∈N be a sequence in

    The following lemma is essentially standard, and below we present the proof sketched

Lemma B.3. Let Ω ⊂ Rd be an open set, let u ∈ L2 (Ω) and let (un )n∈N be a bounded
sequence in L2 (Ω) such that un → u in L2loc (Ω). Then un ⇀ u weakly in L2 (Ω).

Proof. It suffices to show that every subsequence (unk )k∈N has a subsequence that weakly
converges to u. Fix any subsequence (unk )k∈N . First, since unk → u in L2loc (Ω), we have
that ⟨unk , ϕ⟩ → ⟨u, ϕ⟩ for all ϕ ∈ Cc∞ (Ω). Moreover, since L2 (Ω) is a Hilbert space and
(unk )k∈N is bounded, let (unkℓ )ℓ∈N be a subsequence such that unkℓ ⇀ w weakly in L2 (Ω)
for some w ∈ L2 (Ω). Now, let v ∈ L2 (Ω). Since Cc∞ (Ω) is dense in L2 (Ω), let (ϕm )m∈N be
a sequence in Cc∞ (Ω) with ϕm → v in L2 (Ω). Then

       ⟨u, v⟩ = lim ⟨u, ϕm ⟩                                      (Since ϕm → v in L2 (Ω))
                 m→∞
               = lim lim ⟨unk , ϕm ⟩                                    (As observed above)
                m→∞ k→∞
                         D           E
               = lim lim unkℓ , ϕm              (Taking subsequence preserves the limit)
                 m→∞ ℓ→∞
               = lim ⟨w, ϕm ⟩                           (Since unkℓ ⇀ w weakly in L2 (Ω))
                 m→∞
               = ⟨w, v⟩                                           (Since ϕm → v in L2 (Ω))
                      D       E
               = lim unkℓ , v                          (Since unkℓ ⇀ w weakly in L2 (Ω)),
                 ℓ→∞

so unkℓ ⇀ u weakly in L2 (Ω) as needed.

Lemma 7.29. Let J ⊂ R be a finite, nonempty open interval. Let f ∈ L2 (J) be nonde-
creasing and let g ∈ H 1 (J) be such that g < 0 in J and g = 0 on ∂J, i.e. the endpoints of
J. Let [a, b] ⊂ J, let δ := f (b) − f (a) and let α := − sup(a,b) g. Then ⟨f, ∂x g⟩L2 (J) ≥ αδ.

   We first give a proof assuming f ∈ L∞ (J), and then generalize to f ∈ L2 (J) via an
approximation argument.

Proof assuming f ∈ L∞ (J). Without loss of generality, we may assume that J = I. Now,
for each sufficiently small ε > 0, recall that Iε = (ε, 1 − ε) and let fε ∈ C ∞ (Iε ) be the
mollification of f . Let fε∗ : I → R be given by
                                    
                                    fε/2 (ε)
                                                  if x ∈ (0, ε)
                             ∗
                           fε (x) := fε/2 (x)      if x ∈ [ε, 1 − ε]
                                    
                                      fε/2 (1 − ε) if x ∈ (1 − ε, 1) .
                                    

Note that each fε∗ ∈ H 1 (I), in particular because the piecewise definition is continuous
and fε/2 is smooth on [ε, 1 − ε]. Also, each fε/2 is nondecreasing and hence so is each fε∗ .
Moreover, fε∗ → f a.e. as ε → 0 since this is true of (fε )ε>0 . Finally, we have fε∗ → f in
L2loc (I) since fε → f in L2loc (Ω), and since ∥fε∗ ∥L∞ (I) ≤ ∥fε/2 ∥L∞ (I) ≤ ∥f ∥L∞ (I) , we have
that (fε∗ )ε>0 is bounded in L2 (I) and Lemma B.3 implies that fε∗ ⇀ f weakly in L2 (I) as
ε → 0.
    Let (an )n∈N , (bn )n∈N be two sequences such that an ↑ a, bn ↓ b, and moreover, for
every n ∈ N, fε∗ (an ) → f (an ) and fε∗ (bn ) → f (bn ) as ε → 0; the existence of such
sequences is guaranteed by the fact that fε∗ → f almost everywhere. For each n ∈ N, let
δn := f (bn ) − f (an ) and αn := − sup(an ,bn ) g. Note that δn ≥ δ because f is nondecreasing
while an ≤ a and b ≤ bn , and that αn → α as n → ∞ by the continuity of g.
   For each ε > 0, integration by parts gives
                                 Z                       Z
                                                  ∗ 1
                    ⟨fε , ∂x g⟩ = fε (∂x g) dx = fε g|0 − (∂x fε∗ )g dx .
                      ∗            ∗
                                      I                          I

Recall that g = 0 on {0, 1}. Moreover, since fε∗ is nondecreasing while g < 0 in I, the
integrand in the RHS above is nonpositive. Hence we can only make the RHS smaller by
restricting the range of integration. Thus, fixing any n ∈ N and using the definition of αn ,
                          Z                     Z

Proof for general f ∈ L2 (J). Again let J = I without loss of generality. We proceed by an
approximation argument. Let (an )n∈N be a strictly decreasing sequence satisfying a1 = a
and an → 0. Similarly, let (bn )n∈N be a strictly increasing sequence satisfying b1 = b and
bn → 1. For each n ∈ N, define fn ∈ L∞ (I) by
                                    
                                    inf [an ,bn ] f if x ∈ (0, an )
                                    
                           fn (x) := f (x)           if x ∈ [an , bn ]
                                    
                                      sup[an ,bn ] f if x ∈ (bn , 1) .
                                    

Note that the infimum and supremum above are finite by virtue of the monotonicity of f
and the observation that each [an , bn ] ⊂ I; thus we indeed have fn ∈ L∞ (I). Moreover,
each fn is nondecreasing and, since an ≤ a < b ≤ bn , we have fn (a) = f (a) and fn (b) =
f (b). Thus the first part implies that ⟨fn , ∂x g⟩ ≥ αδ for α as in that statement and
δ = fn (b) − fn (a) = f (b) − f (a). Finally, we have fn → f in L2 (I); indeed, letting c := a+b
                                                                                              2
for convenience and using the monotonicity of f ,

the last step by the continuity of the functions x → ∥f ∥2L2 (0,x) and x → ∥f ∥2L2 (x,1) and the
fact that an + (1 − bn ) → 0 as n → ∞. Hence ⟨fn , ∂x g⟩ → ⟨f, ∂x g⟩, and the conclusion
follows.

Lemma 7.44 (From “almost Lipschitz” to Lipschitz). Let M ∈ R≥0 and let N ⊂ I be a
measure zero set. Suppose f : I → R satisfies |f (x) − f (y)| ≤ M |x − y| for all x, y ∈ I \ N .
Then there exists a M -Lipschitz function g : I → R such that f = g in I \ N .

                                                       219
Proof. Let f : I → (−∞, +∞] be given by f := f in I \ N and f := +∞ in N . Define
g : I → R by                  (
                               f (x)             if x ∈ I \ N
                      g(x) :=
                               lim inf z→x f (z) otherwise.
Note that g is real-valued because, when x ∈ N , we may find a sequence (zn )n∈N ⊂ I \ N
such that zn → x and then, by the definition of lim inf by subsequential limits and the
assumption on f ,
                                                                         
g(x) = lim inf f (z) ≤ lim f (zn ) = lim f (zn ) ≤ lim f (z1 )+M |zn −z1 | = f (z1 )+M |x−z1 | < +∞ .
         z→x          n→∞          n→∞            n→∞

Clearly g = f in I \ N . We claim that g is M -Lipschitz. By the assumption on f , we
do have |g(x) − g(y)| ≤ M |x − y| for all x, y ∈ I \ N . Now, let x ∈ N and y ∈ I \ N .
By the definition of g, fix a sequence (zn )n∈N ⊂ I \ N such that zn → x and moreover
limn→∞ f (zn ) = g(x). Then

 |g(x) − g(y)| = lim f (zn ) − f (y) = lim |f (zn ) − f (y)| ≤ lim M |zn − y| = M |x − y| ,
                  n→∞                    n→∞                  n→∞

as desired. The case when x, y ∈ N follows by the triangle inequality: suppose x < y
without loss of generality, choose any z ∈ (x, y) \ N , which is possible since N has measure
zero, and use the above to conclude that

    |g(x) − g(y)| ≤ |g(x) − g(z)| + |g(z) − g(y)| ≤ M |x − z| + M |z − y| = M |x − y| .
